#5550. Will plants survive climate change? The 'Great Dying' reveals clues about their resilience.

Plants can't easily pick up and move when environmental conditions get rough, so they've evolved strategies to weather harsh conditions in place. That could help some plants survive in the face of a warming climate.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

About 251 million years ago, volcanic eruptions wiped out most life on Earth. For about a million years, the Siberian Traps in Russia spewed huge clouds of carbon dioxide into the atmosphere, leading to the most severe extinction event in our planet's history.

Equatorial temperatures spiked, making much of the land too hot for life. Meanwhile, the oceans became acidic and most of the creatures in them suffocated from lack of oxygen. Known as the Permian-Triassic mass extinction or the "Great Dying," it ultimately destroyed more than 90% of ocean life and 70% of terrestrial species.

However, in a corner of what is now China, a refuge of pollen-producing plants avoided that fate and created an oasis for surviving animals. Research suggests that this was not the only place where plants were able to weather catastrophic climate change during the Great Dying, and that plants may have been better able to withstand the extinction event than other organisms.

"Plants do indeed sometimes have a lot of resilience because they can't move individually, and even as a population, they're not very good at moving," Chris Thomas, an ecologist and evolutionary biologist at the University of York in the U.K., told Live Science. As a result, many plants have evolved mechanisms to survive in place despite difficult conditions, he said.

As climate change raises global temperatures and throws ecosystems into upheaval, plants will need to draw once more upon this resilience. Many plant species have evolved unique adaptations that help them weather harsh conditions. Experts told Live Science that these adaptations — along with a hefty dose of luck — will now decide the difference between survival and extinction.

Unlike most animals, many plants have the ability to regenerate limbs — and those limbs can make them better able to adapt to new environment conditions. Plants in general have higher developmental plasticity than animals, which means that they have greater flexibility to change how their cells and tissues grow and develop.

"Many plants have what we call indeterminate growth and modular body plans," Amy Angert, a botanist at the University of British Columbia in Canada, told Live Science. Indeterminate growth means that an organism continues to grow and develop indefinitely throughout its lifespan.

"If we lost an arm, it would be gone forever. But on plants, if you cut off a branch, they can sprout new ones and those new ones can then receive signals from the current environment instead of being stuck with sort of developmental programs from the past environment."

For example, researchers found that resprouts on holm oak trees (Quercus ilex) are better able to cope with drought than those that had not resprouted.

And because buds can go dormant, many plant species can resprout after catastrophic events, such as fire. Cape Floristic Region in South Africa, for example, is the smallest of the world's six recognized floral kingdoms, and most of its plant species depend on fire — its plants resprout after fire and many of their seeds require fire to germinate.

Some plants, such as the Snow Gum (​​Eucalyptus pauciflora), have a thick layer of insulating bark to protect their buds, while others like stinging nettles (Urtica dioica) bury them underground in a "bud bank". The soil insulates the seed's buds from extreme heat and cold and protects most of the plant from life-threatening conditions. This widespread adaptation allows herbaceous plants to endure through the winter and plants with bulbs to hide their tissues from the ravages of fire.

Snow gums (Eucalyptus pauciflora) under snow at Mount Franklin, Namadgi National Park, Australia.

Plants can also store seeds underground when times are difficult. Almost 350,000 plant species produce seeds. For most of these species, the seeds contain dormant tiny versions of the young plant, with all the structures that will turn into leaves and roots.

"Seeds can live essentially in stasis in the soil," Daniel Anstett, an evolutionary ecologist at Cornell University, told Live Science. "You can have the seed bank where certain seeds might come back, and there's access to the backward library of genetic information that many plants can ultimately draw upon."

Seed banks are a vital tool for resilience in many plant species. They create the opportunity for metabolically inactive individuals to reanimate when environmental conditions improve, according to a 2021 paper published in the journal Nature Communications. "When lucky, at least some of these individuals will awaken in the future when the environment is more conducive for growth and reproduction," the authors wrote.

Seedbanks can also be reservoirs of genetic diversity, which is integral to being able to adapt to new environments.

"The raw material for adaptation comes from genetic variation," said Angert. "Genetic variation is larger in large populations, and genetic variation can be shared between populations as the environment changes — if individuals can disperse successfully between them."

In a paper published in journal Science earlier this year, researchers, including Angert and Anstett, showed rapid evolution saved scarlet monkey flower (Erythranthe cardinalis) populations from prolonged drought in the U.S. and Mexico between 2010 and 2018.

"They can either grow really fast, flower and put out seeds before the fall drought takes effect, or they can grow much more slowly and bunker down,” Anstett explained.

Scarlet Monkeyflower (Erythranthe Cardinalis) in a greenhouse drought experiment.

The researchers sequenced the genomes of 55 scarlet monkeyflower populations to find areas within their genomes linked to coping with stress and drought. They tracked the populations and how they changed over the period. Some populations experienced rapid evolution to cope with the changing environment.

They found that, based on populations’ genetic diversity, they could predict whether a population would be able to survive the drought — even before it started.

While this is only one plant species, Anstett believes it could be true of other organisms too.

Some plants are able to transform how they produce energy to endure a changing environment. This is what Zhen Xu, a paleobotanist at the University of Leeds in the U.K, thinks allowed some plant species to survive the Permian-Triassic mass extinction and might become increasingly important for plants in today's changing climate.

Plants use photosynthesis to convert carbon dioxide and water into sugars and oxygen, and use stomata, or small pores on their leaf surface, to regulate the amount of carbon dioxide entering the plant.

Reproductive cone (strobilus) of a Triassic lycophyte from South China. It is approximately 2.4 inches (6 centimeters long).

Many plants open their stomata during the day to let in CO2 and release oxygen. Others use CAM photosynthesis, which stands for "Crassulacean acid metabolism," to close their stomata during the day and protect themselves from the heat. At night, they absorb CO2 and transform it into an acid, which they store in their tissues to be used to make sugars during the day.

"CAM photosynthesis is very common in present day desert plants, like the cactus and succulents," Xu told Live Science. "It's a very smart way to escape from the high temperature or aridity during the daytime."

In a study published in journal Nature Ecology & Evolution earlier this year, Xu and colleagues showed that plants that used CAM photosynthesis had an advantage during the Permian-Triassic mass extinction.

They found that Lycophytes, or are ancient vascular plants, switched to CAM photosynthesis around the time of the mass extinction. "We think CAM photosynthesis may be the special strategy to survive" and allowed these plants to dominate after the mass extinction, Xu said.

Some plants go to even more extreme lengths. During cataclysmic events, plants have turned into "hopeful monsters" to survive environmental upheaval, past research shows.

In nine separate events over the past 150 million years, many flowering plants duplicated their whole genome to give themselves a better chance of survival, according to research published in journal Cell earlier this year. The duplicate genome gives them more opportunities to evolve and adapt to challenging environmental conditions.

"We see clusterings of whole genome duplications in time, and every time it corresponds with a described, important geological event, whether it's a global cooling period, whether it's a global warming period, or whether there's an extinction event," study co-author Yves Van de Peer, a genome biologist at Ghent University in Belgium, told Live Science at the time.

Still, the survival of specific plant populations or species often boils down to luck, experts told Live Science.

Plants "essentially have this broad toolkit" but "some of the plants have some pathways, and other plants have others, and some have some traits, and some have others", Anstett said. Whether the particular tools they have will help depends on the conditions they face.

During the Triassic-Permian mass extinction, the plants in refugia in China (and elsewhere) were in the right place at the right time, with the right suite of potential adaptations at their disposal and enough genetic variety to tap into them.

The same holds true for modern-day species facing a changing climate and potential extinction. Angert said there are ways to make plant species "luckier" in the years ahead, such as connecting fragmented populations to increase genetic diversity, which increases the suite of potential tools at a plant species' disposal.

"The best thing we could do is to leave larger and better connected populations on the landscape," she said.

Anstett agreed. "If populations become really disconnected, then potentially we're eroding away whatever toolkit that these plants have to be able to adapt."

Sarah Wild is a British-South African freelance science journalist. She has written about particle physics, cosmology and everything in between. She studied physics, electronics and English literature at Rhodes University, South Africa, and later read for an MSc Medicine in bioethics.

Since she started perpetrating journalism for a living, she's written books, won awards, and run national science desks. Her work has appeared in Nature, Science, Scientific American, and The Observer, among others. In 2017 she won a gold AAAS Kavli for her reporting on forensics in South Africa.

#5549. Was van Gogh’s ‘The Starry Night’ a flight of fancy or a real sky?

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

New calculations point to a specific July 1889 constellation, but not everyone agrees

“The Starry Night,” one of Vincent van Gogh’s most famous paintings, was painted in the summer of 1889. Now researchers say they've figured out the exact night that inspired the painting.

This is a human-written story voiced by AI. Got feedback? Take our survey . (See our AI policy here .)

Vincent van Gogh’s “The Starry Night” may have been based on one specific night sky: July 22, 1889, just before dawn over France.

If that’s true, it means the famous painting was based on real observations, not just van Gogh’s imagination, astronomer Elijah Jeong-hyun Kim and colleagues report October 22 in Scientific Reports.

“How could an invented sky fit the sky of a particular date this closely?” the team writes. “Observation and imagination have commonly been treated as an either/or, yet ‘The Starry Night’ shows the two coexisting on one canvas.”

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Art historians typically date “The Starry Night” to mid-June 1889, based on one of van Gogh’s letters to his brother Theo.

“At last I have a landscape with olive trees, and also a new study of a starry sky,” van Gogh wrote on or about June 18. About a month earlier, van Gogh had entered a hospital in Saint-Rémy-de-Provence, France. “The Starry Night” is thought to represent the view through the window of his room. A short time later, van Gogh experienced an illness during which he didn’t paint for about six weeks, so most historians think “The Starry Night” must have been painted before then.

That letter is taken as strong evidence, says astronomer Donald Olson of Texas State University in San Marcos, who has written about how astronomy shows up in art history. Historians favor the June date because “van Gogh himself said so,” he says.

That assumption has led to some confusion, though. In the 1980s, two astronomers tried to match the painting’s stars to real skies in Saint-Rémy in mid-June. One proposed that the sky was from June 19, 1889, and the collection of swirly stars is the constellation Aries.

But the other concluded it couldn’t have been painted on June 19, based on cloudy weather reports in Aix-en-Provence, France, about 70 kilometers away. That study claimed the painting was “a celestial composite, constructed to make an image of the morning sky.”

Kim, who founded a telescope-building company called SLLAB Inc. in Seoul, South Korea, didn’t know about the June date when he became interested in “The Starry Night.” “Unlike previous astronomical studies, we could start freely,” he says.

Given his job, Kim had an intuition for which stars would be visible when. “People like me, who guide a telescope through its first alignment with the sky, naturally memorize where the stars are,” he says.

He was skeptical of the Aries theory. The stars in that constellation are faint enough that they wouldn’t be all that visible in the predawn sky, “hardly enough to inspire a painter,” he says.

Instead, Kim saw the stars as the Hyades star cluster, which forms a sideways V shape in the constellation Taurus. To test that theory, he and his colleagues applied a statistical technique called Procrustes shape analysis to put a number to how well the stars in the painting matched any particular constellation.

Researchers compared the V-shaped collection of stars in “The Starry Night” (first image) with those in the Hyades star cluster (middle image). The resulting graph (last image) maps the real stars (blue) and the painting’s stars (orange) against each other. They don’t overlap perfectly, but they’re suspiciously close, researchers say. The label Sn stands for “The Starry Night.” while the constellation’s Greek letters represent typical astronomy notation.

The team tested the method on another van Gogh skyscape, “Starry Night Over the Rhône,” which clearly depicts the Big Dipper. “Apart from the Big Dipper, there is no star pattern we can say for certain that Vincent painted,” Kim says. The degree of matching between the art and the sky for both paintings turned out to be similar, a major clue.

Kim also used the position and phase of the moon (a waxing gibbous on June 19, in contrast to the sharp waning crescent in the painting) and Venus to further constrain the painting’s date. He used computer simulations that weren’t available in the ‘80s to calculate the positions of celestial objects on certain dates in 1889 down to the second. He even traveled to France in June and July 2019 to try to find the field of view van Gogh would have seen from his window. Ultimately, July 22, 1889, was the best fit for the team’s data.

“I still cannot forget the moment we finally found the spot, overlaid the drawing on the view and saw it fit perfectly,” Kim says. “I was standing where Vincent had stood 130 years earlier. And the thrill I felt when I confirmed that the moon, the Hyades and Venus of July 22, 1889, fit exactly within the drawing’s field of view is still with me.”

Not to douse Kim’s enthusiasm, but that field of view may not be exactly what van Gogh saw, Olson says.

“The authors’ assumed narrow field of view, essential for their theory that the stars of the Hyades fill most of the canvas, is inconsistent with the much wider field of view required to encompass ‘The Starry Night’s’ round hills and cliffs,” he says. That casts doubt on the paper’s conclusions.

Olson proposes an experiment: Tell an amateur astronomer that “The Starry Night” depicts the Hyades and ask them to point out the star Aldebaran, which is at the bottom left of the V. In real life, this is the brightest star in the Hyades. But the painting has other much more prominent stars.

“If [the authors’] theory is correct, then this should be easy,” Olson says. Kim and colleagues identify Aldebaran as one of the two stars to the left of the cypress tree in “The Starry Night.” But Olson thinks others will see it differently. “A decent guess is that an experienced amateur astronomer, even when prompted to see the Hyades, will not identify Aldebaran as either of the two stars to the left of the cypress tree.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

E.J. Kim et al. The actual sky of van Gogh’s Starry Night: a multidisciplinary reconstruction. Scientific Reports. 2026. doi: 10.1038/s41598-026-73025-5

Lisa Grossman is the astronomy writer. She has a degree in astronomy from Cornell University and a graduate certificate in science writing from University of California, Santa Cruz. She lives in Minneapolis.

#5548. Fall allergy season is lasting longer, and air pollution could be to blame

Ragweed keeps releasing pollen until a hard frost, and frost-free fall days are lasting longer.

Most people expect fall allergies to fade once the leaves start turning. Ragweed doesn’t keep that schedule. The weed releases pollen until a hard frost kills it, and in most U.S. cities, the frost-free fall stretch is longer now than it was in 1970.

That makes for a long fall for the roughly one in seven people in the U.S. who are allergic to ragweed pollen, according to the Asthma and Allergy Foundation of America (AAFA). Mugwort, sagebrush, pigweed, lamb’s quarters, cocklebur, and Russian thistle release pollen through the same weeks.

Climate Central, a research nonprofit, counted the frost-free days in each fall, from September through November, in 202 U.S. cities.

Its fall analysis, released in September, found that this frost-free stretch grew by 11 days on average in 177 of those cities between 1970 and 2025. In 67 cities, it’s now at least two weeks longer.

An earlier Climate Central analysis measured the whole frost-free stretch, from the last spring freeze to the first fall one, in 198 cities from 1970 through 2025.

That stretch grew in 173 of them, by an average of 21 days. The Northwest gained the most, at 31 days. Raleigh, North Carolina, gained 41 days, and Reno, Nevada, gained 100.

“As temperatures rise, freeze-free seasons are stretching in every region of the country, giving plants weeks of extra time to grow and release pollen,” said Kristy Dahl, vice president for science at Climate Central.

This fall started warm in the forecasts, too. NOAA‘s Climate Prediction Center favored above-normal temperatures across much of the country for October through December. Its forecasters also expect a very strong El Niño through the fall and winter.

Freeze dates only stand in for pollen. A 2021 study in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences tracked pollen itself. William Anderegg and his colleagues at the University of Utah gathered records from 60 pollen-counting stations across North America, covering 1990 to 2018.

Over those years, pollen seasons started about 20 days earlier and lasted about eight days longer. The total amount of pollen in the air rose about 21%.

The team then used climate models to separate out human-caused warming. They estimated that it accounted for about half of the shift in season timing and about 8% of the rise in pollen. In their analysis, temperature did far more than carbon dioxide across the continent.

Carbon dioxide still matters at the scale of one plant. In a 2002 greenhouse experiment, ragweed grown in air with double the carbon dioxide made 61% more pollen. Warming isn’t the only factor in cities, either, where artificial light at night has also been tied to longer pollen seasons.

AAFA’s 2026 Allergy Capitals report ranks the 100 most populated U.S. metro areas. It weighs pollen levels, over-the-counter allergy medicine use, and the number of allergy specialists.

On weed pollen alone, the 10 worst were San Diego, Salt Lake City, Ogden, Spokane, and San Francisco, followed by Knoxville, Raleigh, Provo, Atlanta, and Winston-Salem.

Three of those cities are in Utah, where mugwort, sagebrush, or both add to the ragweed. Spokane adds mugwort, and San Diego adds sagebrush. In the four southeastern cities, AAFA lists ragweed as the only major weed pollen.

“For people with multiple pollen allergies, symptoms can be present nearly year-round due to an earlier start to tree pollen season and a longer-lasting weed pollen season,” said Melanie Carver, AAFA’s chief mission officer.

A single ragweed plant can release up to a billion pollen grains. Wind can carry the grains hundreds of miles, and an AAFA research analyst says levels near the plants are highest on warm, windy mornings.

AAFA suggests keeping windows closed on high-count days and running a portable air cleaner in the room where you spend the most time, often the bedroom. Its other tips are small habits.

Take your shoes off at the door, wash your hair at night, and don’t wear your outside clothes to bed. Wipe pets off before they come inside.

According to a 21-page position paper published in the 2025 journal Allergy, air pollution can actually change pollen.

The paper’s 14 authors work at German universities, the national weather service, and the federal environment agency. Claudia Traidl-Hoffmann of the University of Augsburg and Helmholtz Munich is the senior author.

The paper gathers earlier work on ragweed growing beside busy roads. That pollen carried more Amb a 1, the protein most ragweed-allergic people react to, than pollen from greener areas. Antibodies from allergic patients also latched onto it more strongly.

In another experiment, ragweed exposed to extra nitrogen dioxide made a new allergen. It resembles a protein found in latex from the rubber tree. Nitrogen dioxide comes from burning fuel, and road traffic is a major source in cities.

The authors’ conclusion pointed past the doctor’s office. Good medical care helps individual patients, they wrote, but only regulations that clean the air will reduce the overall burden of allergic disease.

Polluted air has also been linked to asthma and to nose bacteria turning into serious infections.

The allergy specialists’ national treatment guideline, written in 2017 by a joint task force of the two main U.S. allergy societies, covers anyone 12 or older. For seasonal hay fever, it recommends starting with a steroid nasal spray alone.

The trials the panel reviewed showed no added benefit from taking an antihistamine pill on top of it. For moderate to severe symptoms, the panel said doctors may add an antihistamine nasal spray.

Le Cui of Peking Union Medical College Hospital in Beijing and colleagues tested whether timing matters. They randomly split 120 people allergic to mugwort pollen in Yulin, a city in northern China, into two groups.

One group started a combined steroid and antihistamine spray on July 21, about two weeks before the pollen arrived. The other group started the same spray once their symptoms turned moderate or worse.

Among the 107 people with complete diaries, early starters averaged about 6 on a 12-point nasal symptom scale during the season, compared with nearly 8 for late starters.

Fewer early starters had asthma attacks (18 vs. 23), but a gap that small could simply be chance. The trial appeared in Frontiers in Allergy in 2025.

AAFA advises starting daily allergy medicine at least two weeks before your local pollen season. Ragweed usually starts in August, so that means late July for most of the country.

Allergy shots and a ragweed tablet that dissolves under the tongue need even more lead time. The tablet label says to start at least 12 weeks before the season, which means starting in mid-May for a mid-August start.

An allergist’s skin or blood test can show which pollens are actually to blame. AAFA points to one clue for telling allergies from the colds, flu, and RSV that circulate in the fall. Itchy eyes and an itchy nose point to allergies.

Allergists have long assumed that ragweed pollen triggers symptoms at lower doses than birch pollen. A small test of that idea came from the Fraunhofer Institute for Toxicology and Experimental Medicine in Hannover, Germany, in May 2026.

Two men with ragweed allergy spent four hours at a time in a sealed chamber. They breathed ragweed pollen at levels the team could set and measure. The difference the team expected between ragweed and birch didn’t show up.

The team also measured how much Amb a 1 the pollen carried. It made up only 0.17% of ragweed pollen by weight. The main birch allergen made up 1.96% of birch pollen.

Two people can’t agree on how little ragweed pollen it takes to start the sneezing. The researchers wrote that larger studies are needed before anyone can name a reliable threshold.

The position paper was published in the journal Allergy.

Learn about advocacy through the Asthma and Allergy Foundation of America if you want to support patients. The group publishes the yearly Allergy Capitals rankings and pushes for clean air policy.

Check your local count on the National Allergy Bureau site. The bureau’s network of certified stations supplied the long-term records behind the 2021 pollen season study.

Support Climate Central if your priority is public understanding. The nonprofit’s freeze-season data gives local meteorologists a way to explain longer allergy seasons on air.

Log when ragweed flowers near you in Nature’s Notebook, the USA National Phenology Network’s volunteer program that records when plants leaf out and bloom.

Check the EPA’s AirNow site before outdoor plans, because its air quality index includes nitrogen dioxide, the traffic gas linked to stronger ragweed pollen.

#5547. Namibia' Black Rhino Is One of Africa’s Most Exciting Conservation Stories

We’d been looking for nearly five hours—scanning the dry grass for upturned pebbles and the horizon for a dark, lumbering shape—when we spotted the rhino bull. Next to me, tracker Mensley Karutjaiva shook his head with a resigned chuckle. We’d been up since daybreak, alongside three other members of the Save the Rhino Trust, looking for critically endangered black rhinos in Damaraland, a land of flat-topped mountains, rocky hills, and stark plains in the Kunene region of northwestern Namibia. While we scrambled about in the heat, the bull had been dozing in the shade of a milk bush. We watched as he stood up, as if posing for us, before lying back down to resume his nap.

I was traveling in a loop around northwest Namibia that took me to three new (or recently renewed) camps. Since photographic safari tourism began here over a century ago, Namibia has largely been known amongst international travelers as a self-drive vacation—a destination with plenty of do-it-yourself road trips through otherworldly desert landscapes. Unknown to many visitors, though, human-wildlife conflict—population activities that negatively affect animals through habitat loss or resource competition— has been an ongoing challenge, and past decades of poaching threw rhinoceros populations into a tailspin. But, in the past few years, Namibia has seen a lot of positive conservation momentum, with these northwest reaches acting as a bellwether. From innovative government measures to an Indigenous-stewarded private reserve, this region is now a story of forward-looking change.

In May 2026, the country’s government launched a new conservation finance deal called Namibia for Life in partnership with the World Wildlife Fund. Worth approximately $63 million, it sets aside funds for Namibia’s 86 community-supported conservancies, which span more than 49 million acres.

Gazing at the black rhino, I felt a thrill as my eyes followed the magnificent swoop of the animal’s two intact horns, the larger of the two over three feet long. In an attempt to deter poachers, wildlife management will often cut down a rhinos’ horns—a humane, if humbling, procedure—so that the sight of a rhino, especially one of this critically endangered sub-species, often entails seeing just nubs.

Wild black rhino populations in Namibia came close to extinction in the mid 90s, but thanks to conservation efforts, have recovered.

Rhino horn can fetch up to $5,400 a pound in the illegal wildlife trade—so just seeing this intact horn was a triumph in itself. Decimated by poaching throughout the 1970s and 80s due to demand in Asia and the Arabian Peninsula, wild black rhino populations had dropped by 96 percent in the mid 90s. They came so close to extinction that only 2,410 were left on the entire planet.

That sighting was a harbinger of better days to come. Thanks to conservation efforts, today some 6,000 black rhinos now roam free, with an estimated half of those living in Namibia. In July 2026, the World Wildlife Fund announced that Namibia had experienced a 53 percent drop in rhino poaching since 2024, reaching its lowest numbers of rhino poaching instances in over a decade.

“Over the past 39 years of visiting Namibia regularly, I’ve been heartened to see a growing understanding of the importance of conservation and community-based tourism–and more recently, how to sustain it,” said travel advisor Nicola Shepherd, who arranged my itinerary. “Namibian organizations such as the nonprofit Integrated Rural Development and Nature Conservation are investing in practical ways to reduce conflict and protect both people and wildlife.”

The founder of the conservation-focused Explorations Company, Shepherd and her team have been arranging customized trips to this country for decades. In the past few years, she’s noted a shift in the advent of luxury accommodations, including Zannier Hotels, Habitas, and others, that have broadened the country's appeal to travelers seeking high-end experiences. It’s all welcome news for the country, which is experiencing an uptick in traveler interest—just two months after my trip, local publications reported that the country’s hotel room occupancy was having one of its strongest years.

More than twice the size of California, Namibia’s expanse includes coastline, semi-arid desert, dry and wet woodlands, and golden savanna. The northwest corner is a rich mix of these terrains–including the otherworldly, white-tipped grasslands I walked through as we returned to Wilderness Desert Rhino Camp after our morning rhino sighting.

“In this area of the Palmwag Concession, the rotation of tourism helps protect rhinos,” Karutjaiva told me. I heard this sentiment echoed wherever I went, although specific numbers are carefully guarded. “It’s a state secret. The only thing I can say is that the population is growing,” he said. The guardedness is understandable, as the threat of poaching is still everywhere. And growth isn’t restricted to the rhino populations.

The lounge area at the intimate Wilderness Desert Rhino Camp, which was formerly a field station for wildlife research.

At Desert Rhino Camp, a full rebuild in 2024 resulted in six stand-alone tented suites, where materials like timber, leather, and stone evoke a modern, desert-chic style. With the Etendeka Mountains rising in the distance, red basalt boulders form the walls of the open-air dining lounge where I enjoyed a delicious slow-cooked lamb potjie stew for dinner.

“We really realized how important tourism was during the pandemic, when most businesses closed and a lot of people lost their jobs,” lodge manager Fanus Katondoka told me that evening, as we sipped on rooibos iced teas. “Now we know it. Operators are building a lot of facilities, lodges, hotels–it's booming.” That growth means more resources for community-supported conservation, so Karutjaiva and his team can continue their work protecting rhinos and other species.

Rhinos have adorned conservation headlines recently, but they’re far from the only draw in northwest Namibia. A new camp from outfitter Natural Selection recently opened about an hour’s flight north, in the desert Kaokoveld region, highlighting another species of desert-adapted wildlife. A few days later, I was headed to see it.

Sitting in the cockpit seat of a tiny four-seater Cessna, I waved goodbye to the rhino trackers and flew over the Namib Desert, one of the oldest arid regions in the world. The landscape flowed in ridges and mountains of faded yellow and whisper pink, where welwitschia–large, low-lying plants that live to be hundreds of years old–sprawled like relics from a Dune film set.

The Namib Desert stretches along the Atlantic coast of Namibia and features some of the highest sand dunes in the world.

It was a fitting introduction to my next stop: the Hoanib Valley, home to a new camp with a mission that’s a bit bigger than rhinoceros. I quickly learned what that something was when guide Howard Groenewaldt picked me up from the airstrip, nosed our vehicle toward camp, and then abruptly cut the engine.

We had stopped at the Hoanib River, a seasonal waterway that runs west from the desert until it reaches the Skeleton Coast on the Atlantic Ocean. Now in the dry season, it turned into a sandy avenue lined with camelthorn and giant ana trees that age more than 100 years old. It was a nature-born runway—and something was stirring around a bend.

A giant, desert-adapted elephant slowly walked toward us. His long tusks were pale white; the bottoms of his feet splayed out wide under his skinny legs, the better to stabilize on sand. There was no sound except the breeze that was stirring the wizened, banana-shaped seed pods in the branches above my head, and the soft fwump of the elephant’s footsteps. I watched, transfixed, as he paused and delicately trailed the tip of his trunk on the ground

The author looks on at a desert-adapted elephant.

This is exactly the kind of encounter the new Natural Selection Hoanib Elephant Camp was built around. Located off the northern bank of the river, its 10 solar-powered suites bring a new level of comfort to the valley. Between game drives, guests here lounge on the veranda overlooking the desert hoping to spy more elephants, or relax in their suites with a massage or nap. Each of the natural-hued suites include a private plunge pool, a deep king-size bed, and a writing desk. Dinner is a multi-course affair in the shared lounge; afterward, a bonfire crackles under the stars. During my stay some visitors curled up on the couches against the evening desert chill, sipping hot drinks spiked with Amarula, a South African liqueur, surrounded by flickering candles.

While the resort is stylish and deeply comfortable, Natural Selection’s approach to community partnership also reflects the shift in this region toward local collaboration. The evening I arrived at camp, I met Petty Daniels, who hails from the nearby Torra Conservancy and brings an acutely local perspective. Years ago, she began working in hospitality as a waitress—today, she’s the manager of both this new camp and its giraffe-focused sister property, the Hoanib Valley Camp. Roughly 97 percent of staff are from surrounding community conservancies, creating a working model of local investment in supporting companies that protect their homeland.

Sundowner drinks are accompanied by a knowledgable guide on a Hoanib Elephant Camp safari.

Within Daniels’ generation, change has been flourishing in the hearts and minds of locals. “Before tourism, people drove wherever they wanted, shooting animals to provide food for their families,” she said. “Not anymore. People are getting into conservation tourism and understanding why they shouldn’t do that.”

Even so, human-wildlife conflict is still a problem. Elephants ramble into gardens and cheetahs kill the occasional goat, but the overall opinion of locals is shifting, especially as the Namibian government signals support for conservation that’s more in step with local interests.

Namibia’s Hoanib Valley was made famous by its desert-adapted elephants.

“Namibia’s communities already help look after lions, elephants, rhinos, water, plants and open land,” Rob Moffett explained a few days later, soon after I arrived at my third and final destination in northwest Namibia. “Instead of saying, ‘Good luck finding money every year,’ the Namibia for Life fund says, ‘Here is a proper money box, a rule book, and a team to help you keep protecting nature.’”

Moffett is the general manager of sales and marketing at Ongava Game Reserve, a private stretch of savanna and mopane woodlands that stretches more than 74,000 acres next to Etosha National Park, one of the country’s biggest visitor attractions. It’s also the site of another positive–and long overdue–story of change.

Wilderness operates single-engine flights from camp to camp that come in handy.

To reach it, I rented a gas-electric hybrid car from ATI Holidays and drove east from the Hoanib Valley. This type of travel is still popular in Namibia, with strong infrastructure supporting visitors. After a detailed briefing with an ATI staff member, I was kitted out with a folder packed with maps, a Namibian cell phone pre-programmed with key contacts, and a first-aid kit. I drove for several hours, listening to music and watching the desert scenery flash by. But driving solo can be tiring, and I was glad for the air travel between previous camps, seamlessly arranged by the Explorations Company. For US travelers unaccustomed to driving on the left side of the road, and often juggling a few shorter vacations, brief single-engine flights from camp to camp are increasingly preferred by high-end resorts opening in the northwest.

The new Horizon by Ongava, where I had arrived, is one of those. Consisting of just three hilltop villas, the property debuted from renovations and a name reset in April 2026, and prioritizes seclusion and comfort. My accommodations unfolded in a graceful series of rooms with couches, a four-poster bed, and a dual-vanity bathroom. Outside, overlooking a plain of the greenest scenery I’d seen so far, a wide deck hugged the hillside, and included two dining spaces, an outdoor shower, and a private plunge pool. My favorite touch was the large bench in the walk-in dressing room, carved in the shape of a serval—a wild cat native to sub-Saharan Africa.

The dining, led by chef Festus Ilyambo, is a feat in its own right. That evening, in the open-air lounge, I asked Ilyambo for the recipes behind his potato pavé—thinly sliced, buttery potatoes infused with organic thyme and fresh cream, then sautéed with garlic—and filet mignon, served with a jus and cognac sauce.

Raised walkways lead to villas at the new Horizon by Ongava.

Alfresco dinners at Horizon by Ongava are a highlight of the day.

The next morning, longtime guide Pascalis Kazimbu led me on a private walking safari in Ongava Game Reserve, pointing out everything from lilac-breasted rollers to white rhino mothers with calves tottering close behind and wildebeest that bleated—“gnuu, gnuu”. Later that afternoon we set out on a game drive, pausing at a watering hole for sundowner drinks as dusk began to settle. I was sipping a local Tafel lager in the safari vehicle when a deep, reverberating roar sounded behind me, vibrating in my bones. Moments later, a male lion strolled out of the thin trees behind me, settling by the pond for a drink.

With game viewing like this, verdant Ongava and its neighboring Etosha National Park are a welcome coda to the scenes of desert and dunes I’d left behind. The circular route of my trip is a popular one: travelers arrive into the capital of Windhoek, travel in a rough circle that begins with awe-inspiring desert and ends near Etosha, and then head back to Windhoek to begin their journey home.

A new community-led concession and lodge in Ongava will protect thousands of acres and help conserve species, including the Southern Yellow-billed Hornbill.

But a coming Ongava project will add a new element for curious travelers. In 2024, the Indigenous San (or Bushmen) Hai//om community and Ongava agreed to a legally binding agreement to partner to manage a new concession, including a new lodge, on the southeast border of Etosha National Park. Slated to open in 2028, the new reserve will secure thousands of acres of land, strengthening wildlife corridors and helping to protect an important waterway called the !Gobaob Spring, where black rhinos and many other species gather to drink in the dry season.

“That place is a miracle,” Ongava manager Moffett said. “In the Hai//om’s oral history, it’s the one spring that has never failed in their entire existence.”

The implications of this new initiative, called the Ongava-Hai//om Joint Venture, extend far beyond wildlife. The concession carries profound historical significance because it includes part of the ancestral homeland of the Hai//om people, who were forcibly removed from Etosha by the government in the 1950s. “Today, most Hai//om people are living scattered on the outside of the park, but this is their home,” Moffett said. “For them to reclaim management and ownership of this concession has the potential to become one of the most special community conservation stories of our time.”

When the 25-year joint venture ends in 2049, the entire business will transfer from its current shared management to the Hai//om community. By protecting endangered wildlife while empowering the Hai//om people to build long-term prosperity, the new concession promises benefits for all involved, including visitors.

The stakes are high for the black rhino, but now, there is also hope.

Learning about this project felt like an encapsulation of everything I’d seen and heard on this journey in northwest Namibia, where community-focused conservation held hands with high-end hospitality. I thought back to the rhino I’d seen in my first days here, and the slow but steady work locals like Karutjaiva are doing to protect the species from the knife’s edge of extinction.

In Namibia, the stakes are high, but so is the promise of a more prosperous, wild future.

#5546. The 9 Best Mini Chainsaws for Pruning, Trimming, and Light Cleanup

These compact tools cut as aggressively as their larger siblings, without the weight and bulk.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

When a full-size chainsaw feels like overkill for quick pruning or trimming jobs, a mini chainsaw might be exactly what you need. These pint-sized chainsaws strike a useful sweet spot between manual hand saws and heavy-duty gas-powered beasts. They cut just as aggressively as their larger siblings, but they’re compact and light enough to maneuver easily, even when you’re reaching for an awkwardly placed branch.

Mini chainsaws come in two main styles. One-handed pruning saws usually have 6- to 8-inch bars, weigh as little as 3.4 pounds, and quickly cut branches up to about 6 inches thick. Small two-handed models with 10- to 12-inch bars give you more control and leverage for thicker limbs, storm debris, and even firewood.

Nearly all of them run on batteries, too, so they’re quieter, cleaner, and easier to maintain than gas saws. There’s no pull cord and no gas can. You can push a button and start cutting. If you already own DeWalt or Milwaukee tools, you may be able to use your existing batteries. If your yard work has outgrown your hand saws and pruning shears, consider this your invitation to upgrade.

I’m a freelance home improvement writer covering tools, DIY projects, and home products for Popular Mechanics and other publications. I’m a former residential and commercial carpenter and an experienced landscaper. I’ve worked on landscaping crews at a large state park and a private campground. All of these roles led me to pick up chainsaws, both mini and full-size, to prune, clear brush, process downed branches after heavy storms, and segment fallen trees for firewood, which helped inform my selections. I included brands I’ve come to respect over the years and design features I find to be especially useful.

Coupled with my own experience and hands-on evaluations, I relied on recommendations from the test editors here at Popular Mechanics. Take the Ryobi PSBCW01K Pruning Chainsaw. Based on the team’s extensive testing, it earned a spot in the 2024 Lawn & Garden Awards, so I’ve included it here. To offer a variety of options for assorted landscaping tasks, I focused on products ranging from compact 6-inch pruners to 10- to 12-inch saws that are small yet capable of handling jobs that call for a standard, two-handed chainsaw. I also counted on the previous contributions to this article by Popular Mechanics Deputy Editor Rachel Klein.

I’ve come to respect this type of tool’s cutting ability as well as its inherent safety risks, and I do my best to stay on top of new trends and models as they enter the market. Here are the best mini chainsaws to buy now.

Stihl has been producing some of the most reliable chainsaws and landscaping equipment for almost 100 years. The GTA 26 pruner is one of them, a powerful woodcutter in a compact, easy-to-use package you can wield with one hand when you need it.

In our testing, we found that the GTA 26 could easily mow through tree branches thicker than 4 inches, which is rare. For tall branches, attach a pole extension (sold separately) to create an extremely effective pole saw for small, out-of-reach branches.

According to Test Editor Brad Ford, pole saws typically have 10- to 12-inch bars and bigger motors. So while this is a great little pruning saw, it’s not a substitute for one of the most powerful and effective pole saws we’ve used.

While it doesn’t have the bar length to serve as a one-size-fits-all chainsaw, the GTA 26 embodies everything you want in a mini chainsaw: high performance in a form factor that makes small cutting chores easier and lets you tackle tricky tasks you normally wouldn’t dare attempt with a chainsaw.

Despite its low price, the Worx WG322 is a powerful two-handed mini chainsaw that delivers serious bang for the buck. Though it has a brushed motor—less durable and efficient than more expensive brushless models—its 10-inch bar and 20-volt motor can handle serious pruning and trimming of larger tree branches.

The saw weighs just 6.2 pounds, so it’s easy to handle and comfortable to lift for overhead cuts. It isn’t the most practical choice for frequent, heavy-duty projects, but it’s a solid option if you have just enough landscaping chores to warrant keeping a chainsaw handy.

Designed for one-handed operation, this lightweight yet versatile Greenworks saw chews through light-duty pruning tasks with ease. Its 24-volt battery offers more electrical potential than the 20-volt setups found on most 6-inch pruners, which should translate into greater torque and the ability to sustain higher chain speeds. Combined with its brushless motor, this saw delivers power more efficiently and lasts longer.

At just 3.4 pounds, the saw is easy to carry and maneuver for better angles when you need to reach and cut an awkwardly positioned branch. If you’re primarily manicuring small branches on trees and hedges, it’s a solid, affordable choice.

Calling a 12-inch chainsaw “mini” is a bit of a stretch, but the DeWalt DCCS621B is an effective, compact chainsaw that can handle tougher cutting projects, like removing and segmenting branches from a downed tree, or even demolition tasks at home.

It’s compatible with DeWalt’s 20-volt FlexVolt battery platform, which includes batteries up to a huge 12.0-amp-hour (Ah) option (sold separately). That lets you tackle longer, more strenuous jobs that would require multiple charges with the 2.0-Ah cell you’ll find on most mini chainsaws. Its brushless motor also improves efficiency, maximizing power output and battery run time for heavy use.

The saw also has strong safety features: A large chain brake immediately halts the saw in a kickback, and the wraparound handle lets you maintain a safe, secure grip while you reposition your hands for angled cuts.

One issue for new chainsaw (or DeWalt tool) owners: This model is primarily sold as a bare tool without a battery, so you’ll need to buy a 20-volt DeWalt battery and charger separately. If you’re already locked into using DeWalt tools, you may even have a compatible battery. If not, some third-party Amazon retailers have packaged the tool and battery together.

Frankly, the DeWalt DCCS621B is overkill for pruning tasks. But if your to-do list extends beyond landscaping to more serious cutting—like breaking down firewood or cutting down a small tree—it’s worth sizing up to a saw like this.

Raw power is useful in a chainsaw, but in a large yard with lots of trees and hedges, generous run time should be your top priority. As part of Milwaukee’s M18 Fuel collection, the 8-inch Hatchet supports up to a massive 12-Ah battery (sold separately), giving you plenty of juice.

This saw runs on an 18-volt system paired with a brushless motor, a combination designed for efficient energy use, fast ramp-up, and reliable torque. While some cordless saws advertise higher voltage, that number alone doesn’t dictate overall performance. The real output depends on motor efficiency, current draw, and battery capacity. In practice, the Hatchet’s design balances cutting power with impressive stamina. It also has some helpful features, like metal bucking spikes to keep your log or branch stable and secure while cutting. (That’s a rarity among mini chainsaws.)

One thing to keep in mind: This specific model doesn’t come with a battery. If you’ve already bought into the Milwaukee M18 Fuel system, you can use your existing batteries. Otherwise, you will need to buy a battery, which can get expensive—for example, a 12-Ah Milwaukee battery and charger costs over $200. (Pro tip: Batteries frequently go on sale during events like Black Friday and Amazon Prime Day. Playing the sales can take the sting out of buying them.)

Milwaukee tools are known for their high quality and high price tags: The M18 Fuel Hatchet is an expensive pruning saw, but delivers features, performance, and longevity that few other options can match.

If wielding an exposed chainsaw bar feels too intimidating, the Black+Decker Alligator Lopper and its jaw-like design offer a safer alternative. The motorized scissor, in a sense, features a 6-inch cutting bar on the top and a spiked clamping lower jaw to hold a branch in place. Bring them together and cut away.

The gator jaws can clamp around branches up to 4 inches in diameter. If you stick to 1.5-inch diameter branches, Black+Decker claims the battery can last for up to 150 cuts per charge.

Long, rubber-coated grips on the handles help maintain an extra-sturdy grip, and safety switches on both handles help prevent accidental starting. Although the bulky handles limit its maneuverability, the Alligator Lopper makes small cutting tasks a little safer and more comfortable for novices who may not be used to a standard chainsaw.

Combining the two-handed design of a larger saw with the short, maneuverable shape of a smaller one, this Craftsman pruner provides a ton of cutting control.

Unlike one-handed 6-inchers, this dual-handled model is a great fit for performing careful, controlled cuts around the yard. While it’s a bit heavier than comparable one-handed saws, the added heft contributes to better overall stability when you’re really sinking into branches with some girth. The integrated tip guard is another control-friendly feature, encouraging you to cut with the mid-section of the bar (the safest, most controlled part) during upcuts.

The included battery and charger make this a great value as well.

Greenworks frequently makes affordable, user-friendly power tools, and its 10-inch chainsaw is a perfect option for first-time chainsaw owners and homeowners who often need a chainsaw for light work. It’s easy to handle and provides an impressive amount of cutting power for a low price.

The thick, rubberized rear handle and wide top handle lend a comfortable grip, and a large guard keeps your hand safe. There’s also a safety lock to prevent accidental starts; you have to press it at the same time as the trigger in order to activate the chain, a feature I’ve found to be especially useful when transporting the saw in a tool bag.

Weighing 6.58 pounds (including the battery), this saw is slightly heavier than our other 10-inch option—the best value Worx above. But I consider the added power and chain speed to be worth a few extra ounces.

With its lightweight design and reasonable price tag, the Ryobi PSBCW01K grabbed our test team's attention, earning a slot in the Popular Mechanics 2024 Lawn & Garden Awards.

With the included 2-Ah battery pack, this 8-inch pruning chainsaw weighs just 6.3 pounds—significantly lighter than the Milwaukee, our other 8-inch recommendation. Even with a larger 4-Ah battery, you’re still carrying less than 8 pounds.

That light weight comes, in part, from its lack of an onboard oil reservoir. That said, it didn’t affect its performance in our testing. If you’re trimming high branches, or just want to keep your toolkit light, the PSBCW01K is a fantastic choice.

There’s no official definition of a mini chainsaw, and no universal dividing line between them and their standard-size counterparts. My recommendations span two distinct types of models: small 6- to 8-inch pruning saws, which have a single handle and are light enough for one-handed cutting, and can handle small branches (think 1 to 6 inches in diameter) much faster than using manual pruning shears or a hand saw.

Additionally, I recommend a few small two-handed chainsaws that have a rear and top handle. They’re the better choice for sawing branches and logs around 6 to 8 inches in diameter, which requires more control and leverage.

Nearly all mini chainsaws are powered by rechargeable lithium-ion batteries, typically ranging from 18 to 24 volts. In a nutshell, the higher the voltage, the more current the motor draws from the battery, which broadly translates to a higher power output. Other factors, such as motor efficiency, can impact performance, but manufacturer-estimated voltage and cutting speed (measured in feet per second, or ft/s) combine to give you a broad but reliable sense of the chainsaw’s power and ability to handle dense cutting tasks.

In many cases, a chainsaw with a lower voltage may be able to accomplish the same cuts as a higher-voltage model, but it will take more time to do so, while placing more stress on the motor and draining the battery quicker.

The run time of a cordless chainsaw depends on the capacity of its battery. Battery capacity, measured in amp-hours (Ah), indicates how long the battery can sustain your saw’s intended power output.

If you already have power tools you like from a brand you trust, it’s smart to continue buying from that same brand, as most batteries are interchangeable between tools. More tools mean more batteries, allowing you to expand your runtime without recharging. Just keep in mind that larger capacity often means a bulkier battery, so make sure the added weight and size are worth it for you.

Bar size determines the maximum diameter of what you can safely and effectively cut. While I recommend a 12-inch option for medium-duty tasks, I generally consider a chainsaw “mini” when the cutting bar is 10 inches or shorter.

All chainsaws, mini and otherwise, have a slightly smaller maximum cut diameter than the length of the cutting bar. A 6-inch pruning saw can naturally cut branches up to 4 inches in diameter. A 10-inch version can take down an 8-inch diameter tree if needed.

If you need to cut a log larger than the length of the blade, you can use a “double-cutting” technique–essentially working through either side of the log separately–but it requires practice to perform safely. With a mini chainsaw, especially, you’re probably better off buying or renting a larger model.

Compared to their larger counterparts, all mini chainsaws are light. Their heft can vary a bit, though, and every ounce matters since many are made for one-handed use. You’ll feel that when you’re extending your arm to trim a tree branch with one hand. Keep in mind, the lightest tool may not always be the best tool—a small battery may not provide enough runtime, and a small motor may not produce enough power—but it’s worthwhile to look for the lightest option that can still handle your task.

Many mini chainsaws include convenience-oriented features that make them easier to use and maintain. I looked for power tools with thick rubber grips on the handle that provide a sturdier grasp while you work. Heavy-duty saws may include “buck spikes,” also known as timber spikes, felling dogs, or felling spikes, which dig into the log as you cut, providing extra leverage. More serious saws have metal spikes. These days, many have plastic ridges to simulate the purpose of the spikes.

To minimize maintenance and extend the lifespan of your tool, look for chainsaws with an automatic chain oiler to handle the occasional chain lubrication you’d normally have to apply manually, and user-friendly chain-tightening mechanisms, which ensure proper tension during heavy use.

Operating a mini chainsaw is every bit as dangerous as using any other chainsaw, so safety features are important.

Pruning chainsaws typically feature an adjustable blade guard positioned above the cutting bar. This prevents wood chips and debris from flying upwards into your face, while adding a layer of protection between you and the blade.

Traditional chainsaws feature a hand guard behind the chain that prevents your front hand from reaching toward the chain if you accidentally lose your grip. On more expensive models, the hand guard also acts as a chain brake, which stops the chain if your hand makes contact with it.

Many chainsaws also feature a safety lock, which you have to press as you pull the trigger to start up the saw. This reduces the possibility of powering it on accidentally.

Considering the injury risk associated with mini chainsaws, it’s always a good idea to spend a few extra dollars to pick up a model designed with premium safety features, especially if it’s for a household with novice chainsaw wielders.

Regardless of the type or quantity of the safety features on your chainsaw, you should always follow the safety protocols detailed in the user manual, and always wear personal protection. That means wearing eye protection and work boots, as well as chainsaw chaps and cut-resistant gloves. Also, avoid loose-fitting clothing and tie long hair back to avoid any possibility of getting caught in the chain–even a moment of carelessness could lead to catastrophic consequences.

As with picking any chainsaw, the first and easiest way to narrow down your options is to think about what kind(s) of wood you normally need to cut. Most one-handed pruning chainsaws have 6- or 8-inch cutting bars, which means they’re made to cut thin branches–think 3 to 6 inches in diameter. Two-handed chainsaws, even the small ones, can handle thicker cuts: A 10-inch chainsaw should be able to cut through a 7- to 8-inch branch.

You should also think about the types of plants you’re cutting and how you’re going to reach them. If you’re primarily working with waist-high hedges, a little extra weight may not matter, but using a one-handed chainsaw makes it easier to cut branches that aren’t easy to reach. Weight is also important if you’re landscaping across a large property–the more you have to walk, the more your heavy tools will weigh you down.

Going the other way, keep in mind that buying a larger two-handed chainsaw keeps the door open for a wider range of chores, such as chopping down small trees and breaking down firewood.

Most mini chainsaws are cordless electric models, which do not require much work to keep them ready to go. I recommend cleaning the saw after every use: Wipe off any tree sap on the body, and use a brush to remove sawdust and wood chips from the chain and the bar. Compressed air is a quick, easy way to blast debris off of, and out of, all the crevices on your chainsaw.

It’s also important to keep the chain tight and properly lubricated. Buying a self-lubricating chainsaw covers half of that problem, but if yours doesn’t have one, make sure to oil the chain according to the provided instructions. Don’t just use any oil lying around either— it’s important to use specialized bar and chain oil.

Check the chain tension before each use. Most saws feature some kind of manual tightening knob—it should feel snug to the bar, but loose enough to move freely. Check your saw’s manual for specific tightening instructions.

If you use your chainsaw often, you should also invest in a sharpening kit, which ensures that your saw is always operating as efficiently and safely as possible.

All three of these lawn machines are made for precision landscaping work, but each one is useful in its own way, and all three are essential pieces in a comprehensive yard maintenance toolkit.

Mini chainsaws are ideal for pruning and cutting branches and small logs, but they’re made for ground-level use. (We do not recommend using a chainsaw on a ladder.)

For cutting tasks above your head, you should reach for a pole saw, which is effectively a pruning chainsaw attached to a long pole. With a good pole saw, you can cut a high tree limb–up to 15 feet, depending on the pole–from a safe, stable position on the ground.

Hedge trimmers take a different approach: They have two rows of teeth–one stationary and one that rapidly oscillates back and forth–that capture and snip smaller (up to about. 0.75 inch) branches. Hedge trimmers are typically longer than a chainsaw cutting bar, with a flat shape that naturally lends itself to cutting and carving large swathes at a time, which makes them better for shaping hedges than for cutting through individual branches.

Alex Rennie is a freelance writer who specializes in the Home Improvement, DIY, and Tool space. As a former residential and commercial carpenter, Alex uses his hands-on experience to write practical buying guides, how-to articles, and product reviews. His work has also appeared in Business Insider's Insider Picks, and before his writing career, he was a full-time carpenter living in New York City. There, he worked as part of a team designing, building, and installing large furniture pieces, as well as performing a variety of home repair and maintenance projects. Alex currently lives in Los Angeles, CA, and spends his free time exploring the beaches and mountains with his fiancé and their dog Louie.

The Martian Moons Exploration (MMX) mission, which launched from Japan on Oct. 19, is on its way to Mars. When it arrives, the probe will attempt to land on one of the Red Planet's twin moons and scoop up rocks to bring back to Earth. Experts tell Live Science what these "transformative" samples could teach us.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Monday (Oct. 19), Japan's space agency launched a first-of-its-kind sample-return mission to Mars. The historic endeavor marks another major step toward humanity's shared goal of understanding — and perhaps eventually colonizing — one of our closest planetary neighbors, experts say.

The Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency's (JAXA) Martian Moons Exploration (MMX) probe is an international effort, constructed with help from NASA, the European Space Agency (ESA) and others. Over the next few years, it will closely study the Red Planet's twin moons, Phobos and Deimos. The spacecraft will snap close-up photos of the pair before attempting to land on the larger satellite, Phobos, to scoop up rocky material that will hopefully be carried back to Earth in around five years.

If the mission is successful, the returned samples will finally settle a decades-old mystery about the origins of both moons. The findings could also help paint a picture of Mars' ancient history and provide clues about what the early solar system was like.

The MMX probe will study Phobos (bottom center) and Deimos (upper right) up close. [This is a composite image made by stitching together individual photos of Mars (left) and its moons.]

"This mission marks a transformative leap in our capacity to investigate the Martian system," Sonia Fornasier, an astronomer at the Paris Observatory who has studied Phobos and Deimos extensively and is a project scientist for MMX's infrared spectrometer instrument (MIRS), told Live Science. It "ranks very high" among the missions we have sent to the Red Planet so far, she added.

JAXA launched the MMX probe on board an H3 rocket, which lifted off from Tanegashima Space Center in Kagoshima prefecture at 3:41 p.m. EDT Monday (4:41 a.m. Tuesday local time), Live Science's sister site Space.com reported. (The mission was initially scheduled to launch in 2024, but it was delayed due to initial concerns about the H3 rocket.)

The camper-van-sized spacecraft successfully separated from the rocket's fairing just over an hour later, and on Wednesday (Oct. 21), JAXA announced that the mission had passed the "initial critical operations phase," meaning the probe is now locked into its roughly 140 million-mile-long (225 million kilometers) journey to Mars, which will take around 10 months.

"MMX took its first step today," Naoki Murakami, general manager of Mitsubishi Electric’s space division, which led the design of the MMX probe, said in a postlaunch news conference early Tuesday morning (Oct. 20), according to The Japan Times. "This marks the beginning of a long, five-year journey."

Large crowds gathered at the Tanegashima Space Center to watch the launch of the H3 rocket carrying MMX in the early hours of Monday (Oct. 19).

Once the probe arrives at the Red Planet in August 2027, it will insert itself into a "quasi-satellite orbit" around Phobos. In other words, MMX will circle Mars alongside the moon but will not be gravitationally bound to it, similar to around half a dozen "quasi-moons" that currently accompany Earth around the sun.

This mission marks a transformative leap in our capacity to investigate the Martian system

After collecting data from a distance for a few months, MMX will swoop down and attempt to land on the surface of Phobos ‪—‬ not once, but twice.

During the first landing, MMX will hover just above the moon and deploy a mini rover, dubbed Idefix, onto the moon's surface. The wandering robot, built by the French and German space agencies, will have to contend with extremely weak gravity as it trundles across the surface of Phobos: The rover weighs around 55 pounds (25 kilograms) on Earth but will weigh only around half an ounce (15 grams) on Phobos, according to ESA.

The MMX probe will arrive at Mars in August 2027, following a 10-month journey from Earth.

Once Idefix has completed its initial assessment of the moon's surface, the entire MMX probe will set down on Phobos, on top of the spacecraft's four-legged lander module. Mission scientists hope this goes a lot more smoothly than the attempted landings of Japan's "Moon Sniper" and Resilience lunar landers, which crash-landed on Earth's moon in 2024 and 2025, respectively.

Two of the MMX lander's legs contain built-in sampling devices ‪—‬ one that will drill into Phobos and another that will hoover up surface material. The second device, dubbed the Pneumatic Sampler (or P-Sampler), was built by the Blue Origin-owned private company Honeybee Robotics on behalf of NASA and will collect its material in a "matter of seconds," Kris Zacny, vice president of the Exploration Technology Group at Honeybee Robotics, said at an Oct. 15 NASA news conference. "You blink, and it's all done."

In total, JAXA aims to collect around a third of an ounce (10 grams) of material from Phobos. That would be around twice as much material as the space agency managed to snag on its most recent sample-return mission, Hayabusa2, which collected samples from the "spinning-top" asteroid Ryugu in 2019, finding materials rich in surprising organic compounds.

The probe will deploy the IDEFIX rover on Phobos before it lands to collect its samples. The wandering robot will provide initial data that will help select a suitable landing site for MMX.

Once the samples have been acquired, at some point between late 2028 and early 2029, the probe will conduct a series of close flybys of Mars' smaller moon, Deimos, before beginning the long trip back to Earth in late 2030 — the first return flight ever attempted by a spacecraft sent to Mars.

When the probe returns to Earth in 2031, it will release its samples in a 20-inch-wide (60 centimeters) container that will fall back to our planet's surface, most likely somewhere in Australia, according to ESA.

Mars' twin satellites, which were first spotted by American astronomer Asaph Hall in 1877, are nothing like Earth's constant companion. For starters, they are much smaller; Phobos is around 14 miles (22 km) across, while Deimos is just 7.5 miles (12 km) wide. The duo also have lopsided shapes and are between 12 and 64 times closer to Mars than the moon is to Earth. (Phobos, meaning "fear," and Deimos, meaning "terror," are named after the sons of the Greek war god Ares, the counterpart to the Roman war god Mars.)

Phobos is dominated by the 6-mile-wide (10 km) Stickney crater, which was left by an ancient asteroid impact. The larger moon is also slowly falling toward Mars and may eventually be ripped apart before it reaches the Red Planet's surface.

The most notable feature on Phobos is Stickney crater, which is more than 5 miles wide. This photo of the crater was snapped by the HiRISE camera on board NASA's Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter, and was released earlier this month.

Other Martian spacecraft, such as the Emirates Mars Mission orbiter, have flown close to the moons, and NASA's Mars rovers have frequently looked up to see the satellites eclipsing the sun in Mars' sky (sometimes both at the same time). Yet many mysteries remain about the pair.

"Mars is right in our cosmic backyard, and yet we haven't explored the planet's moons," Nancy Chabot, a space exploration expert at the Johns Hopkins Applied Physics Laboratory in Maryland and the deputy principal investigator of NASA's MEGANE instrument on board MMX, told Live Science in an email. "MMX will do this!"

MMX will closely analyze Mars' moons using nearly a dozen different sensors and instruments to help pin down the mysterious origin of the twin satellites.

The biggest mystery surrounding Phobos and Deimos is their origin. Some researchers suspect the satellites are asteroids, or maybe even comets, that have been captured by Mars' gravity, while others think the moons emerged from the debris of an ancient impact on the planet's surface, similar to how Earth's moon formed.

Neither theory is conclusive, meaning the moons' origins "remain enigmatic," Christopher Edwards, a planetary scientist at Northern Arizona University and one of NASA's participating scientists for MMX, told Live Science.

For example, explaining how Mars captured the moons is "quite hard from an orbital mechanics perspective," but if they did form from a debris disk from an ancient impact, they should have coalesced into better-defined spheres, Edwards said.

"MMX will solve this debate thanks to the spacecraft observations and the detailed analysis of the samples of Phobos on Earth," Fornasier said. The data the probe collects from its flybys of Deimos will also allow researchers to generate a "full characterization of this body," she added.

This photo of Deimos was snapped by the Emirates Moon Mission in 2023 and is one of the best images of either of Mars' moons to date.

Fornasier is "particularly excited" by the possibility that the moons are captured asteroids because that would mean they would likely contain "organic and hydrated materials," which would have a big "astrobiological implication" and would shed light on what the early solar system was like.

Regardless of how the twin moons formed, researchers also stand to learn a lot about Mars and how planets form and evolve.

"Phobos and Deimos are interesting, but not just in their own right," Tom Statler, an MMX program executive and scientist in NASA's Planetary Science Division, said at the Oct. 15 news conference. "Their presence is a key piece of the larger story of the Mars system. By uncovering the moons' origin, we'll gain new insights into the history of both Mars and Earth."

MMX has been more than a decade in the making and involved input from agencies and companies around the globe. This photo shows the project's manager, Yasuhiro Kawakatsu, standing in front of two parts of the probe inside a clean room at the Tanegashima Space Center in August.

If the moons did form via an impact scenario, the samples MMX collects may also contain actual pieces of Mars for researchers to study.

"If these objects are in fact pieces from the Martian surface that have coalesced moons, we'll collect the best-preserved samples of the Martian surface," Edwards said. Other fragments of Mars have already reached Earth as meteorites. However, entry into Earth's atmosphere can transform them.

If Phobos is a chunk of Mars, that would be a real boon to NASA, which canceled its Mars Sample Return program — a mission that would have retrieved samples collected by the Perseverance rover — earlier this year. The agency's researchers will receive up to 15% of the MMX samples to analyze.

MMX will also shed light on Mars' ancient history and evolution, as well as analyzing the planet's moons. It will depart the Red Planet at some point in late 2030.

During its three-year stay in the Red Planet's system, the Japanese probe will also collect lots of data on Mars that could contribute to a wide range of future findings. "MMX will conduct in-depth studies of the Martian atmosphere, with a particular emphasis on dust and water transport mechanisms and the escape processes shaping Mars' atmospheric dynamics," Fornasier said.

This atmospheric data could temporarily fill a gap left by the recent death of NASA's iconic MAVEN spacecraft, which stopped sending data back to Earth late last year.

Analysis of craters on the Martian moons could also provide clues about other events in Mars' history. For example, some researchers think the Red Planet may have once had rings like Saturn or a larger moon.

Experts have described MMX as a "transformative leap" and "paradigm-shifting" for Martian science.

Other experts are hopeful that the MMX findings could help lay the groundwork for future missions to explore ‪—‬ and perhaps colonize ‪—‬ Mars. "The samples from MMX will bring forth paradigm-shifting science on the Mars system for generations to come, especially as NASA prepares to send the first humans to Mars," Nicky Fox, associate administrator for NASA's Science Mission Directorate, said in a recent statement.

The next sample-return trip to Mars will be China's Tianwen-3 mission, which is scheduled to launch in 2028 and will aim to land on and retrieve samples directly from the Red Planet's surface, according to Space.com.

Harry is a U.K.-based senior staff writer at Live Science. He studied marine biology at the University of Exeter before training to become a journalist. He covers a wide range of topics including space exploration, planetary science, space weather, climate change, animal behavior and paleontology. His recent work on the solar maximum won "best space submission" at the 2024 Aerospace Media Awards and was shortlisted in the "top scoop" category at the NCTJ Awards for Excellence in 2023. He also writes Live Science's weekly Earth from space series.
