#5544. Tesla (TSLA) proxy shows a board getting ready for a SpaceX merger

Tesla’s proxy for its 2026 annual meeting, filed today, confirms that one of its supposedly independent directors now sits on SpaceX’s board. Ira Ehrenpreis, who chairs both Tesla’s compensation committee and its governance committee, joined SpaceX’s board in February.

That’s one of several details in the filing that line up with a Tesla-SpaceX merger. There’s a three-item ballot, an auto-voting program that kicks in for the next vote, and Musk pay terms that pay out on a deal price alone.

Tesla’s 2026 annual meeting is set for December 3 at Gigafactory Texas, for shareholders of record as of October 13.

They get three things to vote on. The first is re-electing Elon Musk, board chair Robyn Denholm and JB Straubel to three-year terms. The second is a non-binding say-on-pay vote, and the third is ratifying PwC as Tesla’s auditor, a job it has held since 2005.

That’s it. No shareholder proposals made the ballot this year. At least, not yet. Last year’s meeting featured several, including one asking the board to invest in xAI, which has since been absorbed by SpaceX.

The board also says it won’t bring back proposals to declassify itself or to drop Tesla’s supermajority voting requirements. Its 2025 attempt drew 73.6% participation but only 39.4% of outstanding shares in support, short of the bar. So the staggered board stays, and the three directors up this year would serve until 2029.

Denholm’s letter makes the pitch directly, telling shareholders that “a steady and experienced hand during this time of rapid technological change is essential to Tesla’s success.”

As chair, Denholm has long been criticized for letting Elon Musk do what he wants, unchecked by the board, but with Musk’s backing, it’s doubtful Tesla shareholders can get her out.

The say-on-pay item asks shareholders to approve Musk’s 2025 compensation, which the proxy reports at $158,359,009,867. Against a median Tesla employee who made $62,786, that’s a CEO pay ratio of 2,522,203 to 1.

None of that was cash. The figure combines $132.3 billion for the 2025 CEO Performance Award, valued as if every milestone gets hit, and $26.1 billion for the August 2025 “interim award.” Musk forfeited the interim award in April after the Delaware Supreme Court reinstated his 2018 package.

Tesla adds its own “realized compensation” table showing Musk received $0 in 2025, for a pay ratio of “0.00:1.” Accurate for 2025. But the same proxy discloses that on June 16, 2026, Musk exercised all 303,960,630 of his 2018 options, covering the $23.34 exercise price by having Tesla withhold 17,531,857 shares. That implies a share price of about $405 and a value of roughly $116 billion at exercise.

Tesla’s income statement tells a third story. The 2025 award has generated $689 million in stock-based compensation expense so far: $162 million in 2025 and $527 million in the first half of 2026. Tesla only books expense for milestones it considers “probable,” and out of 12 operational milestones it rates exactly one as probable: 20 million cumulative vehicle deliveries.

FSD subscriptions, Optimus, robotaxis and every EBITDA target sit in the “not probable” bucket, worth $105.8 billion to $120.4 billion in unrecognized expense.

All of this comes after a year where Tesla’s net income fell 46% to $3.86 billion and revenue declined for a second straight year, to $94.8 billion.

As we noted when Tesla got the SEC’s OK to auto-vote retail shares, the 2025 award works differently in a change in control. The filings lay out exactly how.

Normally, each of the 12 tranches needs a market cap milestone, measured as a trailing average over six months and 30 days, plus an operational milestone. In a change in control, operational milestones drop out. Tesla’s market cap gets measured once: shares outstanding multiplied by the higher of the last closing price or the per-share value shareholders receive in the deal. Any tranche earned that way vests immediately instead of after 7.5 to 10 years.

With 3.95 billion shares outstanding, the first $2 trillion tranche works out to about $506 per share. A deal valuing Tesla at $3 trillion, or about $759 per share, would earn Musk three tranches, or 105.9 million shares, without a single robotaxi or Optimus milestone.

The April 2026 Implementation Agreement for the 2018 package follows the same logic. The 286.4 million restricted shares Musk received from the June exercise don’t vest until January 19, 2028. A change in control while he’s still CEO, or running product or operations, vests them right away.

Whether a Tesla-SpaceX combination would count as a change in control depends on how it’s structured. But both of Musk’s packages are now built to pay out fast if it does.

This meeting is also the launch of Tesla’s “Voluntary Retail Voting Program.” Retail shareholders who vote through ProxyVote or Robinhood will be offered enrollment in a standing instruction for all future meetings.

There are two options. The first votes your shares with the board on everything. The second votes with the board on everything except a “Special Matter,” which Tesla defines as a contested director election or a merger, acquisition or divestiture that needs shareholder approval.

There’s no option to vote against the board. Shareholders who pick “all matters” will have their shares counted for the board’s position on a merger unless they log in and override it. Tesla’s only commitment is a reminder before that meeting.

The instructions don’t apply to the December 3 vote. They start with the next shareholder meeting, which is where a SpaceX deal would land.

Ehrenpreis isn’t the only link. Elon Musk runs SpaceX, and his brother Kimbal Musk, also a Tesla director, has served on SpaceX’s board. That’s a third of Tesla’s nine-member board.

Tesla’s board still classifies Ehrenpreis as independent. As chair of the Nominating and Corporate Governance Committee, his job description includes “considering questions of possible conflicts of interest of Tesla’s directors and officers.”

Tesla is also a SpaceX shareholder now. Its $2 billion investment in xAI converted into SpaceX Class A stock after SpaceX acquired xAI Holdings in February, and the purchase closed on March 12. The SpaceX S-1 put that stake at 18,990,195 shares, under 1% of the company.

The money flows the other way too. Tesla’s proxy shows $430.1 million in 2025 revenue from xAI, mostly Megapacks, and another $143.3 million from SpaceX, mostly vehicles.

On the Q2 earnings call in July, Musk said a combination would have to “be done with the appropriate process.”

Ehrenpreis has been on Tesla’s board since 2007. The 2018 changes came out of the Tornetta mess. And the auto-vote program is sold as a fix for low retail turnout.

Seems to be a lot going on to ensure that this merger happens without a hitch including SpaceX stock promised to every child in America added to their Trump accounts as a gift. Its not a joke, I read that this morning that the US treasury laid out new rules for these accounts and no capital gains taxes on them. The Treasury Department is automatically enrolling up to 60 million children in the Trump Accounts program. Not sure what to make of all of these things and this new government business collaboration, but the old saying is beware of Greeks bearing gifts.

Put them side by side, though, and it reads like a checklist for a merger vote. Musk’s pay packages pay out quickly on a deal price. The director in charge of reviewing conflicts sits on the other company’s board. The board that would negotiate the deal is locked in through 2029. And retail shareholders are being offered a button that turns them into standing votes for the board, merger included, unless they pick the carve-out.

The problem is who’s on the other side of the table. Musk owns about 20% of Tesla and controls roughly 85% of SpaceX’s voting power. Whatever exchange ratio the two boards land on, he benefits from both ends of it. That’s exactly when Tesla shareholders need the most independent board possible. Instead, the board is getting closer to SpaceX, not further away.

Musk is right that this needs “the appropriate process,” but of course, he doesn’t really mean it. He has engineered Tesla’s board and shareholder base for years to answer to one person: him.

#5543. The 7 Best Multimeters for Reliable Electrical Measurements

Whether you’re diagnosing car troubles or testing electrical components, these devices will help you safely measure voltage, current, and resistance with precision and confidence.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

When working on electrical projects around the home—whether you're troubleshooting an HVAC issue, figuring out why a wall outlet suddenly went dead, or testing a car battery before you reach for a jump starter—a multimeter is essential. These handy little devices measure electrical properties such as voltage, current, and resistance by converting electrical signals into readable readings, eliminating guesswork and letting you diagnose problems accurately.

Modern multimeters use selector dials to choose measurement types and ranges, along with insulated test leads to contact circuits and other components. Results appear on a digital display (or on a swinging needle on an analog model), so you can quickly confirm whether a system is working properly. If a reading shows a battery is weak, a good car battery charger can often bring it back.

Since accurate readings are particularly important in the electrical world—for both personal safety and project success—it’s important to choose the best multimeter for your particular task. A multimeter is one electrical tool every homeowner should own. Drawing on my experience, observations from electricians on different job sites over the years, and extensive research, I’ve assembled this list of high-quality, well-respected multimeters for you to choose from.

As a former contractor who worked closely with electricians on commercial and residential construction projects, I have basic multimeter experience—including general home testing. While assembling this list, I also did extensive research to confidently recommend the options below. This included combing through trade and electrician message boards to ensure I didn’t miss any key multimeter capabilities or popular, reputable models.

I ruled out options with numerous problematic customer reviews and focused on multimeters with durable cases, helpful features, sturdy probes, and easy-to-read displays, while sticking to brands with a history of producing high-quality, well-respected products like Fluke, Klein, and Hioki. These multimeters are top-tier for a variety of uses, budgets, and features, so whether you need a heavy-duty multimeter or a simple option for home use, I've got you covered below.

Professional electricians trust Fluke multimeters for their reliability and accuracy, making this our top overall pick. It handles AC and DC voltage up to 600 volts and current up to 10 amperes, and its large white LED display provides clear readings.

This model also offers True RMS capability, ensuring maximum accuracy regardless of the current type. Its auto-range makes it easy to use, reducing the chance of inaccurate readings. It’s also comfortable to use with one hand, thanks to the offset control dial, and it includes a built-in kickstand for hands-free use on a flat surface.

This digital multimeter costs significantly less than the other options here but still covers a wide range of basic residential and automotive tasks. It features AC and DC voltage capabilities, so you can test wall outlets and batteries, and measure DC current to troubleshoot automotive issues. Although the LCD display isn’t as large as those on other models—and it only shows three digits—its backlit screen is convenient in low-light workspaces.

This multimeter isn’t as versatile as other options, but the compact size, straightforward controls, and low price make it a great choice for beginners or as a backup to a higher-end unit.

With a reasonable price tag and straightforward design, this Klein Tools multimeter is a great option for the average homeowner. While it doesn't come with auto-range capabilities, it still measures AC/DC voltage up to 600 volts and 10 amperes of DC current, so it’s versatile enough to cover the majority of residential tasks. It even has a battery testing feature (always a nice perk) with 9V and 1.5V settings, which cover AA, AAA, C, and D-cell batteries, and it also functions as an audible continuity tester.

The backlit LED display is easy to read in low-light conditions, and the large digits reduce the chances of misreading measurements. A handy kickstand props it up for hands-free use, and the included case protects the device from drop damage up to two meters.

The CL800 from Klein Tools combines the convenience of a clamp model with the increased accuracy of a standard multimeter. It provides a variety of measurement and test options—such as capacitance and temperature readings—and its True RMS capability and auto-ranging ensure more precise results than some other models we recommend.

This model also includes test leads, and a convenient holder keeps one lead mounted on the unit while you probe with the other. The narrow shape is comfortable to use while wearing outdoor gloves or protective equipment, and the magnetic strap allows you to easily mount it to a wall and work hands-free.

In addition to a fully functional digital multimeter, this versatile kit includes two voltage testers (a non-contact, pen-style option and one with built-in test leads) and a GFCI receptacle tester. Together, these devices ensure you’re well prepared to handle a wide range of electrical testing tasks.

You also get a soft-sided zippered case to keep everything secure and organized when not in use, plus all the batteries you need to get testing right out of the box. I’m particularly impressed with the non-contact voltage tester, which features LED indicators, an IP67 rating to resist water damage, and a built-in flashlight for low-light environments (powered by its own battery, so it doesn’t require external voltage to operate).

If you don’t need the functionality of a larger unit, or just want something more portable, this pen-style option from Hioki could be exactly what you’re looking for.

My favorite feature of this little unit is how convenient it is to operate in tight or awkward spaces, since the tip acts as one of the test leads. (Standard meters have two separate leads, requiring you to handle three elements with two hands.)

When not in use, the wired lead fits into a small recessed storage area, which is a nice design perk. The tip also features a built-in flashlight, and combined with the backlit LCD screen, it makes a great option for low-light environments.

If you prefer the real-time response and visual feedback of a bouncing needle over a digital display, this analog model from Hioki could be exactly what you’re looking for. It offers the same voltage, resistance, and current capabilities as digital versions, but displays them with a large needle on a white background, whose speed and rhythm let you better visualize the incoming data.

I’ve always enjoyed analog meters for this reason and find a swinging needle more useful for interpreting fluctuations than rapidly changing digits on a screen. The thick plastic construction is durable enough for regular use, and the hard-sided carrying case provides even more protection during storage and transport.

Multimeters measure electricity in outlets, batteries, switches, and other devices that carry current. The electrical current passes through the multimeter probes, allowing the device to measure voltage. To measure resistance, the multimeter sends out its own current and calculates the difference between that and the device’s current.

When choosing a multimeter, it’s important to know what task you need it to perform. All models offer basic functions like checking current and measuring voltage and resistance—enough for a range of light-duty, residential tasks. If you’re just interested in projects like testing car batteries, outlets, extension cords, or wall switches, you don’t need anything more than a basic multimeter (like the Klein Tools MM325 Multimeter).

More advanced models, like the Fluke 115 Digital Multimeter, offer additional functions like frequency (used for troubleshooting frequency levels in electronics like speakers), continuity (to detect open or short circuits between two points), capacitance (the ability of an object to store an electrical charge), and temperature (using a thermocouple attachment). The most basic models also usually can’t measure alternating current—only direct current.

Advanced multimeters commonly feature auto-range. This means the unit automatically adjusts to the voltage or current range you’re testing. (More basic models require you to do that step yourself, and if you forget and set your range too low, you can blow a fuse or otherwise get a bad reading.)

Although a premium multimeter's bells and whistles are useful, they can be overwhelming if you don’t plan to use them, and there’s no reason to pay for features you’ll never use.

Multimeters almost always include a pair of test probes to connect to the device you’re testing. Some accessories are for specific tasks or workspaces, like thermocouples for measuring temperature, alligator clips for hands-free operation, and extra fuses so you can keep working if one blows. The Klein Tools MM320KIT Digital Multimeter Electrical Test Kit, for example, includes a separate receptacle tester for evaluating both standard and GFCI outlets. Other helpful bits include magnetic mounting straps and holsters so you can keep your multimeter on you while you scale scaffolding or squeeze into tight spots.

It’s important that your multimeter provides accurate measurements, but some applications require higher accuracy than others. If you’re working with more complex systems, like HVAC control systems or variable-frequency drives (VFDs), look for a multimeter like the Fluke 115 Digital Multimeter or Klein Tools CL800 Digital Clamp Meter that provides True RMS (root mean square). Essentially, this lets the multimeter measure fluctuating values more precisely, whereas a standard multimeter uses an average formula that gives a less precise result.

Retailers often include an accuracy percentage in multimeter product listings, shown with a +/- symbol. This figure indicates the potential difference between the actual measurement and the displayed measurement, sort of like a margin of error. This can range from +/- 0.025 percent in high-end units to +/- 2 percent in more basic, affordable models.

The type of multimeter you get also affects its accuracy. Digital multimeters have LED displays with large, clear digits and space for several decimal places, making them easy to read and log precise measurements. On the other hand, analog multimeters like the Hioki 3030-10 HiTester are designed with a large dial and a swinging needle that shows your results, so you won’t get a reading that displays several decimal places. Although analog multimeters are simpler to use, they aren’t practical for precise measurements.

#5542. Sandals Promo Code: Save $1,500

No brand has stoked our travel imagination quite like Sandals, whose ’90s-era TV infomercials told stories of white-sand beaches, turquoise waters, and sun-kissed lovers—all set to hits like “Time of My Life” and “One Love.” With 17 resorts located across the Caribbean—including Jamaica, Barbados, Curaçao, and more—Sandals has the all-inclusive model down to a science. Whether you’re looking for some solo R&R time, planning a honeymoon, or facilitating a group getaway, Sandals is already one step ahead, with butler services, scuba diving excursions, and plenty of ways to celebrate.

Founded in 1981 by Gordon “Butch” Stewart, Sandals started out with its flagship all-inclusive, couples-only resort in Montego Bay, Jamaica. #5541. Watch bizarre snake 'cartwheel' away from danger at more than 12 feet per second

Researchers filmed dwarf reed snakes (Pseudorabdion) from different angles to better understand how the serpents pull off their remarkable rolling behavior.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Cryptic snakes in Southeast Asia have evolved a remarkable trick for evading predators: Instead of slithering, they arch their long bodies and tumble away at high speeds.

The dwarf reed snakes (Pseudorabdion) pull off this acrobatic feat by keeping their head in a relatively fixed position while rotating the rest of their body, according to a new study published Monday (Oct. 19) in the journal Current Biology.

Researchers filmed the snakes from different angles to better understand the mechanics of the rolling, or cartwheeling, behavior. They found that the snakes were capable of reaching velocities of up to 12.1 feet per second (3.69 meters per second), which is much faster than they can slither (technically referred to as undulating locomotion).

"Rolling allows them to escape from danger very quickly," study first author Yosuke Kojima, a lecturer in the biology department at Toho University in Japan, told Live Science. "In our study, their peak forward velocity during rolling was, on average, four times that of normal undulating locomotion."

Dwarf reed snakes are secretive, often nocturnal species that live across Southeast Asia, where they are prey for birds and other snakes. Although the snakes are extremely cryptic and usually hidden beneath leaves or other natural objects, people occasionally observe the serpents' dazzling rolls.

In a 2023 study, researchers formally documented the rolling behavior for the first time in one dwarf reed snake species (P. longiceps), which researchers observed in northwestern Malaysia in 2019. Kojima's team started independently observing the behavior around the same time in the Malaysian state of Sarawak, on Borneo.

"I had never seen anything like it before, and I immediately wanted to learn more about it," Kojima said.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

For the new study, researchers filmed the rolling behavior multiple times in three dwarf reed species ‪—‬ P. saravacense, P. collaris and P. longiceps — between 2019 and 2022. The team also studied three reed snakes from another genus (Calamaria), which didn't perform the behavior.

Kojima said their study builds on the 2023 research by quantifying the rolling movements, confirming the overall movement pattern that research described, and revealing further details, including how the snakes stabilize their heads.

Snakes aren't the only tumblers in nature. For example, some desert spiders can roll away from danger, as can mother-of-pearl moth caterpillars (Pleurotya ruralis or Patania ruralis). However, the snakes are unique in that they seem to have good control over their rolls.

Rolling movements typically destabilize the head and disrupt sensory-guided adjustment, especially at high speeds, according to the authors of the new study. In other words, it's hard for animals to control their bodies when they're rolling around. The snakes' technique of keeping their heads relatively fixed and often elevated off the ground likely counters what would otherwise be destabilizing effects.

Pseudorabdion saravacense is one of three dwarf reed snake species now known to perform the rolling behavior.

In addition to studying speed and form, the researchers found that the snakes could move in a relatively straight line, readily moving along a "racetrack" that was only 8 inches (20 centimeters) wide. The researchers noted that the snakes often ended their tumbling routine by diving underneath nearby objects, so they seem to remain aware of their surroundings.

The rolling is a behavioral innovation that allows the snakes to overcome their physical constraints and pull off an otherwise impossible movement, according to the researchers.

"Behavioral innovation can give rise to surprising new abilities that we would not expect from an animal's appearance alone," Kojima said.

Researchers have speculated that the bizarre behavior could surprise and confuse predators, in addition to being a rapid means of escape. The authors of the 2023 study also hypothesized that rolling would result in a patchy scent trail, and because king cobras (Ophiophagus) use scent cues to locate their prey, this could make it harder for the predators to hunt dwarf reed snakes.

Kojima, Y., Fukuyama, I., Hossman, M. Y., Kaliang, C. H., & Nishikawa, k. (2026). Locomotor kinematics and head stabilization in rolling snakes. Current Biology, 36(20). https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2026.08.057

#5540. A nanoparticle revealed the tiny impact of a single gas atom

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

The technology could help scientists measure extremely low pressures

Scientists trapped a nanosphere (the bright spot at center) with a laser inside an experiment that revealed the collisions of individual gas particles.

It’s one of gentlest caresses imaginable: the touch of a single atom of gas. Now, scientists have measured its impact.

A levitating nanoparticle jiggled when hit by individual gas atoms or molecules in a nearly empty vacuum chamber, scientists report in the October 23 Physical Review Letters.

The force exerted by a gas is usually the result of many atoms or molecules bouncing around all at once: Think of the air inside a balloon keeping it inflated. But even an individual atom can exert a tiny force. To detect that force, researchers used a laser to levitate a glass sphere, just 100 nanometers across and weighing about a quadrillionth of a gram, inside a vacuum chamber. Then, the researchers added small amounts of a heavy gas, either krypton, xenon or sulfur hexafluoride. The first two are gases made up of atoms, while the third consists of molecules.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

The nanosphere was about 10 million times as massive as an individual gas particle, making the impact extremely difficult to detect. But, by monitoring the laser light that reflected from the sphere, the researchers could measure its tiny movements when hit by a gas particle. The resulting impulses were as small as a billionth of a Newton acting for one ten-trillionth of a second. And the rate of the collisions grew as the researchers introduced more gas into the chamber, increasing the pressure within.

That suggests a possible application for the technique. “This could enable a very good way to sense pressure,” says physicist Yu-Han Tseng of Yale University, especially at very low pressures — such as in space or in vacuum chambers. The system could also distinguish between the different types of gases Tseng and colleagues studied, as each one interacted differently with the nanoparticle.

A device like this could help reveal the composition of particles in space, and even on other worlds, Tseng says. “You can imagine putting it on another planet and [using] that to study the environment there.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

Y.-H. Tseng et al. Optomechanical detection of individual gas collisions. Physical Review Letters. 2026. doi: 10.1103/xtzh-6t1f

#5539. Subaru’s next EV will be smarter, more efficient, and less like a Toyota

Subaru is testing its own tech for its next-generation EVs, breaking away from its longstanding partnership with Toyota.

After falling behind Chinese brands like BYD and other emerging EV makers like Tesla, Subaru and Toyota revealed plans in 2024 to co-develop four new electric SUVs by each brand by the end of 2026, including the updated Solterra and bZ4X ( now the bZ in the US).

The partnership was designed to share development costs and manufacturing space to cut costs and speed up development.

Subaru is now preparing to branch off from its partnership with Toyota for its next-generation electric vehicles as it looks to establish its own brand in the EV space.

According to Automotive News, Subaru revealed its latest in-house tech on Sunday that will power its upcoming next-generation electric vehicles.

“By developing vehicles in-house and having EV technologies on our own, we can quickly deliver to our customers the peace of mind and driving pleasure that define Subaru,” said Takatomo Watamori, executive officer in charge of engineering.

The new tech includes Subaru’s first dedicated EV platform, plus new batteries, electric motors, and an integrated control unit to enable over-the-air updates.

Subaru showed a prototype to reporters at its test course just north of Tokyo. The camouflaged test mule used an all-wheel-drive powertrain with a combined 509 horsepower (380 kW). Subaru said the new setup enables the EV to accelerate from 0 to 62 mph (0 to 100 km/h) in under 4 seconds.

While it didn’t reveal a specific launch date, company executives said some of the new tech will begin rolling out around 2029.

The prototype featured the new platform mounted under the body of an Outback. It used an e-axle developed with Toyota’s supplier Aisin Group and a battery pack from Panasonic Energy.

Subaru said the components are lighter, smaller, and more efficient than those used in its current electric vehicles.

Subaru’s engineers say the motors deliver a 30% efficiency increase compared with the industry average. The battery pack, meanwhile, uses 4,000 cylindrical cells from Panasonic Energy. It’s 24% lighter than the previous pack, and Subaru aims for an energy density 20% higher than the industry average.

Although it used an Outback as a test mule, Subaru quickly clarified that the design is not final and could change for the production version.

Subaru is developing an in-house integrated ECU that will support over-the-air (OTA) updates and integrate its EyeSight advanced driver assistance system.

being associated with Toyota on EVs is a drag.

Will see, if they can do better (shouldn't be hard) on their own. Or, they could just outsource the battery technology from Chinese companies, and make good value for money off roaders like they used to

By streamlining them across its lineup, Subaru said the new ECUs will help reduce costs. It will also develop a custom chip with Advanced Micro Devices that will utilize artificial intelligence in future EyeSight updates.

Subaru has big ambitions. By the first half of the 2030s, it aims to deliver 1.2 million vehicles, topping its record of 1.1 million in 2018. Last year, it sold about 924,000 vehicles, a fraction of Toyota’s at over 11 million.

#5538. The 6 Best Benchtop Planers I Recommend, Reviewed

Whether you're an experienced woodworker or just getting started, these planers turn rough lumber into smooth boards.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

No matter what woodworking project you’re tackling, your workpieces should be as flat and smooth as possible. A benchtop planer—a portable version of the industrial machine commonly found in professional woodshops—does just that. It removes rough patches and irregularities, creates a consistent thickness across entire boards, and turns rough-sawn wood into a smooth, uniform workpiece that needs far less time on an orbital sander.

When you’re faced with the alternatives, hand planing or sending wood out for professional milling, these compact machines save significant time and money while giving you complete control over lumber preparation. They also handle a wide variety of wood species, from softwood construction lumber to exotic hardwoods, making them invaluable for everything from shed construction to fine furniture crafting and the projects in between.

The benchtop planer category offers options to match different needs and budgets, from basic 12-inch units for hobbyists to premium models with helical cutterheads for serious woodworkers. Some prioritize portability for jobsite use, while others focus on power and precision for projects on a sturdy garage workbench. Features can vary widely, too, from single- or dual-speed motors and straight knives to advanced spiral cutterheads, and from a simple shop vac port to fan-assisted chip ejection that can feed a full dust collection system.

With prices typically starting around $300 and climbing well into four figures for professional-grade models, choosing the right benchtop planer means understanding which features matter most for your needs. I’ve used my own experience and our staff's expert insights to review the top models across different categories and budgets, and to guide you on the key factors to consider before making this significant investment.

For more great woodworking power tools, check out our top picks for table saws, power drills, and miter saws.

While assessing a wide range of benchtop planers, I drew on my experience using them as a residential and commercial carpenter. As a member of a fine furniture crew, I used several types of planers over the years, and I drew on that hands-on experience and insight to put together the list below. When evaluating models for this story, I made sure to include brands I've come to know and trust, such as DeWalt and Grizzly. To give options for those who want pro-style results (or just want to upgrade their current mid-range planer), I replaced several similarly designed straight knife models with two dedicated helical options.

This DeWalt two-speed high-capacity benchtop planer is a powerful, solid workhorse. It delivers 10,000 rpm to cut up to ⅛-inch deep, and the three-knife cutterhead design means blades last longer and are easier to replace.

A 2-speed gearbox lets you choose the speed that works best for your task (96 cuts per inch is a good choice for dimensioning and 179 cuts per inch for finishing), and a range of preset thickness settings ensures you won’t remove more material than intended.

This benchtop planer has a sturdy 19.75-inch cast aluminum base, and its 92-pound weight provides a rock-solid foundation. The automatic carriage lock helps reduce snipe, and the fan-assisted chip ejection keeps chips out of the way and away from the cutterhead during operation.

(This 735X model is identical to the 735, but includes feed tables and extra blades.)

This Craftsman wood planer is a reliable, surprisingly budget-friendly benchtop option.

The 15-amp motor drives double-edged, reversible blades at 8,000 rpm and delivers a feed rate of 26.2 FPM (just as productive as higher-cost units like the DeWalt DW735X and DeWalt DW734). I also appreciate the 61-pound weight (the lightest on this list), which makes it especially useful for those who value portability.

It can accommodate board widths of 12.5 inches for cuts up to ⅛-inch deep, though the relatively short in- and outfeed tables, and their limited support compared to longer options, increase the chances of snipe.

If you’re realistic about its abilities and don’t expect the precision and pro-style results of a high-end machine, this is a perfectly fine entry-level option, or for casual woodworkers who only need to use it occasionally.

This Wen planer offers wide-ranging versatility by accommodating boards up to 13 inches wide and 6 inches thick. With 26 staggered HSS blades, this planer easily creates a smooth finish on a variety of wood types and is especially well-suited to figured wood with uneven grain.

Each of the 26 cutters has two sides, so you can simply rotate them when they dull, or, as I’ve experienced, when a nail or other piece of metal hidden in your workpiece damages them.

As is typical with spiral models, this cutterhead commands a higher price (though still much lower than a true helical option), roughly $70 more than the comparable Wen model with a straight-blade cutterhead. If this single-speed model lacks the versatility you need, you can opt for the two-speed spiral version for about $200 more.

If you want cut quality, minimal post-planing sanding, and (relatively) quiet operation, this powerful Grizzly helical planer is a fantastic option.

Its 30 inserts are arranged in two rows on the cutting head, and their spiral staircase-style formation creates the smooth shearing cuts you expect from a helical model, with minimal tearout and a smooth, professional-looking finish.

The carbide construction makes these inserts more heat- and chip-resistant than steel versions, retaining their edges longer and increasing their overall lifespan.

I’m also a big fan of this planer’s granite base, which not only provides an extremely stable foundation but also eliminates the rust or warping concern of a steel base.

It’s certainly not cheap, but if you have the budget and need for a true helical benchtop planer, look no further than this model from Oliver.

Released in May 2025, the top-of-the-line planer features 40 cutting heads arranged in a spiral pattern around the cutterhead (unlike less effective “spiral” or “helical-style” options), shearing wood at the slight angle needed to generate clean, smooth cuts with minimal tear-out.

You can remove and rotate these four-sided inserts individually if they become worn or damaged. Its 15-inch maximum width supports a wider range of projects, and a digital readout lets you set and read the current material removal setting precisely.

If you don’t need the versatility of my top overall pick, the DeWalt DW735X, but still want the quality construction you expect from a DeWalt tool, consider this slightly stripped-down DeWalt benchtop planer.

It includes the same 15-amp motor, 3-blade design (DeWalt claims the cutter head provides 96 cuts per inch), and a three-year warranty, but with a slightly lower max cutting width (12.5 versus 13 inches) and one speed instead of two.

While these differences make it marginally less versatile than the DW735X, they also make it about 12 pounds lighter, boosting portability if you plan to transport it.

Most benchtop planers have a 13- to 15-amp motor (roughly 2 horsepower), which provides enough power for basic woodworking projects with softer lumber species, such as pine or cedar. For furniture-making and similar projects that demand harder wood varieties (oak, beech, maple, and walnut), I recommend a 15-amp model, since harder woods need more power for clean cuts. If you frequently work with particularly hard species, consider going higher and choosing a 20-amp model like the Grizzly 13-in W 20-Amp Benchtop Planer.

Benchtop planer cutting elements, often called knives or blades, are straight, spiral, or helical. A straight-knife cutterhead (the spinning metal assembly inside the unit) typically has two blades, though some models have more for an even smoother surface, like this DeWalt model I chose as the “Best Overall” pick. Spiral cutterheads also feature uniformly distributed, insert-mounted blades around the cylinder (like my “Best Spiral” pick). The blades are often two- or four-sided, so you can rotate them after the edge gets dull or damaged.

Premium models with helical blades use multiple rows of compact, square cutting inserts, typically mounted in replaceable holders, so maintenance is straightforward when you need a replacement. Helical cutterheads also cost more upfront than other types, but their longer-lasting cutting edges and quieter operation can be a fair trade-off if you do a lot of planing.

Remember, a planer is only as good as its blades, and proper maintenance matters more than the technology itself. A well-maintained $400 two-blade planer will outperform a neglected $1,000 helical model, so understanding your user manual and following proper maintenance protocols is crucial.

That being said, when properly maintained, helical heads do offer genuine advantages over straight knives. They’re quieter, produce smoother cuts on difficult grain patterns, and are easier to maintain because you can rotate or replace individual inserts instead of precisely setting full-length blades. However, these benefits only matter if you’re committed to keeping your equipment in good condition. Bottom line: focus on maintenance first, then consider the technology upgrade if it fits your needs and budget.

The width of a benchtop planer refers to the maximum board width the machine can accommodate. This is typically 12 or 12.5 inches for more compact models, like the Craftsman model, and 13 inches for conventional sizes. Some machines, like the Oliver, feature 15-inch widths, which can be convenient for those who plan on working with wider boards.

With benchtop planers, consider the cutterhead speed and the feed rate (how quickly the board moves through the machine). I recommend a benchtop planer with a cutterhead speed of at least 8,000 revolutions per minute (RPM). The feed rate, measured in feet per minute (FPM), determines how fast material passes through the cutterhead and affects both productivity and surface-finish quality. There’s also the cutting speed—the number of cuts per minute–measured in CPM—but since not all brands advertise this measurement, it’s not the most useful for comparisons. Most benchtop planers have only one speed, though some high-end models offer two: one for dimensioning and one for finishing.

Planing wood can get messy. Dust and wood chips can accumulate quickly, which is why most benchtop planers include some kind of dust collection system. Most include a port you can connect to your shop vac to pull chips away as you work (just make sure the diameter is compatible), while others use fan-powered chip ejectors to blast shavings into a dedicated dust-collection bag.

Some benchtop planers come with extra knives (another perk that made the DeWalt DW735X such an easy choice as my best overall pick), which come in handy when your blades get dull. Many also include one or more feed tables, which help level and support the board as it moves through the planer for a smooth infeed and outfeed. I’ve found that short feed tables don’t offer much support, which makes snipe more likely.

Planer “snipe” refers to small dips at the beginning or end of the board, caused by the cutting head removing slightly more material than intended. Snipe is nearly unavoidable on benchtop planers, so you'll need to sand or hand-plane the area, but with proper technique and practice, you can minimize its impact. A trick I learned in my woodshop is to feed in a piece of sacrificial scrap lumber before and after the workpiece.

#5537. Why is the Plaza Hotel Closing? The Story Behind the Façade

Of the 700+ hotels in New York City, The Plaza Hotel stands apart. Surrounded by skyscrapers, the stout, eighteen-story building at the corner of 59th Street and 5th Avenue, just south of Central Park, is more of an icon in the cityscape than any of its super-tall neighbors could hope to be. It hosted The Beatles just before their breakthrough performance on The Ed Sullivan Show, welcomed F. Scott and Zelda Fitzgerald, inspired the eponymous Neil Simon play Plaza Suite, acted as a backdrop in Home Alone 2, and was the home of the fictional Eloise, the little girl who roamed its elegant halls. Now, nearly 120 years after it first opened, it’s shutting its doors—at least for a few years.

The Plaza's façade and the interior of the hotel are registered landmarks, recognized by both the national and city government.

Starting in 2027, the Plaza Hotel will embark on a four-year-long renovation of its marble façade. First designed by Henry Janeway Hardenbergh in 1907, the hotel’s base and lower levels are crafted from rusticated marble, with the upper levels being made from a polished, smooth marble exterior. Rusticated marble deteriorates more quickly when exposed to the elements, and can often need to be repaired and restored within twenty years. Coincidentally, the 2027 renovation comes just 19 years after the last major renovation to the hotel in 2008.

Restoring the hotel’s façade will require more than just throwing up the scaffolding and calling in a team. The façade and the interior of the hotel are recognized as registered landmarks both nationally and by the city. The Landmarks Preservation Commission is working closely with the hotel to help with the restoration efforts and preserve the architectural beauty of the building.

Interior View of the Palm Court Restaurant, 1957

“Designated as both an individual and interior landmark, the Plaza Hotel’s elegant French Renaissance-style architecture, distinctive roofline, and prominent location have made it an iconic feature of the New York City skyline,” says Courtney Metakis, from the New York Landmarks Preservation Commission. “Its grand, public interiors have made it an enduring part of the city's cultural identity for more than a century." The commission did not comment further on the plans for the renovation or guidelines to keep in line with the commission’s protected status.

While this oversight is meant to preserve the history of the hotel in the event of, say, billionaire real estate developers encroaching in the vicinity of the building, it also can make restoration projects like this vastly more complicated. The commission will require the restoration process be carefully managed to preserve the integrity of the marble and any historical details. The hotel has enlisted Allison Doyle Partners (ADP) Architects for the job—the same firm that worked on the previous renovation of the interior architecture. Design firm HLZAE will oversee the façade’s masonry restoration in particular.

Plaza Hotel, Fifth Avenue Entrance, New York, New York, 1929. The iconic hotel has been open for 120 years.

“The undertaking reflects the complexity of maintaining a property of such architectural and historical significance, with careful consideration given to preservation requirements and the building’s long-term care, as well as the safety of our guests, employees, and the wider community,” the hotel’s management said in a statement. “The scope and complexity of the façade works require detailed planning and coordination with relevant stakeholders, alongside adherence to applicable historic preservation requirements. Further considerations relating to the planning and execution of the works remain under review as the process advances.”

While the restorations are only to the marble surface of the building, the meticulous nature of the job would have disrupted all aspects of the Plaza experience for would-be guests. If you spent $1,500 for a room (or upwards) and woke up not to the sun cresting over Central Park, but because a man on a scaffold was drilling just outside your window, you would likely be upset about it.

The Plaza Hotel entrance in New York City, 1988. The last major renovation to the hotel was in 2008.

“With a building of that age, the level of architectural significance and importance to the city, requires a great deal of care and planning and execution for any restoration,” explains Frank Mahan, principal at SOM, the architecture firm that worked on a similar restoration of the historically protected façade and interior of the Waldorf-Astoria hotel in midtown over the course of ten years. “Any Landmarks building requires a certain degree of engagement with the LPC staff and sometimes at a public hearing.” Similar to the expectations of the Plaza Hotel, the Waldorf Astoria’s owners wanted to restore the façade rather than reinvent it in a new, modern image, so the LPC was involved through the process.

There’s never a good time to announce a multi-year closure, but the hotel is now working to make good on many of its long-term plans guests have made. Last April, bride-to-be Christine Carney booked the Plaza for her wedding, and was still sorting out down payments for the booking as of just a few weeks ago. Since the announcement of the closure, she's had to start looking for an alternative venue in time for the big day. The hotel is also working with union The Hotel & Gaming Trades Council to place the Plaza’s 378 staffers into new jobs for the interim of the restoration.

The real question is: Will four years be enough time? Or will the Plaza need to extend the expected closure once unforeseen needs arise, as they so often do with older buildings. “Any project with an existing building involves unexpected existing conditions,” Mahan adds. “It’s rather inevitable that once you start working on the building, you uncover things you may not have expected or that may differ from what the original drawings predicted. There’s a certain amount of flexibility and nimbleness expected on the design side.”

Luckily, the neighborhood has no shortage of historic hotels from a bygone era of the city. If you were planning a trip to stay at The Plaza (or had one booked and this is how you’re learning that it’s been canceled…) consider going just across the street to the Sherry-Netherland, up 5th Avenue another block or two to The Pierre, or around the Eastern side of Central Park for a night at the Lowell or the Carlyle.

For more of our favorite New York City hotels, see here.

#5536. Carnival Unveils ‘The Next Course’ Culinary Strategy

Carnival Cruise Line is embarking on the next phase of its culinary evolution with “The Next Course,” a program introducing new restaurants, bars, menus and dining services across its fleet. Juan Fernandez, Carnival’s vice president of food and beverage, said the initiative combines innovations aboard upcoming Excel-class ships with enhancements to the line’s existing 29-ship fleet.

At the center of the program are four new restaurants planned for Carnival’s next Excel-class ships, the Festivale, scheduled to debut in 2027, and the Tropicale, expected in 2028. Emeril’s Coastal Seafood, developed in partnership with Carnival Chief Culinary Officer Emeril Lagasse, will draw inspiration from the coastal cuisine that influenced Lagasse’s career, featuring dishes ranging from grilled seafood to New England classics such as jumbo lump crab cakes.

Uku Lei Lei will combine Hawaiian influences with bold Asian flavors in a concept Carnival says is designed to offer something distinctive. Fetaccine will blend Italian and Greek Mediterranean cuisines, bringing together dishes and flavors inspired by feta and fettuccine. Bistro Musicale will take a Parisian approach, offering French favorites including croissants, quiche, croque madame, French onion soup and steak frites, accompanied by music.

The Festivale’s broader food-and-beverage program will be tied to its music-focused identity. New bars include The Spark, featuring cocktails inspired by songs, a guitar-shaped bar and live performances; Mix, inspired by electronic music; and Festival Grounds Coffee & Bar, which will transition from a daytime coffee venue to an evening music and cocktail destination. Carnival also plans to expand its signature Alchemy bar concept.

The Next Course is also bringing changes to dining across the existing fleet. Carnival has adjusted dining times to accommodate guests who prefer earlier meals and introduced Express Dining, a shorter three-course dinner option for passengers with busy schedules or evening entertainment plans.

Menu updates are being guided in part by data from Carnival’s more than 100 million meals served annually. While staples such as filet, barbecue ribs, shrimp cocktail, kung pao chicken and chocolate melting cake will remain, new offerings include duo of lamb, Beef Wellington and bacon-wrapped pork tenderloin. Health-conscious choices such as stuffed portobello mushrooms, grilled salmon, chicken breast and strip loin are also being added.

Other fleetwide updates include expanded Bagels at Sea offerings, new Lido Marketplace choices such as chicken and waffles, family-friendly menu options and milkshake pop-ups aboard Excel-class ships. Menus will also be tailored to itineraries, with longer cruises featuring additional comfort-food selections such as barbecue bourbon-glazed meatloaf, fish and chips, fried chicken sandwiches and loaded mac and cheese.

Fernandez said Carnival’s scale and efforts to reduce food waste help fund culinary improvements while managing costs. New dishes undergo extensive testing aboard ships, with chefs refining portions, flavors and accompaniments to ensure consistency across the fleet.

Ultimately, Fernandez said Carnival’s culinary strategy is built around four principles: food should be delicious, approachable, timely and consistent. The goal, he said, is to provide guests with a broad range of complimentary dining experiences while maintaining a reliable standard across Carnival’s ships.

Excerpted from the Fall 2026 Cruise Industry News Quarterly Magazine.

#5535. 21 Best Places to Visit in December in the USA (2025 Edition)

We’ll never forget the first time we visited New York City in December. We’d seen it in movies, but standing there, seeing the Rockefeller Center tree all lit up for the first time, with skaters below and festive music in the air… it was pure magic.

But December travel isn’t just about snow and Santa. We’ve also spent the holidays escaping to the warmth, trading snow boots for flip-flops.

In fact, we’ve been to almost all 50 states and spent a lot of winters trying to find the most fun destinations and best weather. We’ve done so many road trips in the USA and flown almost everywhere, trying to find the best places to either escape the snow or enjoy the winter, you would think we are obsessed. And we want to share all of that knowledge with you.

If you’re looking for the perfect winter getaway, you have a clear choice: either lean into the winter magic or escape it completely. Are you dreaming of a warm-weather escape with sunshine and sand, or a storybook Christmas town that looks like a real-life snow globe?

Based on all those experiences, we’ve put together this list of our favourite places to visit. Whether you’re looking for a festive city, a snowy mountain, or a sunny beach, we’ll help you find the perfect spot.

Here is a quick look at some of the best places for a December getaway in the USA, from festive winter wonderlands to sunny escapes.

December is a month of pure magic and festive energy. It’s the perfect time to use up that last bit of vacation time for the year. The biggest draw is the distinct choice it offers: You can either dive head-first into a winter wonderland, complete with snow and holiday markets, or you can completely escape the cold weather and find yourself on a sunny beach. It’s a month of festive celebrations, incredible decorations, and finding the perfect atmosphere to end the year.

If you’re anything like us, sometimes the best gift in December is a break from the cold. Trust us, we know after living in Toronto, Canada. Those winters are brutal. These are our favourite spots to find sunshine and warm weather, where you can trade your winter coat for a light jacket or even a swimsuit.

We love the laid-back, quirky vibe of Key West, and it’s even more special in December. We have a great memory of biking around the island, seeing the historic inns draped in holiday lights, and then ending the day at Mallory Square for the sunset celebration. It’s a perfect escape from the cold.

Why Visit in December: This is the perfect time to visit. The summer humidity is gone, the weather is sunny and warm (often in the high 70s), and the risk of hurricanes is very low. The whole island is in a festive spirit, with unique events like the “Bight Before Christmas” boat parade.

Where to Stay: We recommend finding a guesthouse or inn in the historic Old Town to be close to the action.

We once went to a holiday luau in Ka’anapali, and it’s an experience we’ll never forget. You’re eating kalua pork and sipping a mai tai, watching a beautiful hula performance… and then Santa Claus arrives on an outrigger canoe. It was the perfect, uniquely Hawaiian way to get into the festive spirit.

Why Visit in December: You get tropical weather, festive holiday luaus, and it’s the official start of the humpback whale watching season. Be aware that this is a popular time, especially around Christmas, so prices can be high and you’ll need to book in advance.

Where to Stay: Look to the resorts in Wailea or Ka’anapali for a classic Maui experience.

We’ll always remember snorkeling the underwater trail at Trunk Bay in St. John. The water was so calm and clear, and seeing all the colourful fish felt a world away from the snow back home. That’s the magic of a Caribbean trip in December, and the USVI makes it so easy for American travelers.

Why Visit in December: It’s the start of the dry season, with fantastic weather and warm, clear water. You get a full-on tropical, warm-weather Christmas experience, all without needing a passport.

Where to Stay: You can choose from large resorts in St. Thomas or more eco-friendly villas and lodges in St. John.

We were not prepared for how magical St. Augustine is in December. We took one of the Old Town trolley tours at night, and seeing the entire historic city, from the old Spanish architecture to the palm trees, draped in millions of white lights… it was breathtaking. It’s a holiday light display unlike any other.

Why Visit in December: The famous “Nights of Lights” festival. The entire city is illuminated, and the mild Florida weather is perfect for walking around and enjoying the atmosphere. It’s one of the most festive and warm places you can visit.

Where to Stay: We recommend a historic inn or bed & breakfast in the downtown district to be within walking distance of all the lights.

While Maui is our go-to for a resort escape, Honolulu is where you go for a vibrant, city-meets-beach holiday. We loved seeing the massive Santa surfing with his reindeer in the fountain at the Iolani Palace. The whole city has this amazing “Mele Kalikimaka” (Merry Christmas) spirit.

Why Visit in December: It’s the perfect blend of warm beach weather, world-class shopping, and festive displays. It’s also a significant month for history, with the memorial events at Pearl Harbor.

Where to Stay: Waikiki has a huge range of hotels, from historic beachfront properties to modern high-rises.

If your idea of a winter getaway is to truly get away from everything, the crowds, the noise, the stress, then South Padre is a perfect choice. We spent a week there, and our days consisted of long, quiet walks on the beach. It was a total reset and the perfect way to escape the winter blues.

Why Visit in December: The weather is mild, and the crowds are gone. It’s one of the warmest places in Texas and offers a simple, relaxing beach vacation without the high prices or holiday chaos of other destinations.

Where to Stay: You’ll find many beachfront condos and hotels offering great off-season rates.

We thought we knew desert holiday lights, but the “Las Noches de las Luminarias” at the Desert Botanical Garden in Phoenix is on another level. Walking the garden paths lit by thousands of hand-lit luminaria bags, with musicians playing, was a top-tier holiday experience.

Why Visit in December: You get two trips in one: perfect, sunny weather for outdoor adventures like hiking during the day, and stunning, sophisticated holiday light displays at night.

Where to Stay: Choose from upscale resorts in Scottsdale or stylish hotels in downtown Phoenix.

Sometimes, the warm weather destinations just won’t cut it, especially if you are looking for that winter wonderland feel. So, this section is for travellers who want to dive head-first into the holiday spirit. These are the cities and towns that go all-out for December, with millions of lights, Christmas markets, and an atmosphere of pure magic.

We’ve visited NYC many times, but there’s a specific energy in December. We grabbed hot chocolates and walked through the Bryant Park Winter Village, watching the ice skaters zip around the rink under the lights of the surrounding skyscrapers. It felt like being in the opening scene of a holiday movie. There is nothing like New York City at Christmas.

Why Visit in December: It is the quintessential American Christmas destination. From the iconic tree to the world-famous window displays, the entire city is on a different level of festive.

Where to Stay: Midtown is convenient for seeing the main holiday sights, but we also love the West Village for its cosy, neighbourhood feel.

We’d heard Leavenworth was a “Christmas Town,” but we weren’t prepared for the reality. We visited during the Village of Lights festival, and walking through the snow-covered Bavarian-style village while every single building was covered in over half a million lights… it’s not an exaggeration. It’s the closest we’ve ever felt to being inside a real-life snow globe.

Why Visit in December: The entire town transforms into a “Hallmark movie” Christmas village. It’s a full-immersion holiday experience with carolers, festive food, and incredible decorations.

Where to Stay: Book a Bavarian-style lodge or inn right in the village. You must book many months in advance for December.

We love Asheville, but visiting in December is all about the Biltmore Estate. We took the candlelight evening tour, and seeing the main banquet hall with its 35-foot tree, fireplaces roaring, and carolers singing… it felt like we’d stepped back in time into a Gilded Age Christmas party.

Why Visit in December: Christmas at Biltmore is one of the most iconic holiday events in the country. The city itself is a cosy mountain retreat, and the Blue Ridge Mountains are just beautiful with a dusting of snow.

Where to Stay: You can stay on the Biltmore Estate for the full experience or choose a boutique hotel downtown.

Here are the remaining destinations for the “Best Christmas Towns & Festive Cities” section, all written with unique, December-focused content.

We’ve visited a lot of Christmas markets, but visiting the one in Bethlehem, known as “Christmas City USA”, just felt right. We sipped hot cider and browsed the stalls in the old quarter, and you can feel the city’s 250+ years of Christmas tradition. The whole place is lit by the glow of a giant Moravian star on the hillside.

Why Visit in December: This is Christmas City USA. It was nicknamed this on Christmas Eve, 1741. It offers one of the most authentic and historic holiday experiences in the country, blending its Moravian heritage with a European-style Christkindlmarkt.

Where to Stay: Look for a hotel or inn in the historic downtown area to be close to the festivities.

Walking into Solvang in December felt like we’d been transported to a Danish village during its Julefest celebration. We spent the day exploring the half-timbered buildings, windmills, and bakeries, all decorated for the holidays. It’s such a unique and charming way to experience a winter getaway in the middle of California.

Why Visit in December: The town’s month-long “Julefest” (Yule-fest) celebration is in full swing. It’s a one-of-a-kind Danish-style Christmas with parades, markets, and, of course, delicious European pastries.

Where to Stay: Choose one of the charming Danish-themed inns right in the village to be in the middle of the Julefest activities.

We had to see the “Christmas Capital of Texas” for ourselves, and it did not disappoint. The entire historic Main Street is transformed. We loved seeing the vintage railroad, which becomes the “North Pole Express” for the month. They take the “Christmas Capital” title seriously; there are over 1,400 events in 40 days.

Why Visit in December: It is the officially designated “Christmas Capital of Texas”. The sheer number of festive events, light displays, and activities is staggering, making it a top-tier destination for families and holiday lovers.

Where to Stay: The Gaylord Texan Resort is an attraction in itself, or you can stay near the Historic Main Street to be close to the events.

A New Orleans Christmas is an experience all its own. We did a “Reveillon Dinner,” a modern take on a Creole holiday tradition, and it was one of the most memorable meals we’ve ever had. Afterwards, we walked to Jackson Square to see the St. Louis Cathedral all lit up while carolers sang.

Why Visit in December: You get to experience unique Creole Christmas traditions you won’t find anywhere else. The weather is mild, the food is incredible, and the city’s lobbies (like at The Roosevelt) have some of the most elegant decorations we’ve seen.

Where to Stay: We recommend a hotel in the French Quarter to be in the middle of the action and see the decorated lobbies.

We went to Natchitoches (pronounced Nak-a-tish) specifically for its Christmas Festival, and it was a total joy. It’s one of the oldest and biggest in the country. The whole city, which is the oldest in Louisiana, lights up with over 300,000 lights along the Cane River, and the weekly fireworks shows are fantastic.

Why Visit in December: It hosts one of the oldest and most celebrated holiday festivals in the US, running for six weeks. It’s a classic, Southern Christmas experience in a beautiful historic district.

Where to Stay: Book a room at a historic bed & breakfast in the downtown area.

Visiting Colonial Williamsburg in December felt like walking through a living history Christmas card. We joined a candlelight tour, and seeing the historic buildings lit by real candles in the windows, with carolers in period costumes and the smell of woodsmoke in the air… it was absolutely magical.

Why Visit in December: You get to experience a traditional, 18th-century “Colonial Christmas”. The decorations are all-natural (no plastic lights), and the atmosphere is incredibly authentic and charming.

Where to Stay: Stay in one of the historic Colonial Houses or a tavern right in the restored area for the full immersive experience.

Here is the final category of destinations. This section is all about embracing the snow and finding that perfect winter wonderland.

For many, holiday season travel is all about finding that perfect, snowy scene. These are the places to go for world-class skiing, cosy lodges, and that magical feeling of a true winter escape.

We’ll always remember our first “après-ski” in Aspen. We had spent the day on the mountain hitting fresh powder whenever we got the chance, and as the sun went down, the sky lit up with this incredible pink and orange light they call “Aspenglow.” Sitting by a fire, enjoying a glass of Christmas cheer, and watching the snow fall on the historic town, it just felt like the peak of a luxury winter trip.

Why Visit in December: It’s one of the world’s most famous upscale ski destinations, and the town is just as much an attraction as the mountains. The festive atmosphere, high-end shops, and restaurants are all decked out for the holidays.

Where to Stay: Aspen is known for its luxury hotels and ski-in/ski-out resorts.

One of our favourite things about Park City is its history. We loved skiing down to the historic Main Street, popping off our skis, and walking straight into a saloon that’s been there since the silver mining days. It feels like a real Western town, not just a ski resort.

Why Visit in December: It’s a historic mountain town with two world-class ski resorts (Park City and Deer Valley). The historic Main Street is incredibly charming, and the “Snow Globe Stroll” features life-sized snow globes you can walk through.

Where to Stay: You can find ski-in/ski-out hotels or stay at an inn on the historic Main Street.

Jackson Hole in December is just dramatic. We took a sleigh ride through the National Elk Refuge, and seeing thousands of elk gathered against the backdrop of the snow-covered Teton mountains… it was a powerful and wild winter experience we’ve never forgotten.

Why Visit in December: It’s the perfect mix of rugged “cowboy Christmas” charm and some of the most challenging ski terrain in North America. The town square, with its famous antler arches, is beautifully lit for the holidays.

Where to Stay: Stay at a slopeside resort in Teton Village or in a hotel in the town of Jackson.

We love Whitefish because it feels like a real, down-to-earth ski town. We spent a day snowshoeing in nearby Glacier National Park, enjoying the total silence and incredible snowy landscapes, and then came back to town for a casual dinner. It’s all the beauty, without the glitz. Just what we were looking for in a winter vacation.

Why Visit in December: It’s a laid-back, charming mountain town with a top-rated ski resort and stunning scenery. It’s also right next to Glacier National Park, which is a spectacular place to explore in the winter.

Where to Stay: You can find lodges on the mountain or stay in one of the friendly inns in downtown Whitefish.

This was a trip purely for the fun of it, and it was 100% worth it. You drive down streets named “Santa Claus Lane” and “St. Nicholas Drive,” the streetlights are shaped like candy canes, and you can visit the Santa Claus House and see his reindeer. It’s a surreal and joyful experience.

Why Visit in December: It’s the ultimate Christmas fantasy come to life. The entire town is Christmas-themed, 365 days a year, but it’s most magical in December with real snow on the ground and the added bonus of possibly seeing the Northern Lights.

Where to Stay: North Pole is very small; most visitors stay in the nearby city of Fairbanks, which has a wide range of hotels.

For the best warm weather, your top choices are Southern Florida (like Key West), Hawaii (like Maui and Honolulu), and the desert Southwest (like Phoenix and Palm Springs). The U.S. Virgin Islands are also a fantastic passport-free tropical option.

Leavenworth, Washington, is often considered the ultimate “Christmas town” as it looks just like a Bavarian snow globe. Other top choices include Bethlehem, PA (known as “Christmas City USA”), Solvang, CA (for a Danish-style Julefest), and Grapevine, TX (the “Christmas Capital of Texas”).

Yes, but it depends on the park. It’s the perfect time to visit desert parks like Death Valley, Everglades, and Big Bend, where summer heat is unbearable. However, many mountain parks like Glacier, Yellowstone, and parts of Yosemite are mostly inaccessible due to heavy snow and road closures.

You are almost guaranteed to find snow in the mountain towns of the Rockies, like Aspen, CO, Park City, UT, and Jackson Hole, WY. You’ll also find snow in the Cascade Mountains (like Leavenworth, WA), Alaska, and the mountains around Lake Tahoe.

December is a magical month to travel. Whether you’re chasing the sun, seeking out that perfect snowy scene, or looking for a town that’s full of holiday magic, the US has the perfect spot for you.

We hope this list, based on our own winter travels, helps you plan your next getaway. Save this on Pinterest for your holiday planning! Where is your favourite place to go in December?

#5534. Brain scans reveal 'number line' that's used to mentally compare values

Although whole numbers and fractions are processed differently in the brain, when you compare their values, a single "mental map" akin to a number line lights up.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Picture a number line, with the values running from the smallest to the largest. New research shows that the human brain uses a similar system when comparing numbers; in the study, scientists uncovered a "mental map" in which numbers have unique coordinates in the brain, which may help us place them in order of magnitude.

"Numbers are abstract concepts, but our brains have ways of organizing them that allow us to compare and reason about their values," said study co-authors Daniela Valério, a postdoctoral researcher in the INSERM-CEA Cognitive Neuroimaging Unit (UNICOG) in France, and Stanislas Dehaene, the director of UNICOG.

"What is fascinating is that the brain can bring together numbers that look very different, such as integers [whole numbers] and fractions, within a common system," they told Live Science in a joint statement.

That system is a map for both sets of numerical values within the brain, they reported Thursday (Oct. 22) in the journal PLOS Biology.

To investigate how the brain represents different types of numbers, Valério, Dehaene and their colleagues used a high-resolution functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) scanner, which tracks blood flow through the brain as an indirect measure of activity. They measured brain activity in 20 adults while they worked through a simple task of comparing numbers — a mix of fractions and whole numbers — and determining whether each number was bigger or smaller than the previous one shown.

The brain scans revealed distinct activity patterns in the anterior inferior parietal cortex — part of the brain that's known to be key for processing numbers. As participants compared numerical values, the brain region lit up in an organized pattern resembling a number line. The whole numbers and fractions were represented along this shared line according to their size, rather than being mapped in separate systems.

fMRI scans performed during the study revealed similar patterns of brain activity when participants compared integers and fractions. Smaller numbers are represented in reddish hues and larger numbers in bluish hues; these activity patterns represent an average across the study participants.

The brain's shared map brings different number formats into a common system based on the quantities they represent, the researchers concluded.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

"For me, it is how the brain gets from a symbol to its meaning," said Abiot Derbie, a cognitive neuroscientist and founder of the company LifeByLogic, which makes psychology assessments.

The fraction "¼" contains the digit "4," but the fraction's value is much closer to zero, he noted. "The activity patterns reflected that numerical relationship, going beyond the digits themselves," Derbie, who was not involved in the research, told Live Science in an email.

The idea that the brain represents numerical magnitude in the parietal lobe is not novel. However, scientists mostly understood how the brain dealt with whole numbers, while its strategy for fractions was not well understood. The new work builds on that existing research by exploring how both numerical magnitude and number type are processed together.

"To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to investigate the geometry of neural representations in a task involving both integers and fractions," the researchers wrote in their report. "In the future, it might be interesting to expand the present work to decimal numbers."

From previous work, "scientists already knew that fractions and whole numbers engage overlapping parts of the brain's number-processing network," Derbie said. "This study gives us a clearer picture of how their values are organized."

In the future, the team could examine how this neural representation varies across people of different ages, educational levels and mathematical backgrounds, Derbie suggested. The study also included only native French speakers, so may not reflect differences across language or culture.

"I would want to see how this organization develops as people learn," Derbie said. For instance, the team could follow children before and after they learn about fractions, to see whether changes in brain activity accompany better understanding.

In the current study, the participants found it more difficult to compare fractions than to compare whole numbers, the researchers noted; they were slower and made more errors while working with fractions, which was expected. But the study's results don't offer hints as to why.

"Our study was not designed to explain why fractions are harder to learn or understand," Valério and Dehaene told Live Science. "What is new in our study is that we show that these different types of numbers are organized within a shared map in the brain."

Valério, D., Debray, S., Karami, A., Cauté, M., Gravel, N., & Dehaene, S. (2026). A cortical semantic map integrates fractions and integers in the human brain. PLOS Biology, 24(10), e3003820. https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pbio.3003820

Her work translates complex medical topics into engaging, evidence-informed stories, drawing on her frontline clinical experience. When she's not writing or caring for patients, she can usually be found planning her next adventure or exploring the outdoors.
