#5533. The 8 Best Outdoor Pizza Ovens That Deliver Delicious Homemade Pies

We tested the Ooni Karu 2, Ninja Artisan, Gozney Tread, and others to find out which models are worth the dough.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Just like tending to the grill and gathering around a firepit are inherently enjoyable, enjoying a fresh, piping-hot pizza is similarly rewarding, and it’s just a bit more special when it’s homemade. Baking a pie in the oven can yield good results, but there’s nothing like the crunchy crust and smoky flavor of a pizza made in a proper hearth. If you’re hoping to replicate your favorite restaurant’s pie, an outdoor pizza oven can take your homemade slices from decent to genuinely artisan.

Stand-alone and countertop outdoor pizza ovens reach much higher temperatures than regular ovens, and in less time. That high heat is exactly what you need for a perfectly cooked pizza crust—and several of the models we tested can cook up a pie in as little as 60 seconds.

Plus, many of the best outdoor pizza ovens we write about are suited for cooking other tasty foods, just like a real hearth. You can make fish, meat, roasted vegetables, and even bread in these wood-fired and gas-fueled models.

To find the best pizza ovens, we looked at models with good features and designs and called in a handful to try out for ourselves. We selected pizza ovens that use a variety of fuels, including gas, charcoal, and wood. Outdoor pizza ovens all pretty much come with a learning curve, but we identified models best suited to beginners and pizza aficionados alike to offer a range that caters to different skill levels.

For testing, we heated each oven for 30 minutes to season them and burn off any oils or contaminants from manufacturing, then let them cool completely. Fifteen minutes after firing up each oven on high using propane burners, we measured the temperatures on the pizza stones with an infrared thermometer.

Then, we made pizza, using the same dough each time, with propane and oak firewood as our fuel. During cooking, we noted how well each oven performed, different features, and its ease of use. Finally—and most enjoyably—we assessed (a.k.a. ate) the pizza. We recommend the following pizza ovens based on our hands-on experience, their specs, and quality.

Over a decade ago, Ooni built its first portable pizza oven with a design that many have mimicked. Our previous pick for the best pizza oven, the Karu 16, has been replaced by the Karu 2 Pro. It’s the exact same as the Karu 16 with three new features: an air vent, Bluetooth-connected thermometer, and an even larger window on the oven door.

Like the Karu 16, the Karu 2 Pro makes 16-inch pizzas fired either with propane or wood. In testing, we liked the locking door with a glass window, which helped maintain a consistent temperature and allowed us to keep an eye on our pizza while it cooked.

Ooni also added a digital thermometer to this model that monitors air temperature in the oven via a wired probe. That info is sent to your phone so you can be in the kitchen prepping more pies or enjoying company instead of babysitting the oven.

Using the propane burner is easy and gets the job done, but we appreciate the large firebox for wood, which provides a lot more flame across the top of the oven. The large capacity also had us refilling it less frequently than smaller wood-fired ovens.

The Karu 2 Pro also doubles as a capable charcoal grill if you want to cook meat or veggies. While we like a dedicated grill for burgers and steaks, the Karu 2 is a do-it-all outdoor kitchen if you have limited outdoor space. The addition of the new air vent even gets it down to a consistent 200 degrees Fahrenheit for low-and-slow cooking.

Full Ooni Karu 2 Multi-Fuel Outdoor Pizza Oven Review

The Ninja Artisan is the only electric outdoor pizza oven we've come across that's worth a double take. It needs to be covered if left outside because of the electrical components, but the body is made of weather-resistant steel.

Our tester, Danny Perez, didn’t have to hassle with pellets or propane to get things started—he simply plugged it in, preheated, and started cooking. It has a max temp of 700 degrees Fahrenheit, which is less than most gas and wood-fired ovens, but it's not necessarily a bad thing.

While a 1,000-degree oven that makes pizza in 90 seconds sounds easy, there's a lot more to it. Shredded mozzarella starts melting in seconds, the dough crust needs a minute to bake, and toppings may require a different cooking time. Electric temperature controls and a lower max heat offer more runway before taking off on your pizza-making journey. Another plus of an electric outdoor pizza oven is even cooking because there's no need to turn the pizza when it's in the oven.

The Ninja has built-in preset pizza modes to cook different styles of 'za: Neapolitan, New York, thin crust, and pan. It preheats to respective target temps for each and then prompts you to add the pizza. Hit start and it starts counting down for a perfectly timed cook.

He tried the thin crust setting, which cooked the pizza at 550 degrees Fahrenheit for four minutes. When the timer went off, out came an evenly cooked pepperoni pizza.

The Neapolitan setting worked great as well. It cooked the pizza at 700 degrees F for two and a half minutes yielding an impressively crisped crust with some leopard spotting.

Full Ninja Artisan Outdoor Pizza Oven Review

A lot of outdoor pizza ovens are portable enough to pick up and move, but they aren't designed with transportation in mind. Fair—most of us park them in the backyard. Gozney put more thought into portability than its competitors with the Tread pizza oven.

It weighs 30 pounds, has two handles on top, and has a manageable square shape. It can go from home to tailgate with ease. We tested the Tread with the collapsible Venture Stand, which gives you a cooking space wherever you take it.

Getting the Tread fired up only takes a few seconds when using the control dial on the side. The flames instantly extended across the roof of the pizza oven, and we wanted things to get hot, so we preheated for about 20 minutes. Once it reached an optimal temperature, we were ready to pitch the first pizza.

The compact size can fit up to 12-inch pies, which is plenty big for personal pizzas. In testing, we found that the oven has great heat retention, so you can cook them one after another. We also found the opening offered enough wiggle room to rotate the pie with a pizza peel for even cooking.

The Tread comes with a built-in thermometer on the side, but we still used an infrared thermometer to measure the temperature of the pizza stone itself. The quick-release gas hose and regulator make for quick setup, too. For those who plan to take the Tread on the go, Gozney also has a handy carrying case.

Full Gozney Tread Portable Outdoor Pizza Oven Review

You'd be hard-pressed to find an electric pizza oven that gets the job done like the original Ooni Volt, but the Ooni Volt 2 is an honorable step up. This model has a smaller carbon footprint than its predecessor, and it's even easier to use.

Messing with temperatures can get complicated quickly, but the Volt 2 has three presets for cooking Neapolitan, New York, and Chicago Deep Dish-style pizzas to perfection. We usually use an infrared thermometer to gauge the temperature of pizza stones, but we trusted the presets and weren't disappointed.

While this electric pizza oven doesn't exactly mimic a wood-fired or propane-fueled model, it has its place in the kitchen. The Volt 2 can be used all year long, is capable of cooking plenty of your favorites, and is much easier to manage. Even if you already have an outdoor model, we'd go so far as to say that you might need this indoor version, too.

Full Ooni Volt 2 Electric Pizza Oven Review

Typically, rotating pizza stones are a gimmick you find on budget pizza ovens to make up for a low temperature range, especially for an electric model. GreenPan’s Stanley Tucci pizza oven, however, has both firepower and a surprisingly effective rotating stone for indoor and outdoor use.

The controls on this model go from 350 to 850 degrees Fahrenheit, so you can use it for more than just cooking pizza. The oven also has a temperature boost feature that allows you to “boost” the top, bottom, or both heating elements. During testing, Ford used this to crisp up the top crust and brown the edges, and this feature offered more flexibility for cooking his pizza exactly how he liked it.

The Stanley Tucci pizza oven uses a round stone, which sometimes can make it harder to accurately pitch your creation onto the stone. With this model, the oven door has a mechanism attached so that when you open it, the stone is lifted and pulled out, making it easier to toss in your pizza and avoid accidental spills and messes.

Overall, this is the best electric rotating pizza oven that Ford has tried yet, though he noted that this design is limited to 12-inch pizzas only.

Full GreenPan Stanley Tucci Pizza Oven Review

The Gozney Dome Gen2 is definitely a luxury pizza oven, but it's chock-full of features that could potentially make it a worthy investment for those who are dedicated to perfecting their pizza and baking crafts.

A 14-inch flue sits atop the roof of the oven, and this was helpful for maintaining airflow and flames. The Dome Gen2 comes with a standard propane burner, and the wood kit is optional. This helps simplify the process for newer pizza-makers, since wood-fired cooking takes some time to master.

One of the highlights we appreciate the most is the size of the oven itself. After preheating, we had no problem cooking two midsize pizzas side-by-side, and we could move the pies around if we needed to slow the cook time.

It takes a little longer for the oven to heat up when using wood, but luckily, changing the fuel mode doesn't make the burner obsolete. We were able to fire up the oven with the burner first, then switched to wood when it reached the right temperature and turned off the propane.

We tested the Dome Gen2 on a somewhat windy day, and the propane burner blew out a few times. To offset this, we kept the temperature a little higher than usual and burned charcoal on the wood tray for better control.

The digital display is a very convenient feature. Instead of using an infrared thermometer, we could monitor levels in real time right on the pizza oven without worrying about accuracy.

Full Gozney Dome Gen2 Dual Fuel Pizza Oven Review

A pizza oven from the smokeless firepit folks? Sign us up. The ease of cooking with a propane burner is tough to beat.

It took us about 20 minutes to heat up the oven using propane, though you should expect a bit more time when it’s relatively cool out and less when it’s hot. In testing, the average cook time was about 90 seconds and required turning the pie every 20 seconds or so.

The Pi Prime’s manual not only had thorough assembly instructions but a lot of helpful notes and tips to get started, including suggested target temperatures for different styles of pizza.

We found that managing oven and stone temperatures didn’t take too much time, although like other ovens without a door, breezy or windy days could make it more challenging. The oven opening on the Pi is 13 inches wide, so 12-inch pies are about as big as this oven can handle.

Similar to Solo Stove fire pits, ventilation holes line the bottom of the pizza oven for plenty of airflow. The Pi Prime is also constructed with durable 304 stainless steel to withstand high temps and different weather conditions.

Full Solo Stove Pi Prime Outdoor Pizza Oven Review

The Arc Lite is about as analog as it gets, equipped with only a pizza stone, ignition knob, and stainless steel insulated housing to bake your favorite pies. While this model is smaller and less features-heavy than the Dome Gen2 we’ve tested, that doesn’t mean it’s any less effective.

It didn’t take long for us to get the Arc Lite up and running. We just connected the hose to the propane tank then pressed and turned the knob to ignite. It took about 20 minutes for the pizza oven to warm up on a cooler spring day, and we maintained an internal temperature of 800 degrees Fahrenheit. The Arc Lite reaches 950, but that high heat requires more attention so you don’t overcook your pie.

The pizza we made was ready in about 90 seconds and had the crunchy crust and bubbly cheese we desired, and the only downside we really came across during testing is that the Arc Lite is limited on size and can only cook up to 12-inch pizzas. Great for everyday bites, but not ideal if you’re looking to host and make multiple pies at once.

Full Gozney Arc Lite Outdoor Pizza Oven Review

Our resident pizza oven expert, Brad Ford, is regularly trying new models to consider for this roundup. In cases where we remove a model we’ve tested in favor of something new, we still like to highlight it here.

The All-Clad Rotating Stone Outdoor Pizza Oven was our previous pick for the best rotating design. It runs on four AA batteries and only requires a push button to start. But for this particular feature, Ford prefers the GreenPan Stanley Tucci Indoor and Outdoor Pizza Oven. It had clear benefits during his testing experience, and the unique lifting mechanism under the round stone made it easier to pitch pizzas into the oven.

Outdoor pizza ovens use gas or wood to create flames across the top that radiate intense heat downward, warming the pizza stone—like preheating an oven in your house. However, unlike your home oven, outdoor pizza ovens can reach up to 950 degrees Fahrenheit.

Once the outdoor pizza oven hits cooking temperature and your pizza is placed inside, it only takes a couple of minutes to cook. The pizza stone or oven floor gets to the same temperature as the roof so the pizza cooks fast and evenly. While it cooks, those flames up top produce a golden crust that’s crispy on the outside and soft and airy on the inside. Because the pizza only takes a minute or so to cook, you can make a few with different toppings one after another.

Wood is the traditional fuel of choice for pizza ovens, favored for the smoky flavor it imparts in the crust and toppings. However, wood (or charcoal) fires take longer to heat up and require more attention to maintain the right temperature. That may not be an issue if you’re only making a couple of pies, but it can become a hassle if you’re cooking multiple pies for a large group, or entertaining a party while you cook.

Propane fuel can heat a pizza oven up rather quickly. Since it only takes around 15 to 20 minutes to preheat, it's hard to deny the convenience of being able to cook a pie in a relatively short time frame. Plus, cleanup is easy when there’s no ashy firebox to empty. Just keep in mind that a propane oven won't give you that unique, wood-fired flavor.

Luckily, you don't have to sacrifice one for the other: A few of the brands we recommend offer extra accessories you can buy to accommodate a different fuel type. There are even some options with combined fuel, so you can heat up your oven fast with the propane and imbue the pizza with wood-fired flavor, too.

The oven’s size determines the maximum temperature output the unit can reach, as well as the size of pie you can cook. Most outdoor pizza ovens are designed for 12- or 14-inch pies. Our Best Overall pick, though, can accommodate pizzas up to 16 inches for oversized, restaurant-like slices.

Pizzas are typically cooked between 450 and 600 degrees Fahrenheit. Classic Neapolitan pizza requires temperatures around 900 degrees—these are the ones with golden crusts, mottled with dark leopard spots. High-end models top out at around 950 degrees Fahrenheit.

The pizza ovens we recommend have max temperatures that far exceed 500 degrees, but note that an oven with a relatively low maximum temperature still gives you a delicious, cooked-through pie—it just may not look quite as picture-perfect.

In short, yes. Outdoor pizza ovens are incredibly versatile. Beyond pies, you can cook meats like steak and chicken, roast veggies, bake bread (like sourdough or focaccia), prep seafood, make cast-iron dishes, and bake desserts. Since it's essentially a super-hot wood-fired oven, you can use plenty of different methods to whip up your favorite foods.

Accessories aren't necessarily a requirement for operating your pizza oven, but they can make the process a lot smoother and safer. We recommend using tools such as a pizza peel to move and adjust your pie, an infrared thermometer to check stone temp accurately, heat-resistant gloves, and a brush or scraper for cleaning the stone.

Outdoor pizza ovens are generally easy to clean and don't require a ton of maintenance since their high temperatures naturally burn off a majority of food residue. Usually, you can just brush off ash and debris after the oven has cooled down. You can also occasionally run it at high heat to get rid of any extra stubborn bits, and wipe down the exterior as needed. Avoid using water or soap on the pizza stone to prevent cracking.

#5532. Why More Women Are Traveling Abroad for Egg Freezing and IVF

My path to seeking reproductive healthcare outside the US began in the same way it would have at home: It started with life not going according to my neatly organized plan.

The writer in Oaxaca, where she went to freeze her eggs

In summer 2025, I found myself freshly divorced and looking out over the proverbial fertility cliff of turning 35. I suddenly felt the need to act fast. I was unsure about having children, but I wanted to keep that door open for the future.

As a self-employed writer with health insurance that leaves much to be desired, I knew going abroad for a more affordable option was my only choice—at least the only financially responsible one. That’s how I found myself in the office of Doctor Daniela Paz Cervantes, an OBGYN in Oaxaca, Mexico, who oversaw my care for two rounds of egg freezing just a few months later.

I’m not unique in my search for medical care abroad. Although exact numbers are difficult to track, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that “millions of US residents travel internationally for medical care each year,” listing reproductive tourism as one of the main categories of services that they seek. While I went to Mexico for my treatments, Spain, Greece, Cyprus, and Barbados are also particularly popular for fertility tourism.

Dr. Mario Vega Croker, Medical Director of Panama Fertility located in Panama City, Panama, has noticed more Americans knocking on his door. Panama Fertility opened nearly 40 years ago as a small clinic catering to local families; fast forward to today, and it’s grown into one of the most prolific clinics in Central America, with half of their patients hailing from the US and Caribbean.

“American patients are one of our fastest-growing groups, especially in gestational surrogacy, [receiving] egg donations, and IVF,” says Dr. Vega Croker. “Many come to us after putting their dreams on hold because treatment at home felt out of reach […] Finding the same expertise and quality of care at about half the cost often gives them the chance to finally begin, or to try again.”

According to FertilityIQ, a data-based fertility education platform, the average egg freezing cycle costs $16,000 in the US. Meanwhile, I spent about $7,000 for one round, which is inclusive of what I paid for flights, food, accommodations, and activities for the three weeks I was in Mexico for my treatment. And I appreciated the quality of my care: My entire cycle was incredibly thorough and personalized, even before I touched down in Oaxaca. It was a no-brainer.

Alisa Purdie traveled to Barbados Fertility Centre for her IVF treatment.

Alisa Purdie, a California-based online business coach, faced a similar set of choices when she got divorced at 31 with the added challenge of managing premature menopause, which can cause fertility issues—though her journey, from ideation to undergoing treatment, took more than a decade to come to fruition.

“After my divorce, my plan was to do embryo adoption because I was in menopause, so I didn’t have any of my own eggs,” Purdie says, referring to the option to acquire an embryo from another couple and carry it herself. “I was just sort of collecting information about all the different options as a single woman and what that [path to motherhood] might look like.”

Then she learned of a friend who had frozen her eggs in Greece, and it sparked a lightbulb. After looking into options in Turkey, Greece, and the UK, Purdie settled on the Barbados Fertility Centre not only because of the 50% cost savings compared to the US, but also because of the warmth and compassion of the staff she met during a visit and exploratory phone calls.

“The information and the data, of course, is really important,” Purdie says in reference to the clinic's 72% success rate with donor egg IVF treatments. “But it's a pregnancy. You're trying to bring a baby into the world. There also has to be kindness, patience, and positivity.”

Life took a few more turns for Purdie, and she didn't begin her fertility treatment for 15 more years, though she knew the clinic would be her path forward. By the time she reached Barbados at age 46, she had married her best friend of 12 years and arrived with a new plan: fertilize a donor egg with her husband, instead of adopting an embryo as she'd initially intended.

Alisa Purdie began researching fertility clinics abroad at the age of 31, with the plan of adopting an embryo—but life took a few more turns first.

At age 46, Purdie underwent treatment in Barbados and had a successful pregnancy with her husband and a donor egg.

With eight donor eggs shipped from their home state of California, Purdie and her partner achieved two healthy female embryos and were able to transfer one to Purdie’s uterus, allowing her to carry the pregnancy. That first attempt was successful, and Purdie’s daughter turns one this month.

“To become a mother at 47 has been the biggest blessing of my life,” she says. She plans to transfer her second embryo next year at age 49.

Inspired by her experience, Purdie now shares her story on social media and runs a community called IVF Sisterhood in hopes of encouraging other women with fertility issues, especially those going through IVF past the age of 45. Though the community isn't exclusively for those looking beyond the US for treatment, there are plenty who are.

“For so long, infertility has carried so much shame and trauma that we just sort of shrink,” Purdie says. “I feel so much joy every single day [since becoming a mother], and I really want that for any woman or family who wants that for themselves.”

Cost and quality of care are often cited as the top motivations for medical tourists. For Felicia Garay-Stanton, who works in public relations and fashion marketing in New York, the current political climate in the US, including the rollback of women’s reproductive rights, also played a role in her decision to travel to freeze her eggs at the IVF-Life Madrid clinic.

She ultimately conceived her son naturally, four years after her egg freezing in Spain, but Garay-Stanton says that she feels at ease knowing her eggs are stored in a safe place should she need them in the future.

“I just like the fact that in Spain they have [legal] protections for all reproductive rights, especially if you're a single female, or if you’re LGBT,” Garay-Stanton says.

While Spain allows single women and same-sex couples to seek IVF treatments with a sperm or egg donor, other countries, like the Czech Republic, only offer these services to heterosexual couples, which is important to understand while researching options. The minimum age for an embryo transfer is typically 18 years old—the maximum, however, varies more widely, with countries like Greece setting the limit at 54; Barbados doesn’t allow transfers past 49. Some countries, including Spain, don’t allow you to choose your sperm donor, if that's part of your plan; and others—like Germany, Denmark, and the Netherlands—only allow pre-transfer genetic testing of embryos when there’s a high risk of severe disease. Understanding a country’s legal and even social parameters for reproductive care is key when choosing where to go.

In South Korea, for example, it’s not strictly illegal for single women to undergo IVF treatments, but in practice it’s almost impossible to access, as clinics typically require proof that patients are in a heterosexual marriage before every IVF cycle.

It’s a fact that Hellynn Jung and her husband know firsthand. After undergoing a lengthy IVF journey in Seoul, Jung, who was born in South Korea but grew up in the US, now openly shares her experience on social media and offers consultations to women seeking reproductive care, especially overseas.

Hellynn Jung and her husband traveled to Seoul for IVF.

After undergoing IVF in Korea, Jung openly shares her journey with others on social media.

Despite the restrictions, Jung says Korea is an attractive option for straight couples who want to engage with a medical system that’s fast and efficient.

“[The process] is very straightforward. No nonsense, and they're really good too,” she says, noting Korea's technological advancements in the medical field. “So that's the reason why I went back.”

The cost savings in Korea may not be as dramatic for Americans as they are in other countries—the average IVF cycle in Seoul costs between $5,000 and $10,000—but according to Jung, people are also drawn to Korea for the possibility of doing beauty treatments and other medical work while there. “Korea is a hub for anything medical,” she says.

Jung inside a hyperbaric oxygen chamber, during her IVF treatment in Korea

No matter where someone chooses to get reproductive care, Jung recommends making the process as lighthearted as possible. After being diagnosed with a diminished ovarian reserve, Jung went through 15 egg retrievals and 5 embryo transfers before eventually giving birth at 43. She knows from experience that staying calm is no easy feat, but she is an avid believer that the less stress you have, the better your chances of success.

“The heavier [this process feels], the more [of a] control freak that you become because you're scared, [and then] the more stress you're going to have, and that's not going to be good for your body,” she says. “If you're traveling for IVF, make it a relaxing, fun trip… Make it about an adventure for yourself, where you're exploring different things.” For Jung, that looked like attending Seoul Fashion Week and indulging in the city’s fine dining scene, while for some of her coaching clients, that meant professional color consultations, beauty treatments, and even weekend trips to Japan.

When Garay-Stanton reflects on her experience, she echoes a similar sentiment. Since the egg retrieval process, which is also the first phase of IVF, requires 10 to 14 days of daily, at-home hormone injections and doctor visits every few days, there is still plenty of time to explore your destination.

“I was going to the appointments, I was doing my shots, but during the day, I could just walk around the city,” she says. “We took a weekend trip to the Canary Islands, and I just brought all of my medication with me. So my mind was off of it, which I think was positive.”

What all these women have in common, myself included, is that we wish we had started our journeys sooner. Whether that means early hormone testing—as Jung and Purdie suggest—or simply knowing that going abroad is an option.

“The US has this mentality that we're so much better than everywhere else, and I think this process has really shown me that there could be even better options [outside the country],” Garay-Stanton says.

For those who may worry about the judgment of others when making this delicate decision, Purdie’s advice is simple: “Be honest with the path that you want in life and don't let anybody take it away from you. Because I almost did.”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

#5530. Transfer Amex Points To Marriott Bonvoy With 35% Bonus: Worth It?

The Amex Membership Rewards program frequently offers transfer bonuses. The program has just launched its latest hotel transfer bonus — while I wouldn’t say it’s particularly compelling, it’s at least worth being aware of.

Between October 20 and December 10, 2026, the Amex Membership Rewards program in the United States is offering a 35% bonus on Marriott Bonvoy® Points transfers.

The transfer ratio is usually 1:1, so through this promotion you can transfer points at a 1,000:1,350 ratio. This bonus is hardcoded into the transfer ratio, so you can take advantage of the bonus as often as you’d like. Transfers from Amex to Marriott Bonvoy are generally instant.

For what it’s worth, we typically see transfer bonuses between the programs an average of around twice per year, and they’re usually in the range of 20-35%. So this bonus is on the higher end of that scale, much better than the 20% bonus we saw earlier this year.

While a 35% bonus might sound exciting, not all points currencies are created equal. Personally, I value Bonvoy points at ~0.7 cents each, give or take. There are certainly ways to get outsized value from the points, but that’s my valuation.

By my valuation, you’re still getting under one cent of value per Membership Rewards point, even with this transfer bonus. That’s quite a ways from my valuation of Membership Rewards points, which is 1.7 cents per point.

Other people will likely have wildly different valuations of Marriott points than I do, and if that’s the case, this bonus could be worth it. That’s especially true when you consider there’s no great way to earn Marriott points through everyday credit card spending anymore. I tend to value points conservatively, and of course there are always ways to get outsized value with the right redemption in mind.

Amex Membership Rewards is offering a 35% bonus when you transfer points to Marriott Bonvoy. In general, I think transfer bonuses are the way to maximize the value you can get from transferable points. However, that’s usually only true for currencies where even transferring points at a 1:1 ratio could make sense.

In the case of Amex Membership Rewards and Marriott Bonvoy, I find that you lose more than half of the value of your points by making a transfer under normal circumstances. Even with a 35% bonus, I wouldn’t consider this to be much of a deal at all.

Of course, some people may have specific situations where this could make sense, and in that case, it could be worth at least topping off an account.

Does anyone plan on taking advantage of this Amex transfer bonus to Marriott Bonvoy?

#5529. Foxconn pays $727M for half of Mitsubishi Electric’s car parts unit in EV push

Foxconn is buying half of Mitsubishi Electric’s auto parts business for ¥115 billion ($727 million), its biggest bet yet on turning years of EV promises into a real car business.

But the target, Mitsubishi Electric Mobility, isn’t an EV company. It’s a traditional Tier 1 supplier that makes everything from alternators and ignition coils to radar sensors, with a smaller electric powertrain business attached.

Hon Hai Precision Industry, Foxconn’s formal name, announced on Friday that its investment arm, Lin Yin International Investment, signed a final agreement to take a 50% stake in the Mitsubishi Electric subsidiary. The two companies will run it as an equal joint venture.

The deal values the business at ¥320 billion ($2 billion) in enterprise value and ¥230 billion in equity value. Closing is expected on October 1, 2027, after antitrust reviews.

The partners want to combine Mitsubishi Electric’s electric powertrain, software and vehicle control technology with Foxconn’s “speed and scale” to develop EV platforms in Japan. They plan to sell them to Japanese automakers first, then globally under a “Made in/by Japan” banner.

They’re targeting more than ¥1 trillion ($6.3 billion) in revenue for the fiscal year starting April 2030.

Jun Seki, Foxconn’s EV chief strategy officer and a former Nissan executive, told reporters that Mitsubishi’s electronic control technology was “the most attractive thing” about the deal. He said Foxconn wants to make Mitsubishi Electric “our anchor in Japan and team up with other parts makers and automakers.”

Mitsubishi Electric spun its automotive equipment group into Mitsubishi Electric Mobility in April 2024. Before closing, it will fold the rest of its car business, including the automotive operations of its overseas subsidiaries, into the unit. That combined business is what Foxconn is buying into.

Here’s what’s in Mitsubishi Electric’s automotive catalog:

Mitsubishi Electric doesn’t break out revenue by product line. But its May 2025 strategy presentation points to “large market share” in rotary motors (alternators and starters) and car mechatronics, and says it runs its EV and combustion engine businesses “in a balanced way.” It’s already exiting car multimedia and fuel injectors, and it handed its lighting business to Stanley Electric. In July, the unit said it was reviewing its combustion engine business as EV demand slowed.

And the business is shrinking. It brought in ¥875.6 billion ($5.5 billion) in revenue and ¥54.4 billion in operating profit, a 6.2% margin, in the fiscal year that ended in March. That’s down ¥43.5 billion from the year before, which Mitsubishi Electric blamed on weaker sales by Japanese automakers in China and a smaller car multimedia business in North America. It expects ¥820 billion this year.

So the ¥1 trillion goal works out to roughly 5% growth a year. For a deal sold as an EV play, that’s modest.

Foxconn isn’t getting Mitsubishi Electric’s power semiconductors. The SiC and IGBT power modules that go inside EV inverters belong to a separate power device business, which Mitsubishi Electric put into merger talks with Rohm and Toshiba in March. The Foxconn deal covers the automotive equipment business only.

It isn’t getting a proven EV components partnership either. Mitsubishi Electric Mobility and Aisin agreed in May 2024 to form a joint venture for traction motors, inverters and control software. Five months later, they scrapped the JV and downgraded it to a business alliance.

Then there’s the price. The ¥320 billion enterprise value is about 0.37 times sales and roughly six times operating profit. The ¥230 billion equity value is also well below the roughly ¥352 billion in net assets on the business’s books (¥727.5 billion in assets minus ¥375.4 billion in liabilities), which is why Mitsubishi Electric expects to book a ¥100 billion impairment this fiscal year.

That’s not how anyone prices a growth business. Mitsubishi Electric is selling down a mature, low-margin division, much like Panasonic did when it sold a majority of its automotive systems unit to Apollo in 2024.

Foxconn has been talking big about EVs for six years. In October 2020, chairman Young Liu said the company wanted to supply components or services for 10% of the world’s EVs, around 3 million vehicles, sometime between 2025 and 2027.

Foxconn doesn't have much technology. It is a highly efficient and organized contract manufacturer. It is basically a modern sweatshop for all cheap labor based manufacturing.

Since then, it has unveiled a series of Foxtron prototypes, bought the former GM plant in Lordstown, Ohio, and lined up the Fisker Pear, and added a pickup and a crossover to the lineup. Fisker went bankrupt in 2024 and the Pear never made it to production. Foxconn sold the Lordstown factory last year for $375 million.

It also approached Nissan about a stake in early 2025, as the Honda-Nissan merger talks collapsed. Nothing came of it.

So far, the only legacy automaker to put its badge on a Foxtron-developed EV is Mitsubishi Motors, a separate company from Mitsubishi Electric. Its ASX VR-e, built by Yulon in Taiwan, is going on sale in Australia and New Zealand. Foxconn also has a bus joint venture with Mitsubishi Fuso and is a partner in Saudi Arabia’s Ceer EV brand.

Foxconn and Mitsubishi want to build the next wave of EV platforms, but you don’t have to wait for them to cut your driving costs: powering an EV with home solar can bring your fuel costs close to zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #5528. Versonix Expands Seaware Booking Platform to All 3 NCLH Brands

Versonix has announced the expansion of its relationship with Norwegian Cruise Line Holdings (NCLH), whose brands will adopt and upgrade its Seaware reservation platform.

As part of the expanded deal, Regent Seven Seas Cruises and Oceania Cruises will adopt the platform, while Norwegian Cruise Line will upgrade to its latest generation.

“Strong partnerships are built on shared accountability and execution. Together with Versonix, we are committed to delivering this transformation in a way that creates lasting value for our brands and guests,” said Daniel Henry, executive vice president and chief technology officer at NCLH.

Versonix describes the platform as a modern, scalable foundation for reservations, inventory, pricing and distribution.

Beyond modernizing core reservation capabilities, the platform is meant to enhance the guest experience with more personalized booking options, improved digital interactions, and a simpler, more seamless journey from trip planning through sailing, noted Versonix.

“We are proud to deepen our relationship with NCLH and support the next phase of its technology strategy. The expansion of Seaware across all three NCLH brands reflects the strength of our partnership and the value of a modern, scalable platform built to support Norwegian as a leading cruise operator,” added Yuri Polissky, chief operating officer of Versonix.

Other cruise lines that recently adopted the platform include Atlas Ocean Voyages, The Ritz-Carlton Yacht Collection and StarDream Cruises.

#5527. Transfer Capital One Miles To Virgin Atlantic Flying Club With 30% Bonus: Worth It?

Capital One has just introduced its latest points transfer promotion, whereby members can receive a 30% bonus when moving rewards to Virgin Atlantic Flying Club. This has the potential to be an excellent deal.

Between October 21 and November 20, 2026, it’s possible to get a 30% bonus when you transfer miles from Capital One to the Virgin Atlantic Flying Club program. This promotion is being offered on Capital One’s end, so you should see the bonus at the time that you make the transfer.

The transfer ratio is usually 1:1, so through this promotion you can transfer miles at a 1,000:1,300 ratio. The 30% bonus should post at the same time as the standard rewards. There’s no registration required, and no limit to how many bonus points you can earn. Transfers from Capital One to Virgin Atlantic are generally instant.

We see bonuses from various transferable points currencies to Virgin Atlantic every so often, in the range of 20-40%. So if you’re in the market for earning some Virgin Atlantic points, this is a good opportunity. We last saw a transfer bonus from Capital One to Virgin Atlantic roughly a year ago, and it was also for 30%.

Virgin Atlantic is definitely a frequent flyer program with more niche redemption opportunities. In addition to being able to redeem Flying Club points for travel on Virgin Atlantic, there’s also good value to be had for redemptions on Air New Zealand, All Nippon Airways, and Delta.

In late 2024 we saw Flying Club introduce dynamic award pricing for travel on Virgin Atlantic. While the changes are a mixed bag, what’s exciting is that on the low end, redemptions are cheaper than ever before. It’s now possible to fly one-way Virgin Atlantic Upper Class between the United States and the UK for under 30,000 points, and that’s before a transfer bonus.

Beyond that, Virgin Atlantic has several airline partners, some of which are more lucrative than others. Probably the best use of Flying Club points is for travel in ANA first & business class. You can find the redemption rates below.

Paying 45,000-47,500 Flying Club points for a one-way business class ticket between the United States and Japan is a phenomenal value to start with. Then when you consider the 30% transfer bonus, that means you could be paying significantly fewer Capital One miles. First class can also be a good deal.

All Nippon Airways’ new first & business class are amazing products as well. The major catch is that availability can be really tough to come by.

Between October 21 and November 20, 2026, you can receive a 30% bonus when you transfer miles from Capital One to Virgin Atlantic Flying Club. This bonus is being offered on Capital One’s end, so you should see the bonus right away, and can take advantage of this as often as you’d like.

This has the potential to be a good deal for redemptions on Virgin Atlantic, SkyTeam partners, and the program’s non-alliance partners.

#5524. Airport Luggage Tag Scam: How to Protect Your Checked Bags in 2026

Imagine walking up to customs after a long international flight and being told that a suitcase connected to your name has been flagged.

Then the officer points to the luggage tag and says, “Then why is your name on it?”

That is the nightmare behind a luggage scam travelers need to understand in 2026. It is called baggage tag switching, and it is exactly what it sounds like: the luggage tag from an innocent passenger’s checked suitcase is allegedly removed and placed onto another bag.

Baggage tag switching scams happen when the luggage tag from a traveler’s checked bag is allegedly removed and placed on another suitcase. To protect yourself, take a video of your suitcase before check-in, photograph the bag on the scale, take a close-up photo of the baggage tag, keep your baggage receipt, place a tracker inside your suitcase, and report any damaged, missing, delayed, or retagged bag before leaving the airport.

According to a W5 investigation, innocent passengers flying from Canada have been detained after their real luggage tags were allegedly moved onto suitcases containing illegal drugs. The investigation focused on flights from Canada and alleged cases connected to Toronto Pearson, but the larger lesson applies to travelers everywhere: once your checked bag disappears behind the counter, you are trusting the system to protect your luggage, your tag, and your name.

We have traveled to more than 130 countries on all seven continents, and checked bags have always been part of travel. But the old habit of simply handing over your suitcase and hoping for the best needs an update.

In 2026, protecting your checked luggage is not just about stopping theft. It is about protecting the paper trail that connects you to your bag.

This is part of a bigger shift in how travelers need to think about airport safety, baggage scams, and common airport security mistakes before they fly.

Editor’s note: This article is based on our video about baggage tag switching, which has reached more than 86,000 views on our travel YouTube channel.

Before you check a bag, take two minutes to create proof that the bag is yours.

It also helps to run through a full pre-travel checklist before you leave for the airport so you are not rushing through check-in.

This may sound like a lot, but it takes less than two minutes at check-in. And those two minutes could matter if your name ever gets attached to a suitcase that is not yours.

We covered this luggage tag scam in detail in the video below, including the simple two-minute routine we now recommend before checking a bag.

Baggage tag switching is when the tag printed for your checked luggage is removed from your actual suitcase and attached to another bag.

When you check luggage, the airline prints a baggage tag that usually includes your name, flight, destination, routing information, barcode, and bag number. That tag is the airport system’s way of connecting that suitcase to you.

The problem is that once your bag goes behind the counter, you cannot see what happens next.

In the alleged scheme reported by W5, corrupt baggage or ramp workers could remove a legitimate tag from a passenger’s suitcase and attach it to another suitcase heading to the same destination. If that second bag is intercepted, the paperwork points back to the innocent passenger.

That is what makes this scam so dangerous. You may have done everything right: checked your own suitcase, kept your boarding pass, and boarded your flight. But behind the scenes, one strip of paper may now connect your name to a bag you have never seen.

Most airport workers are honest. Let’s be clear about that. The vast majority are doing hard jobs under pressure, moving thousands of bags through complex systems every day. But a baggage system only needs a few bad actors to create a very serious problem.

This story has received attention because of Canadian cases, but this is not only a Canada problem.

The bigger issue is that every major airport has the same basic weak point: you hand over your suitcase, it enters a restricted area, and from that point on, you are relying on the system to protect the tag, the bag, and the records connected to your name.

That is true in Toronto. It is true in New York, London, Paris, Dubai, Bangkok, Mexico City, Sydney, and anywhere else checked luggage moves behind the scenes.

International travel also adds another layer of risk. If your name gets tied to a suspicious bag in another country, you may be dealing with a different legal system, different customs procedures, and possibly a language barrier.

The Government of Canada warns travelers that drug offences abroad can have serious consequences and that you should never carry anything across a border for someone else. The Canada Border Services Agency also specifically reminds travelers that trafficking cannabis or other drugs across the border is a criminal offence and warns people to beware of anyone asking them to carry something.

That is why documenting your own luggage matters. You are not being paranoid. You are creating a simple evidence trail.

Before your suitcase disappears behind the counter, take a clear video of it.

Do a full 360-degree view. Show the front, back, sides, wheels, handles, color, brand, straps, stickers, scratches, dents, or anything that makes your bag identifiable.

Yes, you may feel ridiculous filming your suitcase at the airport. Do it anyway. People film lattes. You can film the thing that might keep you out of a holding room.

This video creates a time-stamped record of what your suitcase looked like before it left your hands. If someone later claims a different bag is yours, you have proof of what your actual luggage looked like at check-in.

When your bag is placed on the airline scale, take a quick photo or video showing the weight.

This matters because weight is part of the paper trail. If your suitcase weighed 19 kilograms at check-in and another suitcase connected to your name weighs 32 kilograms, that difference may matter.

You are not trying to become a detective. You are simply creating a record that says this was your bag, this was the weight, this was the condition, and this was the tag attached to it.

That is the kind of simple documentation that can help if your bag is delayed, damaged, retagged, or questioned later.

Once the airline attaches the baggage tag to your suitcase, take a close-up photo or video of it before the bag goes onto the conveyor belt.

Make sure you can clearly see the barcode, destination airport, flight number, routing, and bag number. Also check that the airline has looped and attached the tag properly.

You are not being difficult. You are checking the one strip of paper connecting you to your luggage. That little tag is doing a lot of legal heavy lifting.

Air Canada’s own baggage information explains that self-service kiosks can print baggage tags, receipts, and boarding passes, and that travelers then attach the tags and proceed to bag drop. That means passengers are often handling or reviewing this paperwork themselves before the bag disappears into the system.

Never throw away the little baggage receipt sticker you receive at check-in.

Keep it somewhere safe. Don’t stuff it in a random pocket, lose it before boarding, or assume the airline app is enough. Put it with your passport or travel documents until your suitcase is back in your hands.

That receipt usually contains your bag number. If something goes wrong, that number becomes part of the evidence trail. That number can help airline staff or investigators compare what you checked, what arrived, and whether someone intercepted or retagged another bag.

This is one of the easiest travel habits to fix, and it costs nothing.

Before you close your checked bag, take one clear photo of the inside.

You do not need to photograph every sock like you are filing a museum inventory. Just take one clear photo of the contents: clothes, shoes, toiletries, chargers, jackets, and the usual chaos of travel.

This helps show what you actually packed. If a suitcase connected to your name contains items you have never seen, you have a record of what was inside your real bag before check-in.

This is especially useful on international trips, where proving what you packed may become more complicated once you are outside your home country.

It is also a good reminder to double-check what not to pack in checked luggage before you leave for the airport.

A luggage tracker will not stop someone from tampering with a baggage tag, but it can help show where your real suitcase went.

We use Apple AirTags, but there are also Tile trackers, Samsung SmartTags, and other luggage tracking options.

The important thing is this: put the tracker inside your suitcase. Do not hang it outside where it can easily be removed.

If authorities are asking about a suitcase connected to your name and your tracker shows your actual suitcase is somewhere else, that becomes another part of your evidence trail.

Trackers are also helpful if your bag arrives late, gets separated from its original tag, or ends up in the wrong baggage area. They are not perfect, but they give travelers one more layer of information when airline systems fail.

We use AirTags in our checked bags, but Samsung SmartTags and Tile trackers are also good options depending on your phone. The key is to keep the tracker inside the suitcase where it cannot easily be removed.

We also recommend pairing a luggage tracker with a few simple anti-theft travel accessories especially on longer international trips.

A plain black suitcase is basically the airport version of camouflage.

Make your luggage easier to identify with a bright strap, ribbon, sticker, or distinctive luggage tag. You want to be able to spot your bag quickly and prove what makes it different from hundreds of others.

But do not put too much personal information on the outside. Your name and phone number are enough.

You do not need your home address waving around the baggage hall like an invitation that says, “Please rob us while we’re in Italy.”

Keep luggage identifiable, but not overly revealing.

This rule has always mattered, but it matters even more now.

Never carry a package, gift, suitcase, envelope, or item for someone else. That means no favors for strangers, no “harmless” packages, and no quick airport errands for someone you barely know.

If you did not pack it, you do not carry it.

The CBSA specifically warns travelers to beware of people who ask them to carry anything and notes that consequences for drug trafficking can be serious in Canada and abroad.

That is the cleanest rule in travel: your bag, your responsibility.

If your checked bag arrives without the original tag, with a damaged tag, with a rush tag, or through a different baggage process than expected, do not just shrug and leave the airport.

Do not rely on a quick conversation at the counter. Conversations disappear the second you walk away. Written reports create a record.

This matters if your bag was mishandled, delayed, retagged, or separated from its original tag.

When your suitcase comes out at baggage claim, do not grab it on autopilot and leave.

Check that it is actually yours. Look at the tag, compare the bag number against your receipt, check your tracker, inspect the condition, and make sure the contents have not obviously been disturbed.

We have seen people accidentally walk away with someone else’s luggage. It happens more easily than people think, especially after long-haul flights when everyone is tired and half the carousel is filled with identical black suitcases.

Before you leave the airport or customs area, take one minute to confirm everything looks right.

Not necessarily. We have traveled for decades, and carry-on only is not always realistic. Sometimes you need to check a bag, especially on longer trips, family trips, cruises, cold-weather destinations, camera-heavy travel, or extended international travel.

For a smoother trip overall, our easy air travel tips can help before you even reach baggage claim.

For longer flights, our long-haul flight tips can also help make the journey easier before you even get to baggage claim.

The point is not to scare people out of checking luggage. The point is to update your habits.

The old way of checking luggage was simple: check the bag, keep the receipt somewhere, and hope for the best.

It sounds like a lot, but it takes less than two minutes — and those two minutes could make a huge difference if your name ever gets attached to a suitcase that is not yours.

If authorities ever question you about a suitcase that you do not recognize, stay calm.

Do not guess, don’t joke and do not become argumentative.

Ask for legal assistance and show the documentation you collected: your suitcase video, scale photo, baggage tag photo, receipt, tracker information, and photos of what you packed.

This article is not legal advice, but from a traveler’s perspective, the goal is simple: create proof before there is a problem.

If your name is ever attached to a bag you did not pack, your documentation may help show what your real suitcase looked like, what it weighed, what was inside it, and where it went.

Before every checked bag disappears behind the counter, take two minutes to create a simple record of your suitcase.

That’s it. Two minutes. No drama. No paranoia. Just smart travel.

Most airport workers are honest, and most checked bags arrive without a problem. But airport systems are massive, complicated, and not completely invisible-proof. Once your bag disappears behind the counter, you are trusting a chain of people, machines, tags, scans, and systems to keep your suitcase connected to your name.

So before your next flight, take two minutes to protect yourself. Film your bag. Photograph your tag. Keep your receipt. Use a tracker. And never carry anything for anyone else.

The goal is not to travel scared. The goal is to travel smarter.

Because when travelers get smarter, scams get riskier for the people trying to pull them off.

Carrion crows made decisions about their visual environments significantly faster than humans and primates tend to, according to a comparison in a new study.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Crows are intelligent animals that can use tools, hold grudges and even host funerals. Now, new research suggests these birds can make certain decisions faster than humans and monkeys — another potential sign of their smarts, the researchers argue.

In a study published Thursday (Oct. 22) in the journal Science, researchers found that two carrion crows (Corvus corone) could distinguish the direction of moving dots on a screen in an average of 235 milliseconds, or less than a quarter of a second. By contrast, historical studies of humans suggest they make decisions in similar tasks six seconds as fast.

The birds were highly accurate, and recordings of their brain activity suggested that their decision-making process resembled that seen in primates and humans.

Given birds and mammals have evolved along separate paths for more than 310 million years, "this suggests that evolution may have independently developed similar solutions to the same computational problem," study co-author Andreas Nieder, a professor of animal physiology and director of the Institute of Neurobiology at the University of Tübingen in Germany, told Live Science in an email.

To understand how crows make decisions using cues in their environments, the researchers trained two carrion crows to complete a visual task on a touchscreen. The birds watched a cloud of white dots moving against a black background. Some dots moved to the left or right while others moved randomly. The crows had to determine the predominant direction of the dots' movement and peck the corresponding target on the screen to earn a mealworm reward.

By changing the proportion of dots moving together, the researchers could make the decision easier or harder for the crow. When most dots moved in the same direction, the answer was fairly obvious for the birds. But when only a small percentage moved together, the birds had weaker visual evidence to base their decisions on.

One of the carrion crows used in the study completes a decision-making task.

The crows reached nearly 100% accuracy when many dots were involved and the motion was clear. But even when just 3% of the dots moved in concert, both birds performed significantly better than just guessing, getting the answer right 50% of the time.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

The birds maintained high accuracy in different rounds of the experiment, suggesting they were not sacrificing judgement for quickness.

The study did not directly compare crows to humans and monkeys performing the exact same task. However, previous studies suggest that humans and monkeys take roughly twice as long on comparable, self-paced visual motion tasks.

To understand how the birds reached their decisions so quickly, the researchers recorded activity from 248 neurons in the nidopallium caudolaterale (NCL) — a region of the bird brain that's considered functionally comparable to the prefrontal cortex in humans and primates. Birds lack the layered cerebral cortex found in mammalian brains, but the NCL performs similarly advanced functions.

In primates, neurons accumulate sensory information and their activity increases until it hits a critical threshold and the brain reaches a decision. The new findings suggest that crows use a comparable process, the researchers concluded.

"The bigger implication is that a brain does not need a mammalian neocortex to perform sophisticated computations," Nieder said.

The team could use neural activity to predict the crows' eventual choice "as early as 70 milliseconds after" the dots appeared, Nieder said. That was roughly 170 milliseconds before the crows' behavioral responses, meaning their pecks at the screen.

The speediness of the crows' decision-making may reflect the demands of the birds' daily lives.

"Flight places enormous demands on visual processing and sensorimotor [sensation and movement] coordination," Nieder said. "Birds must navigate three-dimensional environments, avoid obstacles, and react quickly to moving objects. Finding food and capturing prey can also require rapid, precise decisions. These pressures may have favoured fast information processing."

But for now, scientists don't know why crows seem to outpace primates in this particular type of decision-making. The study included only two birds and no direct comparisons to primates, so more work could be done to confirm the results and explore that question further.

For Nieder, observing the faster decision-making process in crows raises a broader question about how scientists define intelligence.

"Rather than assuming that the mammalian brain is the blueprint for intelligent behaviour," he said, "we can study different species to discover which computational principles are shared across animals and which reflect adaptations to their particular environments."

Schmidbauer, Philipp., Nieder, Andreas. (2026). Ultrarapid decision-making in an avian brain. Science (394). https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aed6459

Kenna is also a book author, with her upcoming book 'Octopus X' scheduled for release in spring of 2027. Her beats include physics, health, environmental science, technology, AI, animal intelligence, corvids, and cephalopods.

#5522. How I Got My Visa to France

I’ve been in love with France ever since my first trip to Bordeaux in 2010. Friends I had met back in Thailand showed me around the area and introduced me to French culture. It was my first time in France and I loved the food, the wine, and the people.

But that love became an obsession when, after Bordeaux, I stepped out of the Paris metro and onto the Champs-Élysées. The lights, the energy, the mystique! There was magic in the air. I felt like I had known the city my entire life and I was simply returning home.

Since then, Paris has had a firm grip on my heart. Over the years, I spent countless visits exploring France and Paris in particular. I’ve run tours in the city and I spent a few months living there in 2019.

Last August, after yet another sojourn, I thought to myself, “What if I moved back?”

I was growing a bit tired of the NYC dating scene, the rising cost of living, and felt like, creatively, I was in a rut. With AI coming for creators like me and the industry changing, I was a little lost on what, career-wise, would come next.

And Paris seemed like the best place to go.

It’s cheaper than NYC, I had friends there already, I’ve always wanted to learn French, it would make a great base for exploring Europe, and I could start writing my next book there. There’s nothing like a change in scenery to get the creativity flowing!

But the question remained: how do you move to France?

After all, they don’t have a digital nomad visa, and you’re limited to three months if you visit on your regular Schengen tourist visa.

Well, it turns out that it’s not actually that hard. (And while I can only speak for Americans, I suspect the criteria are similar for Canadians, Aussies, Kiwis, and other “developed” nations.)

If you want to move to France, you have four main options:

The student visa is pretty straightforward. You need to enroll in a full-time university program and show you have enough funds to support yourself (around 600 Euros per month). A friend is currently there on this visa, as she is doing a nine-month intensive French program to become fluent. You still have to apply for the visa, but if you’re enrolled in an accredited school, you’ll likely get approved. Another benefit to this visa is that you can work part-time on it!

But this visa is contingent on being in school, so if you leave or quit, it’s voided. However, when you are done, you can change this to a “job seeker” visa, which can give you up to another six months in France (or more depending on how long your program was).

I thought about applying for the entrepreneur visa, but the application process is pretty complex and can take months to be approved. You have to set up your business in France — and that is a lot of paperwork. You have to show that your business makes money, has clients (at least one of them being French), and can support you full-time (you have to make at least the French minimum wage). They will scrutinize your assets and financials a lot and you’ll need to register your business in France and pay French taxes.

Additionally, France offers a talent visa. If you’re an expert in your field (with verifiable accreditations and accolades), this could be a good visa to apply for, especially if you plan to stay in France for a long time and want to physically work there. But, again, you’ll need to have some source of income and plan to do physical business in France. If you’re not an academic but someone in the arts, you have to show how you are going to add to the “culture of France” in some way.

While I would meet the requirements for both those visas, the process for either would have been time-consuming, and, since I’m not sure France is going to be my forever home, I decided not to go through that process.

So I went with the long-term visitor visa, officially called VLS-TS visiteur. This allows me to stay up to 12 months in France and is renewable in the country. It does come with a lot of restrictions, however: I can’t physically work here and I am not allowed into the French social welfare system.

It’s also the visa a lot of Americans (and most retirees) are coming in on. If you have passive income or retirement savings and just want to live in France, this is a good option for you. It allows you to set up a bank account and it can be renewed pretty much indefinitely.

The paperwork for this visa (which I’ll get into in a bit) essentially comes down to whether or not you can support yourself. French authorities want to make sure you won’t be a burden on the system.

My biggest question centered around whether or not I could “work” on this visa. A lot of creators and digital nomads are applying for — and getting — this visa.

But remember: you aren’t allowed to work on this visa so how are you going to work if you can’t work?

So let me take a moment to talk about “work” from a legal standpoint.

As I mentioned, there’s no digital nomad visa that allows you to work in France. And the French tax office said last July that remote work is considered taxable — but there are no official laws that say so. That’s just their opinion and there’s been no progress in codifying that opinion. In fact, my visa doesn’t even come with a tax number, so I can’t be taxed. I couldn’t pay taxes even if I wanted to.

The visa office asks how you’ll support yourself when you are in France and one of the sources of income they accept is “income from work.” I was very open that my means of support would be the income from this blog and my books.

The system there really hasn’t caught up to remote work. It still defines “work” as something you do physically that could take away a job from a French person.

So could I run walking tours in Paris? Nope. Could you work at a café? Nope.

But does writing this blog post count as work? After all, no one is paying for it. It’s a free resource. Does going to a café to work on the next great American novel count? What about my weekly newsletter that has affiliate links (that generate income) in it? Is writing that considered work?

The gray area is considerable, so the best thing to do is to talk to a lawyer. I recommend Daniel Tostado (yes, that’s his real name), one of the biggest American-French immigration lawyers in Paris. He has a huge law firm and regularly meets with government officials to clarify the rules on this. He gave me some solid advice.

For this long-term visitor visa, the application process is pretty easy. You go to the French government website, fill out your application, take the application number to the TLScontact website, and make an appointment. (France outsources the appointment process to this company, which then sends everything to the French consulate for approval.)

The main things you’ll want to demonstrate are that you have accommodation for 90 days on your application and enough income to support yourself. They just want to make sure you aren’t going to be a burden on the system. I went overboard. Here’s what I brought to my appointment:

I didn’t need the second bank account, tax returns, or W-2 (they weren’t required) but the woman at the appointment said the more proof you had the better and it wouldn’t hurt. And another friend who got this visa didn’t bring anything beyond what was asked in the application form. But I wanted to take no chances!

At the appointment, the staff double-checks your paperwork, takes your biometric data, collects the fees, and then sends your passport to the embassy for processing. Officially, it takes up to 15 days to process your visa, but everyone I know seems to get it back within a week.

The worst part was not knowing if you are approved until your passport is returned. While you can track the progress of your application, you don’t know the verdict until your passport is back in your hands. While I was waiting (and anxiously spiraling), I looked on Reddit and found that most denials were for lack of income or issues with their accommodation, which seem to be the two most important things to have perfect on your application.

Once you get your visa back, you can enter France any time after the start date. After you enter, you have 90 days to register your visa with the state (they give you a little QR code with instructions). This allows you to “officially” be on this long-term visa. (It’s a more paperwork kind of thing.)

Once that is done, there’s nothing else to do and you can stay in France (and Europe) until your visa expires!

So, if you’re considering moving to France, these are your options. But, as always, I’m not a lawyer and this is not legal advice. In regard to work and other issues or questions, it’s important that you seek legal counsel and get their expert opinion!

#5521. Japan is Making it Harder for Non-Citizens to Become Permanent Residents

Tourists from around the world continue to flock to Japan’s bustling megacities and quiet onsen towns, generating a record-setting (and sometimes unmanageable) influx of travelers: an increase from 25.1 million in 2023 to 42.7 million in 2025, according to the country’s tourism board. In the face of this boom, Japan is setting more boundaries with visitors, including levying an increased tourist tax and, as of October 1, making it harder for non-citizens to obtain residence status.

The number of foreign residents in Japan reached a record 3.96 million people in June 2025, prompting Japan’s immigration agency to tighten its eligibility guidelines for the country's permanent residency permit, which is available to foreign workers who have continuously lived in Japan for at least 10 years. The new rules are just one part of a larger framework of policies adopted by the Japanese government on January 23, 2026 called the “Comprehensive Measures for the Acceptance and Orderly Coexistence of Foreign Nationals.”

The government says the policies are designed to address “anxieties and feelings of unfairness felt by some citizens as the number of foreign residents increases,” Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi wrote in a September 26 post on X. That means applicants to Japan's foreign residence permit will soon face stricter language proficiency requirements, more expensive fees, and a higher income threshold.

If you're pursuing permanent residency or a long-term stay in Japan, here's what you need to know.

The new requirements dictate that applicants must earn more than the average Japanese household income (which was about $36,350 per year in 2024, according to a survey by the country’s health, labor, and welfare ministry). In addition, applicants must now show pension benefits that match what they would have received after 30 years in the country’s employee pension program. (If the applicant falls short on the pension requirement, the guidelines say they can supplement with additional “financial assets," but does not provide specifics beyond that.)

The government also announced a dramatic 20-fold hike in the application fee, which is now 20,000 yen, or about $1,260. That said, the cost is still relatively affordable compared to permanent residency-related applications in countries like the UK, which cost 3,226 British pounds (about $4,261), and the US, which can cost upwards of $10,000, according to Arton.

Qualifying for Japan's permanent residency visa isn't just about financial eligibility. Applicants will also need a proficient understanding of the Japanese language, which the guidelines define as at or above a B1 level on the Japanese-Language Proficiency Test; however, it’s not yet clear how applicants will be asked to demonstrate their language ability.

The income changes will retroactively affect applications filed from April 2026. Other changes, such as the pension requirement, will be implemented starting April 1, 2027.

“For Americans thinking of making Japan their long-term home, the message is clear: financial security alone is no longer enough,” Armand Arton, CEO of citizenship firm Arton Capital, tells Condé Nast Traveler. “Applicants must also demonstrate a genuine commitment to living and working in the country, as well as integrating into Japanese society.”

To apply, you need to have lived in Japan for 10 continuous years, with at least five of those years under a work or residence visa. If you’ve been out of the country for more than six consecutive months without a “reasonable” justification, or for a total of two-and-a-half years over the decade, your application could receive “negative” marks.

That said, there are exceptions to the 10-year rule: For example, if you’ve been deemed a “highly skilled foreign professional” or if you’ve been married to a Japanese resident for at least five years (and your spouse has lived in the country for at least three continuous years).

Japan doesn’t want new residents who will bounce in and out of the country, but rather those who will commit to building a life locally: “It’s offering quality of life and cultural immersion, rather than passport power and access,” says Arton.

If you meet these payment, income, language, and residency requirements, you can submit your application to a local immigration office in Japan. Applicants should prepare documents such as a copy of your health insurance card and proof that you’ve paid your resident taxes on time (through means such as providing a copy of your bank passbook)

After everything has been submitted, expect a four-to-six-month processing timeline (all form and application details are available at Japan’s immigration agency website).

If you don’t meet the requirements for a permanent residency permit, Japan offers several temporary relocation pathways.

If you’re deemed a “highly skilled professional” who will drive the local economy and academic research, you could get a five-year highly skilled professional residence visa. If you work as an entertainer, a chef specializing in an international cuisine, or in one of the other qualifying industries, you could apply for a working visa, which lasts between three months and five years. (You can check whether you qualify for the highly skilled professional or work visa by checking the evaluation guidelines on Japan’s immigration services website.)

If you have Japanese ancestry, consider applying for a long-term resident visa, which grants you between six months and five years in the country. Or, if you work remotely and want to stay in Japan for six months, the digital nomad visa may be the best fit.

A comprehensive list of all of Japan’s visas is available on its foreign affairs ministry website.
