#5520. Reality Might Be ‘Leaking’ Into a Fifth Dimension—And Dark Energy Could Be the Smoking Gun, Scientists Say

If we find that gravity starts acting weird at the scale of a micron, it could trigger a process of discovery that could finally explain the biggest mysteries of the cosmos.

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

Imagine you are standing in a dark, quiet kitchen at midnight. You’re reaching for a glass of water, confident that the counter is where it’s always been, that the floor is solid, and that the air around you is, well, just air. You feel like you have a pretty good handle on how the room—and the world—works.

But what if there was a trapdoor? Not a physical one in the linoleum, but a hidden dimension tucked into the very fabric of space, no larger than a speck of dust, yet governing everything you see.

That’s a good analogy for a cosmic puzzle that scientists have been trying to solve for decades. It stems from the fact that our best approximation of how the universe works just doesn’t fit with reality. On one hand, we have the standard model, our remarkably successful “instruction manual” for subatomic particles. On the other, we have dark energy—that mysterious, ghostly pressure pushing the universe to expand faster and faster. The problem is that our math says dark energy should be enormous, but our telescopes show it’s incredibly tiny. It’s a mismatch of epic proportions.

So, how do we fix a universe model that doesn’t add up?

In a paper published on arXiv in September 2024, researchers Ignatios Antoniadis, PhD, from Sorbonne University and Dieter Lüst, PhD, from the Max Planck Institute have proposed a radical “top-down” explanation. They aren’t just tweaking the dials; they are suggesting that our entire four-dimensional reality is actually “leaking” into a fifth dimension.

They call it the “Dark Dimension” scenario.

In the world of string theory—our best guess at a “theory of everything”—we usually imagine extra dimensions as being curled up so tight they are practically invisible, like the thickness of a thread viewed from miles away. But Antoniadis and Lüst suggest something different. Using a framework called “weakly coupled string theory,” they’ve derived a model where one of these extra dimensions is actually quite large.

That might sound small, but in the world of particle physics, a micron—the size of a single bacterium—is gargantuan. It’s a mesoscopic scale, a middle ground between the quantum and the cosmic. If this dimension exists, it’s like finding out your studio apartment actually has a massive, hidden ballroom just behind the wallpaper.

This is called “UV-IR mixing.” Usually, we assume that the physics of the very small (the ultraviolet) and the physics of the very large (the infrared) don’t talk to each other. They stay in their own lanes. But the authors argue that in quantum gravity, these scales are tethered together. The tiny value of dark energy we observe is actually a signal—a breadcrumb trail—pointing directly to the existence of this hidden, micron-sized dimension.

It is a bold claim, but the math is compelling. By looking at how strings “slosh” around on a worldsheet (the two-dimensional surface a string traces through space), the researchers found that if you want to keep the universe stable and explain dark energy without “fine-tuning”—which is physicist-speak for "fudging the numbers"—you almost inevitably end up with this dark dimension.

If this fifth dimension is real, it changes everything about our future in the stars. It means that gravity isn’t just a weak force; it’s a force that is “diluted” because it’s spreading out into that extra space. It also suggests that there is a “tower” of light particles—a cosmic ladder of new physics—waiting for us just beyond our current reach.

Experimentalists are already looking for “short-range” gravity deviations. If we find that gravity starts acting weird at the scale of a micron, we’ll know Antoniadis and Lüst are onto something. We would be looking at the first potential physical proof of string theory.

But for now, it remains a beautiful, mathematically rigorous hypothesis. We are still peering through the keyhole, trying to see the shape of the room beyond.

And that’s the thrill of it. We are finally learning how to ask the right questions. The dark dimension might be a mirage, or it might be the foundation of everything. Who knows what we’ll see when we keep peeking around corners?

Paul M. Sutter is a science educator and a cosmologist at Johns Hopkins University and the author of How to Die in Space: A Journey Through Dangerous Astrophysical Phenomena and Your Place in the Universe: Understanding Our Big, Messy Existence. Sutter is also the host of various science programs, and he’s on social media. Check out his Ask a Spaceman podcast and his YouTube page.

#5519. Philippine Airlines Business Class Airbus A330 Review: Friendly Crew, Terrible Seats

To kick off the return portion of my first class quest to Japan, I flew Philippine Airlines’ Airbus A330-300 business class on the 3hr15min flight from Osaka Kansai (KIX) to Manila (MNL). This flight was a mixed bag.

On the plus side, the crew was friendly, and the food was quite good. However, this particular version of the Philippine Airlines A330 has what I’d consider to be the world’s worst fully flat business class. The seats are just so poorly designed, that I wished I just had the carrier’s standard regional business class, with non-flat beds.

Of course on a relatively short daytime flight this wasn’t a huge issue, but I still judge airline products competitively, and in that sense, this is a really underwhelming hard product.

I booked my return itinerary from Osaka to Manila to San Francisco as one ticket, with the first flight on the Airbus A330, and the second flight on the Airbus A350. I booked the following ticket, which cost $1,746.63:

9/19 PR411 Osaka to Manila departing 3:15PM arriving 6:40PM9/19 PR104 Manila to San Francisco departing 11:15PM arriving 9:10PM

However, I ended up offsetting the cost of this ticket with points, so I really booked it with 116,442 Chase Ultimate Rewards points, because I could redeem for 1.5 cents each toward the cost of the ticket. That’s a reasonable paid fare, and in this case, I preferred redeeming points rather than having to spend the cash out of pocket.

I arrived at Kansai Osaka International Airport Terminal 1 at around 1PM. I had to stop at the Philippine Airlines check-in counter, since online check-in wasn’t possible for my itinerary, for whatever reason. That was painless, except I was informed that my seat was inoperable, so I had to be moved to another seat.

Security and immigration were quick, and I headed to the KIX Lounge Kansai, which I had access to on account of my ticket. I won’t be reviewing the lounge as it was full, but it was decent enough for a contract lounge.

My flight was departing from gate 21, just a short walk from the lounge, with boarding scheduled for 2:30PM, 45 minutes before departure. There I saw the 12-year-old Airbus A330-300 with the registration code RP-C8766, which would be my ride to Manila.

Boarding started right on schedule, with business class passengers being invited to board first.

I boarded the Philippine Airlines A330 through the forward left door, where I was greeted by the friendly crew. I headed straight to seat 1C, which I had been assigned (well, reassigned), and that’s the aisle seat on the left side in the first row.

Let me mention that Philippine Airlines has two A330 configurations — the superior version has business class seats in a 1-2-1 configuration, and the inferior one (which I was on) has business class seats in a 2-2-2 configuration. This configuration I was on is used primarily for routes within Asia, as well as for flights to Saudi Arabia.

The business class cabin had 18 seats, spread across three rows, in a 2-2-2 configuration. Look, I don’t want to be a drama queen (okay, too late!) — these seats aren’t the end of the world for a fairly short daytime flight. But still, who on earth designed these?! I’m not exaggerating when I say that I’d prefer a much less spacious recliner seat to this, because this seat’s design is so unusably stupid.

For what it’s worth, this is the same type of business class seats you’ll find on some of Royal Air Maroc’s Boeing 787s. The difference here is that the cabins are even more beat up, there’s no seat back entertainment, and the seats are even narrower (given the reduced width of the A330 cabin).

So where do we begin? First, in terms of padding, these seats are like sitting on an ironing board. These have to be among the thinnest and least padded business class seats out there. Also, as you can see below, there’s just nowhere to place things. There’s a tiny storage compartment behind the seat and to the side, but it’s almost unusably small.

Then the seat controls as well as the charging (AC and USB-A options) are on a panel between seats. There’s also a small cutout where you can place a drink.

In theory you can sort of place things on the back of the seat in front (it’s where the menus and slippers were stored during boarding), but you can’t use that space during takeoff and landing, and you also have to stand up to reach it.

In order to make the layout as tight as possible, these seats have what I like to call the top/bottom layout. The idea is that when seats recline, one seat raises, while the other lowers. The logic is so that one footwell can be on top of the other. It’s just absurdly stupid and unpleasant when one person reclines, and one doesn’t.

The tray table folds out from the side of the seat. As if the design isn’t poorly thought out enough already, the tray table also struggles to “latch on” to the opposite side, so it constantly just “bounces” down. It’s not often everyone has to take out their tray table to even have a pre-departure beverage, but that’s what happened here. And let’s not even talk about how many drink spills I saw on this flight.

With no seat back entertainment, can I also mention how plain low cost and unappealing the cabin looks?

That says nothing of the cabin maintenance issues.

I had initially assigned myself one of the center seats in the third row, and both of those center seats had headrests that said “do not occupy.” Does anyone know the story here? Did they both happen to be broken, are these just permanently blocked for crew rest (even if there’s not an augmented crew), or what?

Unfortunately I can’t even give the airline credit for having individual air nozzles on these A330s, as those weren’t present either.

I’m going to argue that this particular plane has the world’s worst flat bed business class product. At least Royal Air Maroc has seat back entertainment. Let me of course acknowledge that Philippine Airlines is currently trying to improve and become more premium, and this is an older generation aircraft for the airline. But still, that doesn’t mean I’m not going to judge the airline based on the product I actually experience.

Philippine Airlines A330s don’t have Wi-Fi, unfortunately. Furthermore, this version of the A330 didn’t have any seat back entertainment. The streaming entertainment included movies, TV shows, audio, games, and even a flight map. The selection wasn’t bad — for example, there were around 100 movies.

After takeoff, the crew came through business class with tablets that could be used to stream the entertainment. The catch is that there’s not actually anywhere to place these, other than your tray table (which is flimsy, and keeps falling). For that matter, there’s nowhere to put this while eating, since the meal tray takes up the whole table. I also wasn’t very impressed by the low quality earbuds offered with this.

When it comes to business class amenities, Philippine Airlines had a pretty nice pillow but a small and scratchy blanket.

Slippers were also placed at each seat during boarding. While they were flimsy, they ultimately served the purpose of putting on when going to the lavatory.

The menu card and drink list was also placed at the seat before boarding.

During boarding, the crew came around to offer pre-departure drinks, with the choice of a kiwi smoothie, orange juice, or the signature “Mabuhay Punch.” I opted for the kiwi smoothie, which was tasty (and quite sweet).

That was it in the way of amenities. As you’d expect, there were no amenity kits on a daytime flight this short.

This Philippine Airlines flight was packed, with nearly all seats (including all business class seats — well, except the two blocked ones) occupied. Nonetheless boarding was efficient, and wrapped up by 3PM, 15 minutes ahead of the scheduled departure time. The door closed immediately, though we didn’t move for some time.

At 3:15PM the captain made his welcome aboard announcement, also informing us of a slight delay due to traffic, and he explained we should be pushing back in around five minutes.

We ended up pushing back at 3:25PM, at which point the crew performed the manual safety demonstration. We started our taxi at 3:30PM, and we then had a bit of a queue for takeoff. At 3:50PM we were cleared for takeoff on runway 6L.

The views on departure were great, and I wish I had a window seat so that I could’ve taken pictures. We even had a nice view of Kansai Osaka Airport on departure, which is always fun, given that the airport is on its own island.

Our climb out was smooth, and the seatbelt sign was turned off five minutes after takeoff.

Lunch was served on this flight, and meal orders had been taken on the ground. You can find the business class menu and drink list below.

Guys, I know pork is a very commonly eaten dish in the Philippines, but is it really necessary to put pork in both of the western dining options? So you can either have pork or chicken with ham? Philippine Airlines is increasingly trying to pick up connecting traffic between other countries, so you’d think they could just put a morsel of thought into catering to diverse preferences and dietary restrictions.

That being said, it wasn’t a big deal for me, since I was going to have the Japanese meal anyway. Service began with a warm towel, served on a cute little tray.

I then stupidly ordered the sparkling wine, which was Bottega Gold Prosecco — I don’t recommend it. That was served with some tasty seaweed and prawn crackers.

The flight attendant saw me taking pictures of things, so asked if I wanted a picture of the bottle. Sure… Ace of Spades it is not (despite the similar-looking bottle)!

The main course was served around an hour after takeoff. The Japanese dish had mackerel sushi, shrimp, a rolled omelet, a skewered chicken meatball, Japanese spinach salad, matcha soda with dried seaweed, pickled radish, miso soup, teriyaki beef rolls, and more.

This was a solid meal, which isn’t surprising, given that it was catered in Japan.

With the main course, I decided to switch from the sparkling wine to the white wine. Of the two options, I selected the Spanish option. While not memorable, a couple of glasses helped with making the flight pass faster. 😉

After the mains were cleared, the crew came around with ice cream, and the choices were green tea, rum raisin, or vanilla.

At the conclusion of the meal I asked for a water, and that was served in a bottle. That’s probably smart, because actually keeping a glass of water anywhere without spilling it takes effort.

I sort of just raw-dogged the flight for an hour, and then a little before landing I ordered a coffee, which was served on a nice tray with some chocolates. I appreciate how they even have little milk pitchers.

Before landing I checked out the Philippine Airlines A330 business class lavatory, located at the very front of the cabin. This was a pretty typical A330 lavatory, and it did have a few Philippine Airlines branded products (hand wash, lotion, and “cologne”).

The highlight of this flight was definitely the crew — they were extremely friendly. The purser came around during boarding to personally greet each passenger, and the flight attendant working my aisle was kind as well. I was addressed by name the entire flight (“Mr. Benjamin”), as was virtually everyone in the cabin.

They also constantly checked on passengers. Admittedly the Philippines has a pretty hospitable culture, but this wasn’t just culture — this crew was much better than on my long haul flight, where it just felt like they were going through the motions with less genuine warmth.

Before I knew it, we were already approaching Manila. At 5:35PM Manila time, the captain was on the PA to say that we’d be landing in around 40 minutes, and a moment later we started our descent. The seatbelt sign was turned on at 5:50PM.

We had a pretty smooth descent, though it was rainy in Manila. We touched down at 6:05PM on runway 24, and then arrived at our gate at 6:10PM, 30 minutes ahead of schedule. While taxiing to the gate, the carrier’s landing song blasted throughout the cabin, which is… an acquired taste. I thought I was in a church service, or something. Seriously, give it a listen. I respect the dedication, at least!

Another thing I found funny is that we deplaned through the second set of doors. There are actually four rows of economy behind business class and in front of the second set of doors. Somehow the crew ensured that business class passengers deplaned first. I appreciate the effort, though I feel like that was more trouble than it was worth, since it required quite some gymnastics.

My Philippine Airlines A330 business class flight was a mixed bag. On the plus side, the crew was friendly and my Japanese meal was decent. On the other hand, the seats are extremely uncompetitive, there’s no Wi-Fi, and there’s no seat back entertainment. It’s rare that you’re in a flat bed seat and wish you just had a less spacious, standard recliner seat, but this was one of those situations.

So while this ultimately did the trick for a relatively short daytime flight, that won’t stop me from giving the airline a somewhat harsh rating for having what I consider to be the world’s most poorly designed flat bed business class seat.

What’s your take on Philippine Airlines’ A330 business class?

#5517. Huawei’s car unit joins CATL’s 2,520-station battery swap network

Huawei’s smart car business is signing on to CATL’s battery swap network, giving the world’s largest battery maker another heavyweight partner for a technology most of the industry wrote off a decade ago.

Yinwang, the Huawei-controlled automotive unit also known as Newcool, signed a strategic cooperation framework with CATL and its swap subsidiary CAES on Monday. CATL’s Choco-swap network already counts 2,520 stations across about 200 Chinese cities.

According to Yinwang’s statement, reported by Reuters, the deal covers both swappable battery packs and the station network. CATL says the focus is on vehicles running Huawei’s Qiankun smart driving systems, which would be made compatible with Choco stations.

The new partnership is betting heavily on automation. CATL wants to link the car, the station and the cloud so the vehicle works out when it needs energy, based on its battery status and the driver’s habits, then finds a station, drives in on its own and lines itself up for the swap. CATL quotes 99 seconds for the swap itself.

CATL chairman Robin Zeng and Jin Yuzhi, Huawei senior vice president and Yinwang CEO, attended the signing.

The new partnership looks serious, but the announcement left out a few key details. No vehicle models, launch date or service areas were announced. It’s a framework agreement, and those don’t always turn into products.

Who’s behind Yinwang? Huawei owns 80% of it, with Seres and Changan’s Avatr holding 10% each. Seres builds AITO, the premium SUV brand Huawei’s software and retail network helped turn into a hit in China, and plenty of other Chinese automakers buy Huawei’s Qiankun systems.

CATL first entered the battery swap market in 2022 with its EVOGO service, then relaunched the effort as Choco-swap around standardized packs at the end of 2024.

The growth since has been fast. Choco-swap ended 2025 with 1,020 stations, passed 2,000 at the start of July, and hit 2,520 as of September 29. As we reported this summer, CATL is building more than 200 battery swap stations every month. It’s aiming for more than 3,000 by the end of the year and 30,000 long term.

Huawei joins a growing list of Choco partners that includes GAC, BAIC and NIO. CATL is also pushing swapping for heavy trucks, including a joint venture with Octopus Energy for the UK and Europe.

For comparison, NIO, which built the first big swap network on its own, hit its 100 millionth swap in February with 3,790 stations. It passed 110 million swaps by June.

I’ve been skeptical of battery swapping for years, and I had good reasons to be.

Tesla demoed a 90-second pack swap in 2013, opened a single pilot station at Harris Ranch in California, and quietly let it fade after owners showed little interest. Better Place raised about $850 million and still went bankrupt in 2013. And every year, chargers got faster, which shrank the one real advantage swapping had.

My main argument was standardization. Swapping only works as infrastructure if lots of automakers share packs, and automakers don’t want to share the most expensive part of the car. NIO proved swapping can work for one brand. But one brand’s network is a perk, not infrastructure.

CATL is the one company that can fix that problem, and it’s actually doing it. It supplies roughly 40% of the world’s EV battery cells, so when it shows up with a standard pack, automakers listen. 2,520 stations in under two years isn’t a pilot.

I can see battery swapping and battery leases being big in the developing world. Possibly Europe. For the five eyes countries is probably a no. Getting people to buy a vehicle and lease a battery hasn't gone over well. The original smart evs were like that. I think the b class was too.

But if someone had a lot of cash to burn building stations though out the us. Then offered a dirt cheap ev with lifetime price battery lease then maybe. Especially as there is a pretty irrational fear of battery replacement. Leased battery you change all the time takes that fear out of the equation. The vehicle would have to be sub $15k possibly under $10k. And battery lease would have to be under $80 a month probably with a cheaper under $50 tier. Even then could be a while to gain traction. Could be part of the solution to urban changing is the best solution for packs sized for real actual use.

And Huawei brings autonomy. A car that drives itself into a station and leaves 99 seconds later doesn’t need anyone to plug it in. For robotaxis and commercial fleets, that solves a real problem.

I still don’t think swapping replaces charging. Most EV owners charge at home, and the fastest chargers in China are now getting close to gas station times. So it’s a complement. But I’m surprised to be writing this: in China, battery swapping is starting to look like a permanent part of the EV mix rather than a gimmick.

Will anyone build something like this outside of China?

If you’re driving an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero, no swap station needed. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. #5516. Edie Rodriguez Named Transcend Cruises CEO

Transcend Cruises has announced the appointment of Edie Rodriguez as chief executive officer and a member of the board of directors, replacing Gregg Marston.

She will lead Transcend Cruises’ strategic direction, brand development and continued growth, with a focus on delivering distinctive, immersive guest experiences while building a sustainable and profitable business, according to a company statement.

Rodriguez brings experience leading brands such as Crystal Cruises, including Crystal River Cruises and Crystal Yacht Expedition Cruises, Royal Caribbean’s Azamara Cruises, Carnival Corporation’s Cunard and Seabourn brands and Ponant Cruises.

“We are thrilled to welcome Edie Rodriguez to Transcend Cruises as our new CEO and a Member of the Board of Directors. Edie is a proven leader with a remarkable understanding of the luxury travel landscape, especially in cruising, with a strong track record of driving growth, and a passion for creating extraordinary guest experiences,” said Shawn Horowitz, chairman of the Board of Directors.

“Her strategic vision, entrepreneurial spirit, and ability to build strong teams make her the ideal person to lead Transcend into its next chapter. We are confident that, under her leadership, the company will continue to challenge convention and elevate Transcend’s offering in Europe.”

Rodriguez’s broader tourism industry background also includes positions as a special advisor to the Public Investment Fund of Saudi Arabia, a member of the board of directors of the Saudi Tourism Authority including chair of their Nominating and Remuneration Committee, and as an advisor to Blackstone, the mega cap Private Equity Firm.

“I’m delighted to be joining the Transcend team. The company is a true innovator and positive disruptor in the charter and MICE space for the European river cruise experiential market and perfectly positioned to attract global clients, with an exciting opportunity to further distinguish the brand,” noted Rodriguez.

“With the exceptional team at Transcend, together we will focus on growing the business successfully and profitably, while continuing to deliver amazing and memorable experiences for our clients and their guests, both onboard and ashore, as they discover some of Europe’s most enchanting destinations. I look forward to building on the company’s momentum as we lead the future of experiential river cruising for the charter and MICE global markets.”

The company also thanked Marston for his leadership and contributions during his tenure as CEO.

He will transition from the CEO role to continue serving the company as a Member of the Board of Directors, providing continuity and strategic perspective as Transcend advances its next phase of development.

The remainder of the Transcend Executive Leadership Team will remain in place working with Rodriguez, the cruise line added.

#5515. 'We're never going to come home': Artemis II commander recalls the most terrifying moment of his 10-day trip around the moon

Artemis II Commander Reid Wiseman felt genuine fear when he saw the Orion spacecraft had gone farther from Earth than anticipated, the astronaut told an audience at the Starmus VIII festival this week.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

TENERIFE, Spain — The commander of NASA's Artemis II mission briefly feared that he and his crew would never return home from their trip around the moon after overshooting their turn-back point.

Commander Reid Wiseman and his three crewmates were supposed to reach 219,667 nautical miles (252,788 standard miles, or 406,823 kilometers) — farther than any humans have ever traveled from their home planet — before turning back toward Earth on the sixth day of their 10-day mission to the moon.

But the odometer on the Integrity spacecraft continued to tick upward as they shot past their intended point of return, leading the astronaut to fear that something had gone terribly wrong, he told an audience here at the Starmus VIII festival earlier this week. (This semi-annual event is held to celebrate achievements in science, exploration, and creativity.)

"Mission control in Houston had told us our maximum distance would be 219,667 nautical miles and that was the furthest we were supposed to be from Earth," Wiseman said. "I was watching this number, and I saw it hit 219,667, and I thought, 'Great, this is the farthest we will be from Earth, and the next number I will see will be 219,666 ‪—‬ we'll be going home.' But about three minutes later, I didn't see 219,666; I saw 219,668. It went up. I was like. 'Huh? That's weird.'

Artemis II astronauts Jeremy Hansen, Victor Glover and Reid Wiseman were presented with the Stephen Hawking Medal for Science Communication at the Starmus VIII festival in Tenerife, Spain, on Oct. 17.

"And then about three minutes later, it goes to 219,669, and that was the moment that I felt real fear because I thought we had just gone too far ‪—‬ we're never going to come home ‪—‬ and so I was very scared," he added.

Then, at last, he saw the counter start to drop. Meanwhile, NASA astronauts Victor Glover and Christina Koch were at the window, doing science observations of the moon, and Canadian Space Agency astronaut Jeremy Hansen was sending reports back to mission control ‪—‬ so Wiseman's moment of terror went unnoticed.

When Wiseman saw the odometer click back to 219,668 nautical miles, he said, "We're going home," and the crew let out a cheer. "I knew there was a good chance we might make it," he added.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

In an interview with Live Science's sister publication The Week Junior Science+Nature, Wiseman paid tribute to NASA's Earth-based teams for the ultimate success of the mission.

"Getting home safely is a testament to the engineering rigor and the flight control teams back here on planet Earth, who put this rocket together and then who controlled it as we went out to the moon and came back. Those are the true heroes of this mission. They have real courage."

The Artemis II mission launched from NASA's Kennedy Space Center in Florida on April 1. It was the first crewed flight of the Orion space capsule and the first crewed mission to travel to the moon since Apollo 17 in 1972, taking nearly 10 days to fly around the lunar far side and return to Earth.

Nearly every moment of the crew's activities in the Orion capsule was livestreamed to the world, garnering massive public interest in the mission. The Artemis II crewmembers were awarded the Stephen Hawking Medal for Science Communication at the Starmus VIII festival.

Are you a NASA nerd? See how well you score on our Artemis quiz to find out!

Dan Green is the editor of The Week Junior Science+Nature.

#5514. Don’t Visit Ko Lipe

After 19 years, I finally went back to Ko Lipe, the Thai island I spent close to a month on in 2006. Back then, it was one of those super off-the-beaten-path destinations that few but the most intrepid travelers visited, where electricity only ran a few hours a day, basic bungalows right on the beach cost something like $2 USD, and there really was a last boat for the season.

There was much to do here but that was the point. You hung out on the beach, read a book, went snorkeling, went back to the beach, drank beers at the one beach bar on the island, rotated meals between the five restaurants there, and went to bed early.

It was paradise – and a place a lot of people got stuck. Days easily turned into weeks here.

If you asked me what the highlight of all my travels was, I would be the time I spent on Ko Lipe. I made incredible friends, lounged around, got to know the locals, learned a bit of Thai, and, overall, lived that idyllic backpacker life we all dream about.

Over the years, I’ve avoided going back to Ko Lipe because the memory of my time there is so strong that I didn’t want to ruin it. Any re-visit would simply be trying to recreated a magic that couldn’t be recreated because the people that it special wouldn’t be there. I’d be chasing travel ghosts. And, since I know my sleepy paradise has been developed greatly over the years, I was also too afraid seeing that would make me sad.

Tourism in Thailand tends toward the unsustainable. No island really develops in a good way. It’s all build, build, build.

And I didn’t want to see my Ko Lipe like that.

But as I was planning my recent trip through Southeast Asia, returning to Ko Lipe made sense. I was heading down the Indian Ocean side of Thailand on my way into Malaysia and I’d pass by it.

And, since I was looking for a lively place for New Year’s Eve, it seemed liked the best choice. I knew there would be travelers there and there were no other nearby islands that would work, especially since Ko Lipe has a boat to Langkawi, which was my next stop.

And I’m sad to report that Ko Lipe took the Ko Phi Phi model of tourism and is now extremely overdeveloped.

Most of the island is now paved over, the old dirt footpaths having become concrete for the cars and construction trucks. Swaths of palm trees are now the sites of high-end resorts with pools (on an island with no natural water supply). Construction of more resorts continues at a fast pace. The coral around the island is dying, a victim of all the boats, anchors, pollution, and overfishing. Beaches are now lined with boats, their exhaust spilling into the ocean, leaving a shiny film you can see as you swim. And the restaurants cater to tourists looking for bad Western food, not great Thai cuisine.

The island’s boom has displaced many locals, who were forced to sell to mainland developers, and much of the island’s workforce is now from the mainland. They see little of the benefits this tourism boom.

So lies Ko Lipe, another victim of Thailand’s all too common overdevelopment and exploitation of limited resources.

I met lots of people there who loved the island. If it’s your first time, I can see why you would love it. After all, the area is postcard perfect, the water is perfectly an azure blue, the sand is a beautiful white, and since you’re surrounded by a national park, a lot of tours take you to some secluded islands.

And, in comparison to Ko Phi Phi, Krabi, or Phuket, it is less developed so I can’t fault someone stepping here for the first time going “wow!”

But, as I reflect on the island and its overdevelopment, I have come to the same conclusion I have about Ko Phi Phi: people shouldn’t visit.

I’m not against growth, but I’m against this kind of growth. It’s not sustainably managed and going there will only tax the island’s limited resources even further. You can’t put the genie back in the bottle and no local is going to say “sure, I’ll stay broke so you can an idealized vision of the world.”

And, with so many other islands to visit that are well managed (Ko Lanta, Ko Jum, and Ko Mook, to name three nearby), I think you should skip Ko Lipe.

A visit there will only make things worse.

It pains me to say that, because it was such a beautiful place, and my original visit had a huge impact on my life. But if we’re going to be good stewards and travelers, sometimes you just have to say enough is enough.

And Ko Lipe is a place where enough is enough.

Go somewhere else that is better managed.

Riding elephants in Thailand went away when consumers became more conscious of it. Eco lodges got big because of consumers. Overtourism is talked about by consumers as much as it is by locals.

Maybe if enough people start to do something, Ko Lipe will change.

But, at the very least, by not going you are at least no contributing to the problem.

#5513. The Conciliator

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Interview: The Conciliator

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

On the night of Sept. 30, the last day that the U.S. Senate was in session before breaking for fall recess, Sen. Jeanne Shaheen strode onto the chamber’s floor and made an unusual request.

The 79-year-old Democratic lawmaker sought to force a vote to confirm 10 new ambassadors. The nominees weren’t the big campaign donors who typically get glitzy posts in Paris and Rome. They were career diplomats, tapped to represent Washington in places such as Benin, Sierra Leone, Malaysia, and Kyrgyzstan, whose nominations are frequently held up for political horse-trading. And sure enough, despite having had successful confirmation hearings, their nominations had stalled amid partisan fighting.

On the night of Sept. 30, the last day that the U.S. Senate was in session before breaking for fall recess, Sen. Jeanne Shaheen strode onto the chamber’s floor and made an unusual request.

The 79-year-old Democratic lawmaker sought to force a vote to confirm 10 new ambassadors. The nominees weren’t the big campaign donors who typically get glitzy posts in Paris and Rome. They were career diplomats, tapped to represent Washington in places such as Benin, Sierra Leone, Malaysia, and Kyrgyzstan, whose nominations are frequently held up for political horse-trading. And sure enough, despite having had successful confirmation hearings, their nominations had stalled amid partisan fighting.

Fed up with the lack of progress, Shaheen—the top Democrat on the Foreign Relations Committee—made her move. She played the China card. Shaheen gave a speech highlighting the dearth of confirmed U.S. ambassadors while Beijing makes diplomatic inroads around the globe.

As she spoke, she gestured to two world maps juxtaposing where the United States and China have ambassadors. While the map of China’s top envoys appeared to be almost filled out, the U.S. map showcased gaping white spaces across most of Africa, with many other unfilled positions in Latin America, Asia, and Europe. Empty posts in Russia and Brazil loomed particularly large.

U.S. Sen. Jeanne Shaheen points to a map of filled U.S. ambassadorships as Secretary of State Marco Rubio testifies during a Senate Foreign Relations Committee hearing on Capitol Hill in Washington on Jan. 28. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images

Shaheen finished her remarks and requested the confirmation of the nominees. None of the other 99 senators objected—an almost unheard-of result in today’s polarized Senate.

“I think this is very uniquely Jeanne Shaheen,” said Naz Durakoglu, the Democratic staff director on the Senate Foreign Relations Committee and a longtime staffer for Shaheen, recalling how other Senate staff for both Democrats and Republicans laughed when they realized what the senator was doing and that she was going to get away with it. “Because she was the one doing it, no one objected.”

Shaheen, who is retiring at the end of the year, has spent nearly two decades on Capitol Hill working in a bipartisan manner to strike modest compromises. That hasn’t always made her popular with the Democratic base, which has been hunting for its own flamethrowers to meet the challenge of U.S. President Donald Trump’s second term.

Flamethrower she is not. Rather, Shaheen operates as a conciliator, doing the often-unglamorous work of trying to build consensus. No matter how outraged she may be over Trump’s latest norm-defying move, she always speaks in measured, even tones.

“You can’t just say ‘I’m going to give up.’ You’ve got to keep at it. I believe in persistence,” the senator said during a June interview in her office in the Senate’s Hart Office Building.

In her two final years in office, Shaheen has had to watch some of her signature foreign-policy achievements—such as securing initiatives to advance women’s rights, which she spent years painstakingly building bipartisan support for—come undone. Will her legacy be defined by Trumpism, or does her story still have a twist in the tale?

Rubio stands between Shaheen and Republican Sen. Jim Risch during Rubio’s Senate Foreign Relations Committee confirmation hearing on Capitol Hill in Washington on Jan. 15, 2025.Jabin Botsford/The Washington Post via Getty Images

Prominently featured in Shaheen’s office is a large wood-and-glass display case full of the challenge coins that the senator has received throughout her long public service career, including from the New Hampshire Air National Guard, the U.S. Navy, and various U.S. embassies around the globe. On the walls is a series of framed woodblock prints by New Hampshire artist Matt Brown, who loaned them to the office.

New Hampshire is not just the state that Shaheen represents—it’s a place whose politics deeply shaped her.

She was the state’s governor for six years, from 1997 to 2003. Because of the moderate nature of New Hampshire’s electorate, which lately trends Democratic but still elects Republicans for statewide office, Shaheen believes that she has bipartisan policymaking in her DNA.

“The whole time I was governor, I had a Republican legislature,” she said. “My career in politics has been about trying to work in a bipartisan manner.”

New Hampshire is unique in having an elected Executive Council, also known as a governor’s council. “It’s a carryover from colonial days, and it approves all nominees and all contracts. And the governor and council meet in public session twice a month,” Shaheen said, adding that throughout her tenure as governor, Republicans held nearly all the seats on the council. “So, in order to get anything done, we had to figure out how to work together.”

The senator’s legislating philosophy is similar to her governing philosophy and can generally be summed up by the old aphorism: “Don’t let the perfect be the enemy of good.”

That ethos has guided many of Shaheen’s conversations with fellow Democrats during the second Trump administration. It has also enabled multiple small but meaningful policy wins, such as in summer 2025, when she voted to confirm Mike Waltz as the U.S. ambassador to the United Nations in exchange for the White House releasing $75 million in withheld foreign aid to humanitarian programs in Haiti and Nigeria.

Shaheen also teamed up with Republican Sen. Jim Risch on legislation targeting Russia’s shadow fleet—the aging, reflagged oil tankers that Moscow uses to evade energy sanctions.

Those penalties eventually made it into the massive new Russia sanctions law that Trump signed in September. Shaheen wasn’t the initial sponsor of the broader law—that was the late Republican Sen. Lindsey Graham—but she played a no less pivotal role in prodding Democrats, who were leery over giving Trump additional powers to impose sweeping tariffs, into holding their noses and backing the legislation to support Ukraine.

“In my mind, it’s a trade-off, and I think the most critical issue right now is putting pressure on [Russian President Vladimir] Putin and trying to do everything we can to end the war in Ukraine,” Shaheen said. “Is it a perfect bill? No. Legislation is never perfect. But would it help us to really put pressure on Putin? The answer is yes.”

With Graham’s unexpected passing, Shaheen stepped forward to shoulder even more of the work of muscling the Russia sanctions legislation through the House and the Senate. As it happened, “the tremendous shock of Graham’s death really crystallized in people’s minds how important it was to get this done,” said Amy English, the deputy staff director for Democrats on Senate Foreign Relations. “This was literally Graham’s final wish.”

Even so, it took Shaheen and her strong working relationships with Republicans such as Sen. Roger Wicker, the chairman of the Senate Armed Services Committee, and Sen. Katie Britt, to hammer out sufficient agreements on amendments with Republicans and Democrats to clear a procedural path to passage.

“It really does take someone that is absolutely dogged in seizing the moment to push something like this through,” English said, observing that Shaheen cleared much of her schedule last month when the House took up and subsequently passed the Russia sanctions bill.

Short-term waivers are a start, but Congress must act on the full repeal Syria needs to rebuild.

Cutting off independent media abroad leaves the United States vulnerable at home.

A senior member of the U.S. Senate Foreign Relations Committee on Nord Stream 2, Belarus, and Biden’s upcoming summit with Putin.

Advancing global women’s rights has been a passion for Shaheen since her earliest years in the Senate, which she joined in 2009.

When I visited Shaheen’s office in June, I was eight months pregnant and more out of breath than I cared to admit from the short walk from the Uber to her office. Shaheen and her staff greeted me warmly, asking how I was managing in the intense summer heat. If they noticed that, beneath my long dress, one of my swollen ankles had burst the strap of my sandal, they were too gracious to comment.

I later learned from one of her aides that all the staff on the leadership teams in Shaheen’s personal office and on the Democratic side of the Foreign Relations Committee are working moms. “It just tells you about her values,” Durakoglu said.

In the Senate, Shaheen has pushed for the establishment of an ambassador-at-large for global women’s issues and painstakingly secured passage during the first Trump administration of her Women, Peace, and Security Act (WPS), which requires the U.S. government to develop strategies for increasing the role of women in conflict prevention and peace processes around the world.

When Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced in April 2025 that he was ending the Defense Department’s implementation of the WPS law, Shaheen released a statement that accused the defense chief of having “absolutely no idea what he’s doing.”

More than a year later, she is still clearly upset with Hegseth’s decision. But she uses the language of a diplomat to express that dismay.

“It’s frustrating, and I think it’s bad policy,” Shaheen said, citing data she has showing that societies that empower women are more stable and tend to be more democratic. “I think Hegseth and the policies of this administration are…”—here she breaks for a prolonged pause to find the right word—“…not only shortsighted; they’re wrong.”

U.S. Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick greets Shaheen before testifying during a budget hearing in Washington on April 22. Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call via Getty Images

For someone just months from retirement, Shaheen doesn’t seem to be slowing down.

After Trump announced on Oct. 9 that he was unilaterally lifting U.S. sanctions on Russian diesel, Shaheen worked quickly with Democrats and Republicans to release a bipartisan statement condemning the move.

“We’re doing so much more than when she wasn’t retiring,” Durakoglu told me. “Part of the reason for that is because, deep down, she probably is worried about what this place is going to look like after her and some of her colleagues with similar mindsets leave. But she wouldn’t be trying this hard and putting herself out there in this way if she didn’t think there was a good chance that her way would succeed.”

The senator’s ability to work with Republicans in specific policy areas such as shoring up support for Ukraine notwithstanding, it’s clear that Shaheen is disappointed with how her GOP colleagues have accepted many of Trump’s foreign-policy moves that they would have surely opposed had they been undertaken by a Democratic president.

Republicans’ failure to conduct oversight of Trump’s nearly eight-month war with Iran, which he launched with little forewarning and no congressional authorization, weighs heavily on Shaheen’s mind. I asked if she thinks that the experience of the Iran war might finally prompt Republicans to reassert themselves to rein in a wayward commander in chief.

She rattled off a long list of what she sees as the war’s strategic failures, from Iran’s increased control of the Strait of Hormuz to having a more hard-line regime in Tehran than there was before the war. “I have not heard one Republican acknowledge that, even behind closed doors,” she said, before adding, “which is shocking.”

I also asked if she sensed in Republicans any greater willingness to defend their constitutional power of the purse.

“They’re really not very willing to engage even in the conversation. It’s more about, ‘Well, that’s Donald Trump. I can’t do anything about him.’ That’s sort of the attitude,” Shaheen said.

In September, the White House canceled almost $1 billion in congressionally directed spending toward programs that mostly benefit refugees and unaccompanied minors crossing into the United States.

“So many of the Republicans who were willing to take a stand against Trump are gone, either voluntarily or like [Sen. Bill] Cassidy, because Trump is opposed,” Shaheen said. Cassidy, who voted to convict Trump in the Senate’s impeachment trial following the Jan. 6, 2021, insurrection, lost in a party primary earlier this year to a hard-line MAGA challenger backed by the president.

Shaheen speaks at the Eleanor Roosevelt Dinner in Concord, New Hampshire, on Oct. 3. Joseph Prezioso/AFP via Getty Images

“My argument is: ‘So you lose your election? What’s more important? Your principles, or staying in office?’ And that argument just doesn’t carry much weight with most people,” Shaheen said.

Polls have been painting an increasingly grim picture for Republicans’ prospects in next month’s midterm elections. If Democrats wind up winning the House and potentially even the Senate, Trump’s war against Iran will have played a significant role in costing Republicans much of their power on Capitol Hill.

Shaheen is hopeful that the November vote could represent a turning point. “It’ll be interesting to see in the upcoming elections if there are any of those states that have historically been Republican but don’t vote Republican this time,” she said. “I think that’s what it’s going to take in order to get people to think about doing more to work together.”

This post is part of FP’s ongoing coverage of the Trump administration. Follow along here.

Rachel Oswald is a staff writer at Foreign Policy. X: @OswaldRachel

#5512. I’ve Spent Years Testing Benchmade Pocket Knives. Here Are the 7 Best, Ranked

These American-made blades aren’t cheap, but they’re worth their price tag for quality and performance.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Benchmade is one of the great American knife brands, with an expansive collection of blades made entirely in its Oregon, U.S., headquarters. Founded in 1987, the company has a long history of pushing the boundaries of production and design.

Today, Benchmade is best known for its modern EDC folders kitted out with the brand’s Axis lock, first put into production in 1999. This once-patented ambidextrous folding mechanism is now employed by nearly every major knife competitor worldwide.

It was the release of the Osborne 940 in 2000 that established Benchmade as a brand worth paying attention to. And in 2017, the debut of the Bugout 535 cemented its distinguished reputation. Both of these pocket knives proved that ultralight materials could still be shockingly strong and durable. In time, Benchmade’s folders, fixed blades, and automatics gained devotees among mountaineers, backpackers, and hunters.

The Bugout 535 was my first Benchmade knife, which I added to my collection upon its release, and it’s become my go-to blade. Over the years, there have been others I’ve tested, and I certainly have my favorites, which I’ve ranked in order below.

I was late to the Benchmade party, as my first of its knives was the Bugout 535 released in 2017. At the time, its 2-ounce weight and stiff, secure lockout was a confusing novelty for people like me who equated “heavy” with “strong” and “durable.” It took me years to fully trust the Bugout because of how light the Grivory handle scales, aluminum liner, and thin-yet-tough CPM-S30V steel make it. Now it’s my go-to for backpacking and backcountry hunts in addition to everyday carry.

Despite a relatively high price tag, the Bugout has sold more than a million knives in less than a decade of production and is arguably the defining pocket knife of the explosion of modern EDC blades. I’ve yet to chip the edge or break the tip despite being a known knife abuser. The slim and light build is barely noticeable in your pocket or pack. Sure, the materials and specs can be found elsewhere today, but the Bugout still sits in my hunting pants and Benchmade continues to push the design with knives like the Bugout Vapyr featured below.

Benchmade makes a whole range of hunting blades, from the new wood-handled Slipridge folder to the Taggedout, which is basically a beefed-up Bugout built for hunters. I always keep a folder in my pocket while hunting, but also have a fixed blade or three in my pack along with a pocket sharpener in case there’s field dressing to be done. The Benchmade Raghorn is a midsize modern hunting fixed blade that rarely leaves my pack and rarely needs sharpening.

Despite being almost nine inches long, the Raghorn weighs just 3.5 ounces thanks to a relatively thin blade and lightweight G10 scales. (My older Raghorn has carbon fiber scales and a blaze orange blade coating but isn’t in production anymore, sadly.)

As with many Benchmade blades, high-end S30V stainless steel is used for durability, edge retention, and toughness. I’ve butchered dozens of animals with mine and it hardly shows.

The Bugout Vapyr released in 2026 stands as evidence that Benchmade isn’t content to rest on its laurels. Nine years after the original Bugout debuted, Benchmade designers revisited the iconic ultralight EDC and found a way to make it even lighter and thinner. And it’s arguably more robust, employing thin aluminum scales instead of lightweight synthetics for an all-metal build.

Considering the featherlight 1.72-ounce weight, it’s an impressive feat of engineering and production. The Vapyr uses an upgraded version of the Axis lock with a slide that’s ridged and easier to grab, but the action isn’t quite as free-swinging as the original Axis.

The Infidel might be Benchmade’s most influential automatic and the Shootout its most popular, but the pricey Auto Fact is my personal favorite. The Italian stiletto-styled side-opening automatic is one of the most distinctive automatics I’ve seen. It’s not as fast as most out-the-front (OTF) switchblades, but the spring-loaded Axis lock deployment is smooth, not jarring like many more powerful side-opening automatics.

The long, narrow blade has a dagger look, but is single-edged, making it safer and more practical for everyday tasks. Still, this is more of a novelty than a workhorse and one of the few knives I keep in rotation for EDC despite its limitations. The action is awesome, but it needs to be kept clean and well-oiled or the action can slow down or fail entirely if you get it too gummed up.

The Osborne 940 isn’t my all-time favorite Benchmade, but it’s arguably the most important Benchmade knife. The Osborne debuted in 2000 and it paired Warren Osborne’s ranch-ready design with the then-new Axis lock. The blend was a hit with a wide range of knife enthusiasts and working people, thanks to a capable build and the intrigue of the Axis.

Since 2000, there have been dozens of Osborne variations and limited releases. The original 940 used 154CM steel and aluminum scales, but today you find countless versions of the original plus a Mini Osborne, Auto Osborne, and customizable Osbornes.

The design has stood the test of time and there are plenty of similar blades from other brands, especially since the Axis lock patent expired. If there’s a downside to buying an Osborne, it’s that the price has climbed to $300 since 2000 and many of those capable imitators (the Kershaw Iridium, as one example) cost significantly less.

I love flipper actions on pocket knives. They rival the speed of an automatic, snapping open fast and locking out securely as a result, but most rely on liner or frame locks, which aren’t as easy to open one-handed, making them less practical everyday tools. Benchmade doesn’t make a ton of flippers, but the Lowden is one of the relatively few Axis lock flippers combining one-handed convenience with the fidget-friendly speed of a flipper.

The Lowden sits deep in the pocket and is easy and fast to deploy. I still prefer the Bugout overall, but the slightly heavier Lowden has an all-metal build and a thicker blade, which makes it feel more robust for everyday abuse. And if you generally prefer a flipper like I do, but also want the ease and security of the Axis, this is one of a handful of knives with that particular combination.

The Benchmade Triage has the distinction of being the rescue knife carried by the crew of the Artemis II for their recent orbit around the moon. Even if you’re not preparing for a space mission, the Triage is a simple folding rescue knife for emergency preparedness and first responders.

The blunt-tip blade helps avoid accidental punctures in high-stress situations and the fold-out belt cutter adds functionality without the complexity and bulk of a multitool. The N680 steel prioritizes corrosion resistance so it’s a knife you can stash in an emergency kit or bugout bag without worrying about it holding up.

Rachel is the Deputy Editor of Reviews for Hearst's Enthusiast and Wellness Group where she supports Popular Mechanics, Runner's World, and Best Products. Her background is in service journalism and earlier in her career she worked as a daily newspaper reporter and book editor. She spent more than a decade as a staff editor and writer at TripAdvisor and Penguin Random House and her byline has appeared in Time Out New York, the New York Daily News, and New York Magazine. Outside of work, Rachel can be found planning her next trip, visiting museums and galleries, and walking her wirehaired dachshund in Central Park.
