#5511. 25 Best Travel Apps for 2026: The Apps We Actually Use

We reviewed every recommendation and added new apps for AI trip planning, eSIMs, flight tracking, budgeting, and travel safety.

After travelling to more than 130 countries, we have used travel apps for everything from finding a last-minute room and navigating a city offline to translating menus, tracking flights and staying connected without paying ridiculous roaming charges. Some apps have earned a permanent place on our phones. Others have become outdated, overpriced or unnecessary.

For this 2026 guide, we cut through the clutter and chose the travel apps that solve real problems before and during a trip. You do not need to download every app on this list. Choose the ones that match your destination, travel style and the problems you are most likely to face.

The best travel apps for 2026 are Google Maps for navigation, Google Translate for language help, WhatsApp for communication, TripIt for organizing reservations, Google Flights for comparing airfare, Booking.com for hotels, Airalo or another destination-appropriate eSIM for mobile data, Uber for rides and AllTrails for hiking. Most travellers need only one or two apps from each category.

The Best Travel Apps for 2026 at a Glance

Our picks are based on usefulness, ease of use, geographic coverage and whether the app solves a genuine travel problem. We have also included alternatives where the best choice depends on your destination.

Before getting into specialized apps, these are the five we would start with. They cover navigation, translation, communication, flight updates and trip organization.

Google Maps is still the app we use most often while travelling. It handles driving, walking and public-transit directions, lets us save hotels and restaurants, and shows opening hours, recent reviews and nearby services.

Before leaving reliable Wi-Fi, download the map for your destination. Offline maps are especially useful when you land without mobile data or drive through an area with poor reception. Keep in mind that live traffic, some transit information and certain search features may not work offline.

What we like: It combines navigation, trip planning and local research in one place.

What to know: Directions, opening hours and business listings are not always accurate. Double-check important information directly with the business.

Our verdict: Download it before almost any trip.

Google Translate has helped us order food, read signs and have simple conversations in places where we did not speak the language. You can type a phrase, speak into the phone or point the camera at text for an instant translation.

Download the languages you expect to use before your trip. That gives you a useful backup when you do not have data, although online translations can be more complete.

What we like: The camera function is incredibly useful for menus, ticket machines and signs.

What to know: Translations can be awkward or wrong, especially with slang, regional phrases and complicated instructions.

Our verdict: It is one of the most useful free international travel apps.

WhatsApp is the default communication tool in many parts of the world. Hotels, tour companies, drivers and local contacts often use it instead of standard text messages.

It supports individual and group chats, voice calls, video calls, photos, documents and live-location sharing over an internet connection. That makes it useful for keeping a travel group together or confirming a pickup without paying international texting charges.

What we like: It is widely used and keeps trip communication in one place.

What to know: It needs mobile data or Wi-Fi. Check the phone number carefully before sharing personal information or sending money.

Our verdict: Install it before leaving home and verify your number while you can still receive regular text messages.

This may not be the most exciting recommendation, but it is one of the most practical. Download your airline’s official app before departure and sign in to your booking.

Airline apps can provide mobile check-in, boarding passes, gate notifications, delay information and rebooking options. During a disruption, the app may update before airport screens or email notifications do.

What we like: It gives you a direct line to the airline’s current information about your flight.

What to know: Push notifications are useful, but do not rely on them alone. Keep checking the departure board and listen for airport announcements.

Our verdict: Download it for every flight, even if you delete it after the trip.

TripIt turns confirmation emails into one organized itinerary. Forward your flight, hotel, rental-car and activity confirmations, and the app puts the details in chronological order.

This is particularly useful on complicated trips with several flights, hotel changes or train journeys. Instead of searching through your inbox at a check-in desk, you can pull up the reservation in one place.

What we like: It reduces the chaos of juggling confirmation emails.

What to know: Review imported details because automatic parsing is not infallible. Real-time alerts are generally part of the paid plan.

Our verdict: Best for multi-stop trips, business travel and anyone who likes everything organized.

AI can be helpful for brainstorming routes, comparing neighbourhoods, creating packing lists and turning scattered ideas into a first-draft itinerary. It should not be treated as the final authority on visa rules, border requirements, prices, opening hours, transit schedules or safety alerts. Verify anything that could cost you money or prevent you from entering a country.

ChatGPT is most useful when you give it real constraints: your travel dates, budget, interests, pace, mobility needs and where you are staying. It can then help turn those details into a practical first draft rather than a generic list of attractions.

We also use AI to compare neighbourhoods, create checklists and identify questions we still need to research. The trick is to treat the result as a planning assistant, not a booking engine or government authority.

What we like: It is flexible and can quickly reorganize a complicated trip.

What to know: AI can produce outdated or invented information. Always confirm bookings, travel rules, prices and operating hours through official sources.

Our verdict: Excellent for planning and editing; unreliable as the sole source of truth.

Wanderlog combines itinerary planning, maps, reservations, notes and collaboration. It is useful for road trips and group travel because several people can add places and adjust the plan.

The visual map helps reveal when an itinerary makes no geographic sense. That sounds obvious, but it is remarkably easy to plan breakfast on one side of a city, lunch on the other and dinner back where you started.

What we like: The shared planning and map view make group trips easier.

What to know: A detailed itinerary can become cluttered. Keep some flexibility rather than scheduling every hour.

Our verdict: A strong choice for collaborative planning and road trips.

Finding a cheap flight is only part of the job. These apps help us compare dates, monitor prices and track disruptions once the trip begins.

Google Flights is not a conventional downloadable app, but it remains one of the fastest ways to compare airfare. The calendar and price graph make it easy to see whether leaving a day earlier or using a nearby airport could reduce the fare.

Set a price alert when you are not ready to book. Google Flights sends updates when fares change, but you will usually complete the purchase with the airline or another booking provider.

What we like: It is fast, flexible and excellent for comparing dates.

What to know: It does not show every airline or every possible fare. Compare the final price, baggage allowance and change rules before booking.

Our verdict: Our first stop for airfare research. Before pressing confirm, read our guide to airline booking traps that can make a cheap flight cost more

Skyscanner is useful when you know you want to travel but have not settled on a destination. Its flexible search tools can surface options by month, country or broad destination.

It also compares hotels and rental cars, although we mainly use it for flight discovery. Once you find a fare, pay close attention to whether the booking is through the airline or an online travel agency.

What we like: Flexible searches are ideal for travellers who can follow the deal.

What to know: The cheapest result is not automatically the best one. Customer service and change policies vary by booking provider.

Our verdict: Best for inspiration and flexible travel dates.

Available on: iOS and supported Apple devices

Flighty turns a flight number into a detailed timeline with schedule changes, aircraft information and disruption updates. Frequent flyers appreciate how quickly it presents complicated flight information.

It is most useful on travel days, especially when weather or airport congestion starts knocking schedules sideways. The free version is enough for occasional travellers, while frequent flyers may value the paid alerts.

What we like: It presents flight changes clearly and can reduce airport uncertainty.

What to know: Some of its best features require a subscription, and platform availability may not suit every traveller.

Our verdict: A specialist app worth considering if you fly often.

Finding the right place to stay is about more than price. We look at location, recent reviews, cancellation policies, hidden fees, and whether the property actually suits the way we travel. These accommodation apps make it easier to compare hotels, apartments, hostels, and vacation rentals without bouncing between a dozen websites.

Booking.com remains one of the easiest places to compare a wide range of accommodation, from simple guesthouses to luxury hotels. The filters are especially useful when we need parking, a specific neighbourhood, breakfast, air conditioning or a refundable rate.

Read recent reviews rather than relying only on the overall score. We pay particular attention to comments about noise, cleanliness, construction and whether the room matches the photos.

What we like: Huge inventory and practical search filters.

What to know: Cancellation and payment rules vary by property. Confirm whether taxes and resort fees are included before booking.

Our verdict: Our top general-purpose hotel app.

Airbnb is most useful when we want a kitchen, laundry, a living area or enough bedrooms for a group. It can also provide options in neighbourhoods with few conventional hotels.

Compare the total price rather than the nightly rate. Cleaning charges, service fees and local taxes can change the value quickly. Read the house rules and cancellation policy before paying, and check whether the host has a substantial history of recent reviews.

What we like: It can provide more space and practical amenities than a hotel.

What to know: Quality is inconsistent, and fees can make a seemingly cheap stay expensive.

Our verdict: Best for longer stays and groups, not automatically the cheapest option.

Best for: Hostels, dorms, private rooms and social accommodation

Price: Free to search; booking deposit or fees may apply

Hostelworld makes it easy to compare hostels by location, room type, atmosphere and recent guest reviews. It is particularly useful for solo travellers who care about communal spaces and meeting other people.

Do not assume every hostel is a party hostel—or that every quiet-looking property will actually be quiet. Read recent reviews for comments about security, lockers, bathrooms, noise and the surrounding area.

What we like: It provides hostel-specific details that general hotel sites often miss.

What to know: A low bed price can rise after taxes, linen fees or deposits. Check whether lockers require your own padlock.

Our verdict: The most useful specialist app for hostel travellers.

There is no single eSIM provider that is cheapest or fastest in every country. Compare the local network, amount of high-speed data, hotspot rules, validity period and whether the plan is data-only before buying.

Airalo makes it straightforward to buy and install an eSIM before a trip. You choose the destination, select a data package and follow the installation instructions on a compatible phone.

We like that it removes the need to find a physical SIM card immediately after landing. Regional plans can also be convenient on multi-country trips, although a country-specific plan may offer better value.

What we like: Easy setup and broad destination coverage.

What to know: Most plans are data-only, and the local network or speed can vary. Check phone compatibility before purchasing.

Our verdict: A convenient starting point for travellers new to eSIMs.

Ubigi is worth comparing with Airalo because eSIM value changes by country. In some destinations, one provider may offer a better local carrier, more data or a longer validity period.

Install the app and eSIM before departure whenever possible. Take screenshots of the setup instructions in case your connection disappears halfway through activation.

What we like: It gives travellers another established option instead of assuming one provider always wins.

What to know: Coverage and value depend on the specific plan. Read the plan details rather than buying based only on the headline price.

Our verdict: Compare it destination by destination.

Getting around an unfamiliar city can be one of the most confusing parts of travel. These apps help us compare public transit, plan longer connections, and arrange rides when trains or buses are not practical.

Citymapper can be easier to understand than a standard map when a city has several transit systems layered on top of one another. It compares routes, shows transfers and often gives useful instructions about which station exit to use.

We find it particularly useful in large cities where choosing the wrong train direction or exit can add a surprising amount of time.

What we like: Clear transit directions and route comparisons.

What to know: Coverage is limited to supported cities, and live information depends on local transit data.

Our verdict: Excellent in cities where it is available.

Rome2Rio is useful during the early planning stage when you know where you want to go but not how to get there. Enter two places and it outlines possible combinations of flights, trains, buses, ferries and driving routes.

We use it as a starting point rather than the final booking authority. Once we understand the route, we verify schedules and buy tickets directly from the transport operator whenever practical.

What we like: It makes complicated overland connections easier to visualize.

What to know: Estimated times and prices can change. Confirm every important connection with the operator.

Our verdict: One of the best tools for answering, ‘How do we actually get there?’

Uber can remove the uncertainty of explaining a destination, handling cash and negotiating a fare in an unfamiliar city. The app shows the route, vehicle and driver details before the ride begins.

Availability and regulations vary widely. In some destinations, another local app is more common, and airports may require pickup from a designated ride-hailing area.

What we like: Upfront trip details and cashless payment are convenient.

What to know: Surge pricing can be expensive. Always confirm the licence plate and driver before getting into a vehicle.

Our verdict: Useful where it operates, but check the dominant local alternative.

Managing money abroad can get complicated quickly. Exchange rates, foreign transaction fees, ATM charges, and shared expenses all add up, especially on longer trips or when travelling with other people. These apps help us understand what things really cost, manage different currencies, and keep track of who paid for what without turning dinner into an accounting meeting.

Wise lets eligible customers hold and exchange several currencies and spend with a linked card. It can be useful for frequent travellers, remote workers and anyone receiving money in more than one currency.

Always compare the total conversion cost with your regular bank or credit card. The best option depends on your country, card benefits and the type of transaction.

What we like: Transparent conversion information and multi-currency tools.

What to know: Availability, card features and fees vary by country. It is a financial service, so protect the account with strong security.

Our verdict: A useful tool for frequent international travellers.

XE is the app we open when a price full of unfamiliar zeros makes our brains quietly leave the room. It converts currencies quickly and helps you understand what a meal, taxi or hotel actually costs in your home currency.

Remember that the rate shown in a converter is not necessarily the rate your bank, ATM or exchange desk will give you after fees.

What to know: It shows a reference rate, not a guaranteed retail exchange rate.

Our verdict: Keep it on your phone for quick price checks.

Splitwise prevents the end-of-trip ritual where everyone stares at a pile of receipts and suddenly forgets who paid for the rental car. Add shared expenses as you go, assign who owes what and let the app calculate the running balance.

It is particularly helpful for groups using different payment methods or taking turns covering major expenses.

What we like: It reduces awkward money conversations and messy arithmetic.

What to know: It records debts but does not automatically settle every balance. Agree on currencies and exchange rates at the beginning of the trip.

Booking tours and activities in advance can save time, especially when popular attractions have timed entry or limited availability. We use these apps to compare tours, skip-the-line tickets, day trips, and local experiences, but we always check the operator, recent reviews, cancellation policy, and exactly what is included before booking.

GetYourGuide is useful when timed-entry tickets sell out or when a guided visit adds real context. It covers major attractions, walking tours, food tours, day trips and airport transfers in many popular destinations.

Read the cancellation policy and identify the actual tour operator before booking. A marketplace can list several versions of what looks like the same experience.

What we like: Strong inventory in major tourism destinations.

What to know: Quality depends on the local operator, not only the booking platform.

Our verdict: A good place to compare organized experiences.

Viator has a broad inventory of tours and activities around the world. We use it to see what is available, compare departure times and identify experiences that may be difficult to organize independently.

As with any marketplace, read the newest reviews and check exactly what is included. A low starting price may refer to a basic option without transport, admission or the advertised upgrade.

What we like: It covers an enormous range of destinations and activities.

What to know: Listings from different operators can look very similar. Check inclusions, pickup details and cancellation terms.

Our verdict: Best for comparing a wide range of tours.

Finding the right trail or campsite takes more than choosing the prettiest photo. We look at distance, difficulty, recent trail conditions, offline map access, weather, and whether the route matches our experience level. These hiking, camping, and outdoor apps help us plan more confidently, but they should always be used alongside proper preparation and local safety information.

AllTrails is an easy starting point for finding hikes by location, distance, difficulty and user rating. Recent reviews can reveal muddy sections, closures, parking problems and trail conditions that a static guidebook cannot.

Download the route before leaving reception and carry a backup. A phone app is useful, but it does not replace proper preparation, weather awareness or a dedicated navigation tool in remote terrain.

What we like: It makes trail discovery accessible and provides recent user feedback.

What to know: Crowdsourced routes can be inaccurate. Do not rely on a single phone app for serious backcountry navigation.

Our verdict: Best for day hikes and casual outdoor planning.

Gaia GPS is aimed at travellers who need more detail than a simple trail list. It supports topographic maps, route planning and map layers useful for hiking, camping and remote travel.

Learn how to use it before heading into the backcountry. Download the required maps, carry power and tell someone your route. Technology is helpful right up until the battery dies.

What we like: Detailed mapping and route-planning tools.

What to know: It has a learning curve, and important map features may require a subscription.

Our verdict: Better suited to experienced outdoor travellers than casual city walkers.

The following apps can be excellent, but they are more specialized or depend heavily on your destination:

Hidden-city ticketing involves booking a connecting itinerary and intentionally leaving at the connection instead of taking the final flight. It can violate airline conditions, makes checked baggage impractical and can cause the rest of an itinerary to be cancelled. We would not build a trip around it.

Price forecasts can be useful signals, but airfare and hotel prices remain unpredictable. Treat predictions as estimates, not promises. Book when the price, schedule and cancellation terms work for your trip.

Use apps to organize information, not to replace official immigration sources. Entry requirements can depend on nationality, residency, passport type, trip purpose and even transit airports.

Google Maps can provide offline driving navigation for downloaded areas, and Google Translate supports downloaded language packs. Outdoor apps such as AllTrails and Gaia GPS can offer offline maps depending on the plan. Save boarding passes, hotel addresses and reservation numbers before leaving Wi-Fi.

AI can build a useful first draft, but it should not be allowed to quietly invent your holiday. Use it to organize ideas, compare possible routes and identify questions. Then verify flights, hotels, opening hours, ticket rules, visa requirements and safety information through official or first-party sources.

An eSIM can be useful when your regular carrier charges high roaming fees. Compare the local network, data allowance, hotspot rules and validity period before purchasing.

Google Maps and Google Translate support downloaded offline content. Some hiking apps provide offline maps through paid plans. Download everything before leaving reliable Wi-Fi.

The best travel app is not the one with the longest feature list. It is the one that solves a problem at the moment you need it. For us, Google Maps, Google Translate, WhatsApp, an airline app and an itinerary organizer cover the basics. From there, we add an eSIM, flight tracker, budgeting app or hiking map depending on the trip.

Download your essentials before leaving home, test that you can sign in and save critical information offline. Then put the phone away once in a while. The app is supposed to help you experience the trip—not become the trip.

#5510. Emirates Will Give You One Mile For Every Minute You Spend In Dubai

Emirates Skywards has just launched a creative promotion. I love when loyalty programs think outside the box, and this is the perfect example of that. Now, whether people actually want to visit Dubai right now is a different question…

Thanks to a limited time promotion, Emirates is offering Skywards members one mile for every minute they spend in Dubai:

While Emirates is an independently profitable airline, there’s no denying that a major incentive for the carrier is also to build up the economy of Dubai, and that largely comes in the form of encouraging people to visit. Keep in mind that the carrier also has the “My Emirates Pass” concept, where your Emirates boarding pass can unlock savings at retailers throughout the UAE.

Of course let me caution that I’m just sharing the promo here, and not making a call as to whether anyone should visit the UAE. As a reminder, the US Department of State just issued a Level 3 travel advisory for the UAE, encouraging people to reconsider travel there.

There’s one major catch with this offer (well, aside from general willingness of people to travel to the UAE). Earning one Skywards mile per minute is quite a generous mileage earning rate. That’s 1,440 miles per day, 10,080 miles per week, or 43,200 miles per month.

So that brings us to the catch — each Skywards member is capped at earning 7,200 bonus miles with this promotion. You’d earn that after spending exactly five days in Dubai. So this promotion creates a nice incentive to plan a stopover there, though a long visit won’t earn you much incrementally.

Emirates Skywards has launched a pretty creative promotion for visiting Dubai. You can earn one bonus Skywards mile for every minute you spend in Dubai. The major catch is that you have to stay for at least 24 hours, and you’re capped at earning 7,200 bonus miles, which you’d reach after five days.

Also, the timing of this offer comes just shortly after the US has issued an elevated travel warning for the UAE, so everyone can make of this what they’d like. I’m certainly not going to the UAE anytime soon!

What do you make of Emirates’ “mile-a-minute” promotion?

#5509. The United States Just Got a New National Park For the First Time in Six Years

The US national park list is growing for the first time in nearly six years. Chiricahua National Monument, located in southeastern Arizona, was officially designated as the 64th national park in the US on October 20, 2026 following President Trump's signature on the Chiricahua National Park Act.

The new park lies just over an hour from the Arizona–New Mexico border and is nicknamed the “Wonderland of Rocks” for its towering rock spires and unusual formations, the result of a volcanic eruption about 27 million years ago.

Chiricahua was originally established as a national monument in 1924, and today boasts more than 17 miles of hiking trails for exploring on foot, as well as the Bonita Canyon Scenic Drive, a picturesque road that winds through rock formations.

For outdoors lovers, the designation is big news. The last US landmark to receive a new national park designation was West Virginia’s New River Gorge National Park & Preserve in 2020, home to one of the oldest rivers in the world and the deepest and longest river gorge in the Appalachians.

Chiricahua joins three existing national parks in Arizona: The Grand Canyon National Park in the north, Petrified Forest National Park in the northeast, and Saguaro National Park just under two hours west of Chiricahua near Tucson. If you're planning a trip to the newest US national park (or perhaps all four Arizona parks), here's what to know.

Chiricahua National Park is famous for its hoodoos and balancing rocks.

Chiricahua National Park is incredibly biodiverse thanks to its unique location in the Chiricahua Mountains between the Sonoran and Chihuahuan deserts, an environment known as a “sky island.”

The park spans roughly 12,000 acres of grasslands, desert scrub, riparian, and pine woodlands, which means there is a huge variety of wildlife and habitat types to see and explore. Aside from the almost 200 species of birds, there are also over 70 species of mammals, 46 species of reptiles, and 8 species of amphibians. Keep an eye out for everything from banded rock rattlesnakes to cactus wrens, whitetail deer, and white-nosed coatis.

While immersing themselves in nature, visitors can also experience the areas's rich cultural history; the park is the ancestral home of the Chiricahua Apache and has ties to prehistoric Indigenous communities, Buffalo Soldiers, ranchers, and the Civilian Conservation Corps.

Visitors can experience the best of the park's offerings by taking the scenic drive to Massai Point, Echo Canyon, and Sugarloaf; hiking the 17 miles of wilderness trails through the rocky landscape; exploring the Faraway Ranch historic district, home to Swedish immigrants in the late 1800s; or stargazing in the area (did we mention it's an international dark sky park too?).

Because Chiricahua was already protected as a national monument, not much will change on the ground besides the new name (and updated signage). Guests can still camp in the park overnight, enjoy scenic drives through the volcanic landscape, go on hikes, and spot plenty of wildlife. For now, parking and the entrance fee to Chiricahua remain free, as they did before the new designation.

The largest impact will come from the increased attention that national park status will bring to the area. Based on a report done in 2018 by independent nonprofit research group Headwaters Economics, in reference to White Sands National Park's change in designation from a national monument in 2019, national parks often see higher visitation rates than national monuments, resulting in increased spending per visitor, and more overnight stays. That means visitors can anticipate higher tourism rates in and surrounding Chiricahua National Park in the near future—which could also lead to more crowds, traffic, and increased development in nearby towns.

Like many national parks, Chiricahua is remote, and doesn't have an overwhelming amount of accommodation options nearby. The nearest city to Chiricahua National Park is Willcox, Arizona, which is roughly 30–45 minutes away by car. Tucson (where guests from farther afoot will likely fly into) is about two hours away. There are a few smaller cities and towns—like Bisbee, Douglas, Portal, and Bowie—scattered within 100 miles of the park, but expect the drive from your accommodations to the park to be on the longer side. For those who want to sleep under the stars, you can camp directly in the park at the Bonita Canyon Campground.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

#5507. Scientists can now 'watermark' AI-generated proteins to deter bioweapon creation. But will it really work?

A new technology encodes an invisible mark into the structure of AI-generated proteins, but experts said the system's guardrails can still be bypassed.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With artificial intelligence (AI), scientists have a powerful new tool for creating never-before-seen proteins, which can be used to make either groundbreaking new medicines or devastating bioweapons.

To help guard against nefarious uses of these proteins, scientists have recently figured out a way to "watermark" them without compromising their function. A team from Google DeepMind developed this technique to better track the provenance of AI-designed proteins.

"This has the potential to reduce biosecurity threats, such as unauthorized toxin creation, and may help with database curation, preventing artificial designs from cluttering the standard databases," said Roy Sleator, a biology professor at Munster Technological University in Ireland, who was not involved in the research.

"In practice, it could help trace a design's origin, flag engineered proteins for extra biosecurity checks, and reduce misleading claims about how a protein was made," Sleator told Live Science in an email.

However, in its current form, the same AI technologies that add watermarks can also strip them away, other experts warned.

The unveiling of the AI program AlphaFold in 2020 was a milestone in protein engineering. The tool, which won Demis Hassabis, John Jumper and David Baker the Nobel Prize in Chemistry in 2024, can reveal the 3D structure of a protein from the sequence of its building blocks, called amino acids. Proteins fold like origami, adopting 3D shapes that dictate their function.

However, AlphaFold's technology also opened the door to generating brand-new proteins that could be used to create biothreats, including viral proteins that can evade our immune systems and existing vaccines.

"Anything that can be used for good can be used for bad," said George Rose, professor emeritus of biophysics at Johns Hopkins University.

Current screening tools identify novel dangerous or restricted molecules by seeing how similar their amino acid sequences are to those of recognized threats. But the problem is that AI could come up with proteins that don't look like anything we've ever seen. That's what the new watermarking technique aims to solve.

The DeepMind team has framed the watermarking effort as a "family" of related techniques they call SynthIDBio. The first member of this family is SynthIDBio-sequence, which encodes watermarks into the amino acid sequences of proteins created by a program called ProteinMPNN.

The 3D shape of a protein is largely determined by its amino acid sequence, or the order of its building blocks. In cells, these blocks are assembled by ribosomes (pictured).

When users tell ProteinMPNN the desired 3D shape for their protein, it chooses amino acid options that ultimately fold into that shape. SynthIDBio-sequence subtly influences how the program chooses each amino acid according to a secret watermarking "key." Once the sequence is generated, anyone who knows the hidden code can assess how likely the sequence is to have been created under the key's bias. In their recent study, the DeepMind team showed that proteins built with this watermark could still bind to their targets as well as non-watermarked proteins.

A sister technique, called SynthIDBio-structure, instead embeds a watermark directly into the protein's structure. It does this by altering the function of AlphaFold 3, DeepMind's flagship protein prediction algorithm.

When AlphaFold predicts a structure, SynthIDBio-structure gives it a side mission: The AI must make minuscule tweaks to how the atoms in the protein are arranged. These tweaks can be picked up by a detector later, and they're small enough that they don't look like obvious distortion.

In a test, these two systems created hidden watermarks that could be detected with incredible accuracy, flagging more than 99% of watermarked proteins and correctly ignoring 99.9% of non-watermarked proteins.

Live Science spoke to several experts who agreed that SynthIDBio was an impressive technical feat. However, they said it wasn't ready for widespread implementation yet.

The two techniques are "effective but brittle," Bruce Wittmann, a principal applied scientist at Microsoft, told Live Science in an email. That's because, as the DeepMind team also notes in their paper, the watermarks can be easily removed.

I imagine it would be very difficult to ensure the regulations were being followed.

"For sequences, watermarks can be removed by 'resequencing' — effectively, altering the protein amino acid sequence such that the watermark is no longer detectable," Wittmann said. "For structures, watermarks can be removed by adding noise to or otherwise modifying the spatial coordinates of the structure." Screening tools wouldn't be able to detect if a watermark had been removed from a protein.

Experts are also skeptical that watermarking would provide a long-term solution for biosecurity.

An indelible watermark could identify who originally made a nefarious protein, or identify when a user was accessing a protein generation software without permission. This might deter some criminals, but terrorists looking to create a novel protein for a biological weapon would be less likely to care about being identified after a successful attack, said Max Brackmann, a chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear arms expert at the Spiez Laboratory in Switzerland.

Additionally, the vast majority of software for protein prediction and generation is open-source, meaning much of the code is freely available to use and modify. That makes it far harder to bake watermarking into these programs. Wittmann warned that the field would have to shift toward closed-source models before watermarks could become an industry standard.

He added that watermarking could act as one layer of a biosecurity defense shield, rather than offering standalone protection. Top-down regulation would be challenging to enforce. "I imagine it would be very difficult to ensure the regulations were being followed," he said.

Rose was even blunter about regulating the use of generative proteins.

"I'm convinced it won't work," he said. "Once there's a sweet technology that does something, government regulation to keep it under wraps has never been really successful."

Stutz, D., Cowen-Rivers, A.I., Ortiz-Jimenez, G., et al. (2026) Function-preserving watermarking of AI-generated proteins. Nature. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-026-10965-y

Help us improve Live Science Pro: We're always trying to make our content better. Leave us feedback about Pro here.

RJ Mackenzie is an award-nominated science and health journalist. He has degrees in neuroscience from the University of Edinburgh and the University of Cambridge. He became a writer after deciding that the best way of contributing to science would be from behind a keyboard rather than a lab bench. He has reported on everything from brain-interface technology to shape-shifting materials science, and from the rise of predatory conferencing to the importance of newborn-screening programs. He is a former staff writer of Technology Networks.

#5505. The Volvo EX60 delivered more driving range in the real world, covering over 350 miles

The new Volvo EX60 has an EPA-estimated range of 312 miles, but in real-world conditions, it drove over 350 miles without stopping.

Volvo calls the EX60 a “game changer” as its fastest-charging, longest-range electric vehicle to date.

In July, Volvo announced the 2027 EX60 P10 AWD received an official EPA certification with an estimated driving range of up to 330 miles, beating its own estimate of 322 miles.

When fitted with the 22″ tires, the EX60 P10 AWD Ultra boasts an EPA-estimated range of 312 miles on a single charge.

During Edmunds recent EV Range Test, the AWD electric SUV proved it can drive further than advertised.

The EX60 P10 AWD (with 22″ tires) covered 352 miles on a single charge during the test, beating its EPA-estimated 312 miles by 13%.

Volvo’s electric SUV also outperformed many luxury competitors that underwent the same test, including the Cadillac Lyriq AWD (319 miles) and Mercedes-Benz EQS+ 320 SUV (349 miles). However, it wasn’t enough to beat Mercedes’ new GLC 400 EV, which managed 392 miles.

It was also more efficient than expected, using just 28.8 kWh per 100 miles, beating its EPA-estimated 30 kWh per 100 miles.

The Edmunds EV Range Test is designed to offer a more accurate estimate of a vehicle’s range during real-world, everyday driving conditions. It consists of 60% city and 40% highway driving with an average speed of 40 mph.

The vehicle stays within 5 mph of the posted speed limit, and the climate control is set on auto at 72 degrees.

Measuring 189.1″ long, 81.4″ wide, and 64.4″ high, with a 116.9″ wheelbase, the EX60 is about the size of a Tesla Model Y.

Edmund’s range test is just not great IMO.Where someone really cares about range is long distance travel, so highway. Thus anything less then 65mph is just not what matters. A few YouTubers do it right, where they do 70-75 and they throw in a charge. Can you tell me if I drive for example Boston to NY which is about 220 -230 miles leave with 100% arrive with minimum 10-20% highway at 70mph. This is where range really matters.

The base 2027 Volvo EX60 P6 starts at $58,400. The twin-motor EX60 P10 Plus Ultra starts at $60,750.

Based on Volvo’s SPA3 platform with 800V fast charging, the P10 AWD version can add up to 165 miles in 10 minutes. The P6 can add up to 155 miles in 16 minutes.

#5504. Why This Company is One of the Best for Finding Work

Most travel is extractive and passive. You show up somewhere, take photos of the same landmarks everyone else takes photos of, eat at a restaurant the guidebook recommended, and hit up some bars. You take some tours to learn about the place and, if you’re lucky, get to meet some locals on your trip. You don’t really go deep.

Even long-term travelers who talk about how they “aren’t tourists” do the same – just at a slower pace and while trying to spend less money.

And there’s nothing wrong with that. That’s just the nature of travel. While we all imagine dropping into a place, befriending locals, and having this crazy time like we’re in some movies. But that doesn’t happen. Because we’re just passing through and, for the most part, locals don’t want to become friends with tourists. In fact, most locals don’t interact with tourists. (I mean it does happen but it’s not the norm.)

If you really want a deep travel experience, you need to stay. If you want to understand a place, you need to stay. The most transformative travel experiences I’ve ever had involved contributing something and staying somewhere long enough to build real relationships. It was being embedded in a place rather than just passing through.

I think that more travelers should give back when they travel. In the old days, it was hard to find out what organizations and opportunities were legit, vetted, and actually made the lives of locals better.

Now, there’s a ton of companies that can help you not only find meaningful volunteer jobs when you travel but also paid jobs when you travel and one of the best ones is Global Work & Travel. Use code NOMADICMATT to unlock a discount on your next trip.

Global Work & Travel is the world’s largest gap year company and has been placing people on working holidays, volunteer programs, teaching positions, internships, and more for nearly 2 decades. They’ve helped over 116,000 people and they cover destinations across, the UK and Europe, the Americas, Asia, Africa, and the Pacific.

Even for experienced travelers, moving abroad can be difficult. Finding employment as a traveler, securing permanent accommodation or getting stuck in shared rooms for months on end, transport costs in a new city, interview after interview competing against locals… This is just the beginning of what long term travelers need to consider.

Fortunately, companies like Global Work & Travel provide the scaffolding or safety net that makes working abroad possible for people who don’t want to figure everything out themselves. They handle job matching, pre-departure support, visa guidance, placement, and ongoing assistance through their gWorld portal, a personal trip management app that keeps everything organized in one place. For first-timers especially, having that support structure can be the difference between actually going and endlessly deliberating. The programs they offer span a wide range:

For the past decade, “digital nomad” has been the aspirational version of long term travel. Work remotely, earn in a strong currency, live somewhere cheaper, repeat.

But that model only really works for a small subset of people. You need a remote job, a certain income level, and often a level of career stability that most younger travelers simply don’t have yet.

Instead of bringing a job with you, you get one when you arrive. You integrate into the local economy instead of floating above it. You meet people through work, not just coworking spaces or short-term hostels. And you build a life somewhere, not just a temporary routine.

In a lot of ways, working holiday makers are having a more grounded and accessible version of what people think digital nomad life is. You’re not just passing through, you’re actually living there. You have coworkers, a schedule, a reason to stay longer than a week, and a chance to build real lifetime connections.

It’s also far more realistic for most people. You don’t need a remote business or years of experience. You just need the willingness to go and the right setup to make it happen.

That’s why I think we’re seeing a shift. Less “how do I work remotely?” and more “how do I actually live and work abroad for a year?”

And that’s exactly where structured programs like Global Work & Travel come in. They remove the biggest barriers, finding a job, navigating visas, getting set up, so people can actually make that shift from idea to reality.

For a lot of travelers, this isn’t just an alternative to digital nomad life. It’s a more sustainable, accessible, and realistic way to actually live and work abroad.

Volunteering abroad has a complicated reputation, and it’s worth being honest about that. There are lots of bad volunteer programs out there in the world. Programs that don’t really give back and are designed to make travelers “feel” like they did something but really are just profit driven campaigns. Global Work & Travel’s vets programs in the areas such as wildlife conservation, community development, education, and healthcare in the locations and countries it operates so you know the program you sign up for is legit.

The case for ethical, well-structured volunteering is genuinely strong. When you work alongside local Zanzibar communities on problems they’ve defined as priorities, or with wildlife and conservation in South Africa or Elephant rehabilitation in Thailand — something shifts in how you understand the world. Not only are you helping to give back to a place but you also understand the true needs of a the people and ecosystems living there, but also helping overcome the struggles they go through. It opens your mind and shifts your perspective.

Their Global Animal Welfare Fund also channels resources directly into conservation projects at their partner sites, so the impact extends beyond individual placements.

Working holidays let you truly experience a country instead of just visiting it. You immerse yourself in the culture by living like a local every day. You meet more people both locals and fellow travellers while building real friendships. Best of all, you get paid to travel, so you can stay much longer instead of rushing through in a few weeks. This lets you experience local life deeply, discover hidden spots, and understand how people really live in different places. You also build valuable skills, boost your resume with international experience, and make long-term travel more affordable since your income covers the costs. Plus, you gain a real cultural understanding that no short holiday can match. All that while stil getting paid? Not bad right?!

Working holidays turn travel into a true lifestyle, not just another 2 week vacation.

Global Work & Travel are the world leaders in long term travel and offer a ton of options for travelers looking to volunteer, work, or spend extended time in a destination. Some things I like about them.

Do I need prior experience to work abroad with Global Work & Travel? For most trips, generally nothing more than basic work experience is required. Teaching programs include TEFL training, and volunteering can be started from no experience.

Travel has always been best when it’s been more than tourism. The moments that stay with you — that you actually tell people about five years later — are never the ones where you queued to see a famous landmark. They’re the ones where you connect with people. They are the moments where you did something.

#5503. Buy World Of Hyatt Points For 20% Off (2.08 Cents Each): Worth It?

World of Hyatt is offering a promotion on purchased points. With this, members can purchase points with up to a 20% discount, which is an opportunity to acquire them for 2.08 cents each. World of Hyatt is a useful points currency, and there’s definitely value to be had here for some, especially if you don’t have access to World of Hyatt points via transferable points currencies.

However, let me of course remind everyone that Hyatt recently introduced more dynamic award pricing, and in many cases it’s really bad for redemption values. Still, there are definitely ways to come out ahead while taking advantage of this offer, though everyone should crunch the numbers for themselves.

Link: Buy World of Hyatt points with this promotion for 20% off

Through 11:59PM ET on Monday, December 21, 2026, World of Hyatt is offering a 20% discount on purchased points. You can unlock this offer as long as you purchase at least 3,000 points in one transaction. If you purchase 1,000 or 2,000 points, you don’t receive any bonus.

Under normal circumstances, World of Hyatt charges 2.6 cents per purchased World of Hyatt point, inclusive of all taxes and fees. If you maxed out the promotion, you could purchase 110,000 World of Hyatt points for $2,288, which is a rate of 2.08 cents per World of Hyatt point.

Ordinarily, when Hyatt has a promotion on purchased points, the program offers either a 20-30% bonus or a 20-30% discount. So this is not at the top end of that range, though some bonus is better than no bonus!

World of Hyatt ordinarily lets members buy up to 55,000 points per account per calendar year, before any bonuses, in increments of 1,000 points. However, during this promotion, that cap has been doubled, to 110,000 points. Note that in order to purchase points, you must have been enrolled in World of Hyatt for at least 60 days.

While World of Hyatt limits how many points you can buy per year, the program also lets you transfer points to other members for free online, with the ability to transfer up to 525,000 points per year. So that could be a way to circumvent this limit while trying to buy points.

When World of Hyatt does offer a promotion on purchased points, it’s most common to see either a 20% bonus or a 20% discount. Personally I’d buy points based on when you’re ready to redeem them, rather than the exact cost. After all, the cost to buy points with a 20% bonus vs. 20% discount is fairly similar.

Below is a chart with some of the recent promotions we saw from the program, before this one.

World of Hyatt points purchases are processed by points.com, meaning they don’t count as a hotel purchase for the purposes of credit card spending. That means if you pay with a Hyatt credit card, you wouldn’t earn any bonus rewards, so it’s not treated as if it’s Hyatt spending.

I’d recommend using a card with which you’re trying to reach a minimum spending requirement, or a credit card that maximizes your return on everyday spending. When in doubt, review which credit cards are best for buying points and miles.

While I wouldn’t buy World of Hyatt points without a specific use in mind, there’s tons of value to be had if buying points strategically.

I’m a huge fan of the World of Hyatt program, thanks to the excellent hotel brands Hyatt has, and the general value of World of Hyatt Globalist status, including the ability to use suite upgrade awards when redeeming points.

I’ve had so many amazing award stays with Hyatt over the years, and you could instantly buy points and redeem them at a property and save over 50% in the process. I’ll get into that in a bit more detail below.

Nowadays World of Hyatt has five tiers of award pricing, spread across eight categories of hotels. Below you can find the redemption chart, with standard room pricing per night.

There are truly endless amazing redemptions, so let me just give a couple of examples of how this promotion could pay off, on both ends of the spectrum.

For example, take the Category 1 Hyatt Place Los Cabos. With the lowest award pricing, it costs 3,000 Hyatt points per night. At 2.08 cents per point, you could buy those points for $62.40.

Meanwhile the cash rate for that same night would be $146.60 inclusive of taxes and fees, so that’s a massive discount right there.

For a luxury example, take the Category 8 Park Hyatt Tokyo. With the medium tier award pricing, it costs 45,000 Hyatt points per night. At 2.08 cents per point, you could buy those points for $936.

Meanwhile if paying cash for that stay, you’d pay $1,398.50, so that represents a significant savings.

Of course let me emphasize that there are also all kinds of situations where you won’t come out ahead buying points. If the points rate multiplied by the cost of the purchase is less than the cash rate, then there’s no reason to buy points.

World of Hyatt points can expire if your account doesn’t have any activity for 24 consecutive months. However, any points earning or redemption activity would reset the expiration of your points.

Everyone will value points differently. Based on my latest miles & points valuations, I consider World of Hyatt points to be worth ~1.4 cents each, and I tend to value points pretty conservatively. So the purchase price is a bit higher than what I value the points, though like I said, there are ways to get much more value.

World of Hyatt doesn’t have blackout dates on award nights, and all standard rooms are supposed to be available for points redemptions at the published costs.

There are lots of circumstances under which it could make sense to buy World of Hyatt points. However, if you want to acquire World of Hyatt points at a lower out of pocket cost, there are some other options involving credit cards.

In addition to being able to earn World of Hyatt points with Hyatt’s co-branded credit cards, you can also transfer points from both Bilt Rewards and Chase Ultimate Rewards:

Then you can also earn points with Hyatt’s co-branded credit cards, which include the World of Hyatt Business Card (learn more) and World of Hyatt Credit Card (learn more).

Through December 21, 2026, World of Hyatt is offering a 20% discount on the purchase of points, which is an opportunity to acquire points for 2.08 cents each. I know many people find it worthwhile to buy World of Hyatt points, especially those outside the United States, who don’t have access to lucrative credit cards.

So while I absolutely wouldn’t proactively pick up points at this cost, there could still potentially be value. As always, you’ll want to crunch the numbers for yourself, based on the potential stays you’d make.

#5502. Myths of Battlefield Triumph Are Destroying the U.S. Military

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Myths of Battlefield Triumph Are Destroying the U.S. Military

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

In launching his war on Iran, U.S. President Donald Trump promised quick and decisive results. More than six months later, despite thousands of airstrikes, thousands of dead Iranians, and the destruction of Tehran’s air force and navy, Iran is unbowed. Its new leaders are more radical and uncompromising than those killed by the United States and Israel at the start of the war. By disrupting traffic through the Strait of Hormuz, Iran holds the global economy hostage as the supply of oil, gas, and refined fuels out of the Gulf remains intermittent. For the Trump administration and its dwindling core of supporters, the hunt is on for excuses.

There is a default narrative for Washington’s military failures—one that is more than likely to be picked up by Trump and only compound his failure. It is the narrative of triumph forsaken: The United States can still defeat Iran if only it has the will and endurance. It just has to endure more pain and launch more missiles. Retired U.S. Army Gen. Jack Keane picked up on this supposed solution: “We really do have the capability to end this militarily if we desire to.”

In launching his war on Iran, U.S. President Donald Trump promised quick and decisive results. More than six months later, despite thousands of airstrikes, thousands of dead Iranians, and the destruction of Tehran’s air force and navy, Iran is unbowed. Its new leaders are more radical and uncompromising than those killed by the United States and Israel at the start of the war. By disrupting traffic through the Strait of Hormuz, Iran holds the global economy hostage as the supply of oil, gas, and refined fuels out of the Gulf remains intermittent. For the Trump administration and its dwindling core of supporters, the hunt is on for excuses.

There is a default narrative for Washington’s military failures—one that is more than likely to be picked up by Trump and only compound his failure. It is the narrative of triumph forsaken: The United States can still defeat Iran if only it has the will and endurance. It just has to endure more pain and launch more missiles. Retired U.S. Army Gen. Jack Keane picked up on this supposed solution: “We really do have the capability to end this militarily if we desire to.”

Triumph forsaken is an old trope in U.S. military history. One prominent revisionist account of the Vietnam War bears that very title. This belief that any defeat is merely a lost victory springs from a fixation on material conditions and numerical calculations of comparative military strength—the bean counting school of military analysis. Since World War II more than 80 years ago, the United States has never fought a foe with peer or near-peer military or economic strength. In many cases, such as Afghanistan, the disparity in power between the U.S. military and its adversary was so large as to be absurd. Yet despite this massive imbalance, Washington has clearly and repeatedly lost wars. Repeating the failures of U.S. policymakers and military planners in Vietnam, the architects of the war in Afghanistan elided the political obstacles to victory to make the case that a flawed strategy could work with just more time and willpower.

While the United States’ wealth, technology, and power projection capabilities remain preeminent for now, armed conflict is never decided by a tally of military assets. In case after case, Washington’s strength has actually played against it. A belief in military force as the sole ingredient for success has enabled a lazy and destructive foreign policy that ignores the will and motivations of both allies and adversaries. Such thinking also elevates tactics above strategy, prioritizing quick battlefield victories over attrition, exhaustion, and will. As a result, the ostensibly weaker side—despite being decidedly outmatched by the U.S. military—has often won.

A key component of this latest triumph of hope over experience—the belief that somehow Trump’s Iran war would be different—was a sanguinary spin on triumph forsaken: the idea that the U.S. military failed to take the proverbial gloves off in Iraq and Afghanistan in order to prosecute those wars with sufficient ruthlessness. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth has been a relentless purveyor of this myth. In his 2024 book The War on Warriors, the former Army platoon leader wrote that he told his soldiers in Iraq to ignore the rules of engagement they’d been issued. During Trump’s first term, Hegseth’s major contribution was pushing the president to pardon two high-profile service members found guilty of war crimes and reverse the disciplining of a third.

Hegseth, with the cadences of the TV host that he was, summarized this unrestrained “gloves-off” approach to warfare as “maximum lethality, not tepid legality.” He has backed up this rhetoric with moves designed to make it easier for U.S. troops to skirt rules of engagement and the laws of war—or even ignore them with impunity. One of his first moves was to fire the military lawyers that commanders rely on for interpreting and applying legal restraints on violence, followed by defunding the office in the Pentagon tasked with reducing harm to innocent bystanders in conflict.

Taking the gloves off has done nothing to defeat Iran. Indeed, it has had the opposite effect. The horrific deaths of more than 100 schoolchildren in Minab on the first day of the war hardened Iranian resolve, pushing even some dedicated regime opponents into public nationalism. The rally-around-the-flag effect remains a predictable consequence of military action, often for the attacked more than the attacker. National unity should come as no surprise when the attacker threatens to destroy not just enemy forces, but an entire civilization.

The Wall Street Journal recently interviewed Iranians who had shifted from regime opposition to ardent support. One young entrepreneur was badly beaten during 2022 protests and tear gassed by security forces in January. Now, after his uncle’s house in Tehran was destroyed and he feared his girlfriend was killed, he has joined the military to defend Iran against what he sees as a civilizational threat.

As Iranian resolve has grown, Trump has continued his pattern of declaring victory while simultaneously threatening to escalate the conflict, most recently with the threat of a massive bombing campaign after the midterm elections.

Despite the clear risks that such an escalation would entail for the region, Trump’s threats come with no clear logic beyond an unfaltering belief that more violence will magically lead to triumph. Others have embraced this faulty but familiar thinking. U.S. Rep. Rich McCormick, for example, said, “We should’ve taken the gloves off right from the beginning and hammered them. You have to make it absolutely miserable on their populace.” This is the logical end point of what the counterterrorism analyst Jonathan Schroden has termed “the asylum of lethality.”

Regardless of the ultimate outcome of this war with Iran, Hegseth is taking steps to ensure that no meaningful lessons will be learned by future U.S. generals. Last month, he signed a one-page memo ending tenure for civilian faculty at U.S. military academies. The academies train lieutenants and ensigns, setting the tone for the careers of many of the nation’s future senior military leaders. Destroying their academic excellence and faculty independence in the name of “the warfighting mission” prioritizes training over education and will erode the ability of cadets to learn strategic lessons during their formation as officers. The abolition of academic independence will also make it easier for Hegseth and his successors to instill their views on the military in future generations.

For those at the pinnacle of their careers, Hegseth has imposed a new hurdle: a Joint Warfighter Evaluation that will screen all future generals and admirals for “their ability to fight, adapt, and win.” What this will amount to is a series of written tests and war games focused on tactical decision-making and lethality.

As Trump’s failed Iran war shows, tactical competence is far down the list of what needs fixing in the U.S. military. This has been true for a long time. As told by U.S. Army Col. Harry Summers, one of the U.S. officials involved in negotiations to end the Vietnam War in Hanoi, he insisted to one of his North Vietnamese counterparts, “You know, you never defeated us on the battlefield.” His former foe replied: “That may be so, but it is also irrelevant.”

The exchange as told by Summers may be apocryphal, but it remained fundamentally true in the two wars Hegseth fought. In Afghanistan, the United States and its coalition partners won nearly every tactical engagement. Western troops killed Taliban fighters at a ratio of more than 20 to 1. But less than 20 years after invading, the United States withdrew in defeat. The U.S. military fell short in Afghanistan, but a lack of lethality and battlefield competence was not the reason it lost.

With a series of costly military defeats since the 1991 Gulf War, the United States has come to embrace a perverse and unsustainable orientation to the rest of the world: extreme belligerence paired with strategic fecklessness. As the Canadian writer Stephen Marche acidly noted, “[T]he U.S. military invents whole new genres of defeat.”

Military defeat usually leads to major policy reforms, but the United States has bucked that trend. The country’s wealth, geographic security, and solipsism have prevented meaningful foreign-policy reflection and reform for the past 30 years. But as failures mount and U.S. power ebbs, the need for course correction has become urgent.

Even as it stares at another defeat in the Middle East, this U.S. military and its civilian leadership appear intent on learning nothing. At an August ceremony commemorating the fifth anniversary of the Afghanistan withdrawal, Hegseth presented three former Marines with upgraded awards for valor. “You are the vanguard of a lethal, proud, and undefeated force,” he told them. Such delusions will do far more damage to American national security than any Iranian missile.

Gil Barndollar a senior research fellow at the Catholic University of America’s Center for the Study of Statesmanship and a non-resident fellow at Defense Priorities.

Jason Dempsey is an adjunct senior fellow in the National Security Human Capital Program at the Center for a New American Security, the author of Our Army: Soldiers, Politics and American Civil-Military Relations, a former special assistant to the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and a former U.S. Army officer with service in Afghanistan and Iraq.

#5501. 13 Dreamy Airbnbs in the Middle of Nowhere

There’s a reason so many people talk about leaving Wi-Fi, cell service, and city lights behind for a remote Airbnb that lets them connect with nature. For some, it’s their greatest nightmare. For others, the height of luxury. The middle of nowhere might be divisive in its allure, but there’s no denying what it can offer a certain type of traveler. Perhaps you want to connect with the great outdoors, or look deeper within yourself; maybe your motivation is as simple as getting some distraction-free reading done. Whatever the case, a remote Airbnb promises peace, quiet, and unparalleled access to nature—whether that looks like the craggy cliffs of an Icelandic fjord, or the lush Green Mountains of Maine. The properties on this list, which span from a vegetable farm in North Carolina to the Andean peaks of Patagonia, will offer you an experience unlike any other.

In the case of these excellent spots, “remote” doesn’t always have to mean inconveniently out of the way; some of these rentals, like a cottage in the Faroe Islands or a cabin in Tennessee, are within 20 minutes of an airport or major city, offering the best of both worlds. Below are 13 remote Airbnbs for when you want to disconnect from the world. Read on for something for every level of adventure, whether you dream of experiencing the stillness of the high desert in California or hanging out at the top of a mountain in Peru.

We've vetted these listings based on Superhost and Guest Favorite status, ratings, amenities, location, previous guest reviews, and decor.

Number of guests: 4Bed and bath: 2 bedrooms, 2 bathsWhy we love it: Hot tub, wood-burning fireplace, vinyl collection

It’s tough to say whether this White Mountain hideaway looks better in the summer when it’s sprinkled with wildflowers, or in the winter when it’s blanketed in snow. Facing westward, the alpine home captures golden afternoon light and mountain sunsets from every room. The living area is a midcentury-modern dream, with its record player and expansive vinyl collection, while the outdoor patio is ideal for stargazing at night, thanks to a catamaran net that allows you to lie flat while staring up at the sky.

Location: Norway, MaineThings to do nearby: Norway Brewing Company, Pennesseewassee Lake, Roberts Farm Preserve Trails

Number of guests: 6Bed & bath: 2 beds, 2 bathsWhy we love it: Infrared sauna, firepit, access to red rock hiking

Set at the base of a private sandstone peak, this luxury container home is pure Utah magic. Crafted from two high cube shipping containers, it features modern finishes, oversized tubs, smart beds, and an expansive 1,000-square-foot deck made for stargazing and sunrise coffee. With a cedar sauna and cozy indoor fireplace, it's as perfect for winter cocooning as it is for summer hikes. It’s a prime base for national park adventures (it’s close to Capitol Reef National Park and within driving distance of Bryce Canyon National Park) and intimate group getaways.

Location: Teasdale, UtahThings to do nearby: Bluebell Knoll, Larb Hollow Overlook, Capitol Reef National Park

Number of guests: 4Bed & bath: 2 bedrooms, 1 bathWhy we love it: EV charger, private patio, long-term stays allowed

If reconnecting with nature is a top priority, this two-bedroom, one-bathroom cabin fits the bill. With its floor-to-ceiling windows and 180-degree vistas, you'll never want to leave. Set on 42 acres, yet just half an hour from Nashville International Airport, the views are of trees, trees, and more trees. Take in the scenery while preparing a meal in the state-of-the-art kitchen (peep those copper pots and pans) or while birdwatching on the terrace. Cozy touches like a wood-burning stove and cushy leather furniture encourage travelers to silence their phones and disappear into a good book.

Location: Goodlettsville, TennesseeThings to do nearby: Cedar Hill Park, Union Hill Trading Post, Nashville

Number of guests: 2Bed & bath: 1 bedroom, 1.5 bathsWhy we love it: Unobstructed Sierra views, deep soaking tub, desert solitude

On the edge of the vast Owens Valley near Death Valley National Park, this poetic desert cabin is framed by Mount Whitney and a sea of grazing cattle. Inside, find unobstructed views from the floor-to-ceiling windows, a wood-burning fireplace, and a warm bedroom with direct access to the outdoor bathtub. You come here not for polished amenities but for the romance of the Wild West—sunrises over red rocks, coyotes in the distance, and the feeling of stepping back in time. It's ideal for writers, dreamers, and anyone chasing the stillness of the high desert.

Location: Lone Pine, CaliforniaThings to do nearby: Mount Whitney, Shark Fin Trailhead, Turtle Creek Trailhead

Number of guests: 3Bed & bath: 1 bedroom, 1 bathWhy we love it: Scandinavian interiors, wood-fired hot tub, cedar barrel sauna

This 200-square-foot tiny home proves small can be spectacular. Tucked on eight quiet acres outside Hillsborough, it features Scandinavian design paired with lovingly handcrafted details. Whitewashed pine, handmade ceramics, and East Fork pottery fill the space; outside, a porch nearly the size of the house leads to a sauna and hot tub surrounded by trees. There’s no TV, but you won’t miss it once the stars come out. This spot is perfect for adult duos looking to slow down and soak up some backcountry bliss.

Location: Efland, North CarolinaThings to do nearby: Kings Highway Park, downtown Hillsborough, Mebane Historical Museum

Number of guests: 2Bed and bath: 1 bedroom, 1 bathWhy we love it: Hot tub, sauna, rain shower

An ideal stop along the Ring Road, this sleek mirrored home beautifully reflects Iceland’s stunning coastline. Inside, enjoy floor-to-ceiling windows, a queen-size bed, and a bathroom with a rain shower. Right outside, guests can soak in a hot tub while taking in the fresh air, or retreat to the separate unit that houses a private glass sauna with views of the fjord (just imagine how magical it’d be to take in the northern lights while breaking a sweat). While you will feel like you’re in the middle of nowhere, the home is only a five-minute drive from the town of Búðardalur, where you can grab a bite to eat.

Location: Búðardalur, IcelandThings to do nearby: Hólar Farm, Eiríksstaðir (Viking Longhouse), Ásgarðsgrund beach

Number of guests: 2Bed and bath: 2 bedrooms, 1 bathWhy we love it: Ocean and mountain views, wildlife sightings, proximity to the airport

Located just a five-minute walk away from the world-renowned Múlafossur Waterfall, this one-bedroom home in the Faroe Islands feels undeniably remote, but is located just a 20-minute drive away from the airport. One of six cottages on the property, you may run into a small community of like-minded travelers as you bask in the dramatic Faroese scenery. You might even spot sheep, birds, and highland cows—and if you’re really lucky, the northern lights. Do note that the house can accommodate two extra guests for an added charge.

Location: Gásadalur, Faroe IslandsThings to do nearby: Drangarnir Sea Arch, Fjallavatn Lake, Fjósið restaurant

Number of guests: 2Bed & bath: 1 bedroom, 1.5 bathsWhy we love it: Private deck, mountain views, fast Wi-Fi

This sleek and cozy “bothy” (Scottish cottage) is a minimalist dream in the Scottish Highlands, with vaulted ceilings, soft Skyeskyns rugs, and uninterrupted views of the Quiraing and Trotternish Ridge. Open-plan and sunlit, it has everything you need to escape: a full kitchen, an en suite bath, and a wooden deck perfect for stargazing (or spotting the northern lights). It’s a peaceful retreat for hikers, writers, or couples who want to immerse themselves in Skye’s cinematic beauty without roughing it. Bonus: you’ll wake to total silence—and maybe a few sheep.

Location: Isle of Skye, ScotlandThings to do nearby: Staffin Beach, Kilt Rock Waterfall, Loch Shianta

Number of guests: 6Bed and bath: 3 bedrooms, 1 bathWhy we love it: River access, bicycles for rent, mountain view

If you’re planning a trip from Tokyo to Kyoto, consider stopping in the countryside to stay at this 100-year-old traditional home once owned by a green tea farmer. Surrounded by lush mountains, bamboo forests, tea fields, and a river, the house is designed to let the outside in, with wide windows and paper sliding doors. To get the most out of this remote retreat, consider booking a bamboo weaving workshop with the host Daisuke (guest reviewers gush that he is the nicest man to ever exist), or rent a few bikes to explore Fujieda.

Location: Fujieda, JapanThings to do nearby: Utoge Waterfall, Kashiba-ya Museum, Mount Bikuishi

Number of guests: 2Bed & bath: 1 bedroom, 1 bathWhy we love it: Hot tub, firepit, free parking on premises

Set in Argentine Patagonia, this whimsical yet serene pod‑like shell (inspired by the Welsh dragon masks created in the region) is one of a kind. It offers sweeping views of the Andean peaks and forests—you may even catch a glimpse of the snow-dusted summits, depending on the season. Inside, the materials and light are simple—thoughtfully composed wood, glass, and metal. Ideal for solo travelers or couples who want to dive into Patagonia’s wild energy: glaciers, trails, cold rivers, and long skies are all part of the package here. Come to unplug, to let nature take center stage, and to sleep in a sculpture.

Location: Trevelín, ArgentinaThings to do nearby: Laguna Brychan, Trevelin lavender farm, Molino Andes museum

Number of guests: 2Bed & bath: 1 bed, shared bathroomTop amenities: Unobstructed views, gourmet meals included, zip line access

Not for the faint of heart, this skylodge offers the chance to sleep in a fully transparent pod suspended 1,200 feet above Peru’s Sacred Valley. You arrive via zip line or a Via Ferrata climb—and the reward is staggering: 360-degree mountain views, an included gourmet dinner and breakfast, and an unmatched adrenaline rush. Best for fit travelers, thrill-seekers, and couples who want a night they’ll never forget (or stop posting about), this is truly a once-in-a-lifetime stay.

Location: Urubamba, PeruThings to do nearby: Flowers Path, Rancho Valle Dorado, Hacienda Puka Punku restaurant

Number of guests: 2Bed and bath: 1 bedroom, 1 bathWhy we love it: Hot tub, patio, mountain view

Situated on a working farm in South Africa’s Overberg region, this off-grid cabin is an unforgettable way to take in the Riviersonderend Mountains. It comes equipped with a king-size bed, a well-stocked kitchen, Wi-Fi, air conditioning—and arguably the best part—an outdoor patio housing a massive wood-fired hot tub. “It felt like my husband and I were the only two people there in the world,” one guest reviewer wrote.

Location: Riviersonderend, South AfricaThings to do nearby: Lord’s Wines, Greyton Nature Reserve, Eseltjiesrus Donkey Sanctuary

Number of guests: 2Bed and bath: 1 bedroom, 1 bathWhy we love it: Outdoor bathtub, covered porch, access to trails

As the host puts it, this New Zealand hut is so wonderfully secluded, “you won’t see a neighbor close by.” Tucked away in the valleys of Mount Somers, the one-bedroom home offers 360-degree views of the surrounding mountains, perhaps best enjoyed from the windows encompassing your bed. You’ll find all the modern comforts too, like built-in USB chargers, heated towel racks, and electric blankets, but perhaps most convenient is the list of nearby hiking and biking trails that guests receive upon booking (there’s a pair of Moustache e-bikes available to use as well).

Location: Mount Somers, New ZealandThings to do nearby: Emerald Pool, Lake Clearwater, Awa Awa Reserve
